Section headers carry "[status: …]"; an appendix row carries it after its budget. Chapter and
appendix headers keep "[PLANNED; …]", which ledgers/check_concepts.py parses for the budgets.

## Design principles
- **Titles are short noun phrases** naming their content: no subtitles, colons, punchlines or epigraphs. The titles below are final.
- **One running example.** Silicon is carried through the whole book. GaAs is the polar, direct-gap
  contrast; Ge the heavier indirect-gap contrast; Al the superconductor; one antiferromagnet (NiO) and
  one Dirac material for the special targets. Parts II–III also cite LiF, sapphire, SiC, diamond, TeO₂, CsI, YIG, MnF₂, Te, α-quartz,
  anthracene, PbS, WSi, NaI, a-SiO₂, ⁴He and Xe where a channel needs them; these are "cited where needed", never running examples.
- **Two axes in Part II.** Chapters follow the solid-state sector (electronic, lattice, mixed,
  spin/magnetic, superconducting, special). Every channel chapter covers one content checklist along the DM
  interaction axis. It is a checklist, not a fixed sequence of subsections: the chapter's narrative decides the order and the headings.
  - (a) the excitation and its response function;
  - (b) scattering;
  - (c) absorption;
  - (d) inelastic and exothermic dark matter;
  - (e) literature formulas recovered as limits, each in a limitbox with its comparison plot (below);
  - (f) how the quantity is computed, woven into the text where the quantity first appears (no dedicated subsection; user 2026-11-01: "\"how to compute it\" doesn't need to be a dedicated subsection per se. we don't want to break the narrative flow too much.");
  - (g) uncertainties and open problems.
  Chapter 9 (the atlas) maps every cell of the excitation × interaction matrix to its section. Chapter 17
  reads the matrix along the other axis.
- **Bibliography per chapter.** Each chapter ends with its own reference list; there is one master .bib (biblatex refsection per chapter).
- **Computational connection** (user, 2026-11-01): "for some quantities such as form factors we definitely want to explain the computational connection in the book (maybe even worth having an appendix on ab initio methods e.g. DFT), just we don't necessarily need to actually do the computational before we write the book". Where a material quantity (form factor, loss function, e–ph vertex, …) is introduced, a short passage in the running text says how it is computed and points to Appendix E for the method; there is no dedicated per-chapter computation subsection, so the narrative is not broken; numbers are shown only where cited or narrative-critical.
- **No exercises; nothing left to the reader** (user, 2026-11-01: "No end of chapter exercises. Nothing is left to reader."). No end-of-chapter exercises, no checkpoints asking the reader to verify a step, no "it is easy to show", "left as an exercise" or "the reader may check". Every step is written out in the text or in Appendix D (long derivations). Worked examples stay; they are demonstrations, not tasks.
- **What the reader brings.** A prerequisite marked "→ READER.md assumed" is quoted in READER.md's own words (the check script
  requires a verbatim match with READER.md's "Assumed" list). Anything else the text relies on is taught: either in an earlier
  section or as a short recap, listed in the section's "Introduces" with "(recap)", at its first use.
- **Forward mentions.** A section may name a concept that a later section defines only in a clause that says so: "(forward: N.M)",
  "precise in N.M" or "treated in Chap. N". Writers keep these markers' meaning: the concept is named, not used.
- **Literature formulas as limits** (redesigned; citations are attribution only, the box must be readable without the source; user 2026-11-01: "more informative way to present a limit is just to show a plot. Writing a chain of citations and procedure doesn't convey useful information"). Each widely used formula appears in a `limitbox` that shows: the literature formula with its source citation in one line; a **comparison plot** of the formula against the book's fuller result as a function of the parameter that controls the approximation, with the region of validity shaded and the leading correction visible where the two separate; and one sentence on what breaks down outside the region. The approximation chain is carried by the surrounding text, not listed in the box; the formal record (source equation, chain, validity, leading correction) goes to the Appendix F table. Where no quantitative comparison can be computed cheaply, the plot shows the formula's parameter dependence with the validity boundary marked. **Plot specification (SPINE-II-REV).** Each boxed row of the Appendix F map states four things: the literature formula in the book's notation; the controlling parameter (the plot's x-axis: the small parameter of the 𝔗 component in which the formula and the fuller result differ); the fuller result it is compared with (an equation or section of the book); and the validity region, shaded where the two agree within 10% unless the row states another tolerance. One panel per formula: a row and its amendment row share a panel, two distinct formulas in one box get two panels. Every plot is listed in its section's figures field ("Limitbox plots") and counted in the Budget. How a box is read is explained once in the front matter, so that Chap. 8 may use boxes before 9.4 builds them.
- **Narrative threads,** tracked in ledgers/THREADS.md:
  - T1 the kinematic edge;
  - T2 screening;
  - T3 sum rules as a consistency backbone;
  - T4 the Born–Oppenheimer hierarchy;
  - T5 the silicon phonon-assisted window;
  - T6 from response to measured signal;
  - T7 theory uncertainty.
- **Concept-order check.** ledgers/check_concepts.py scans every field of every Part I entry (purpose, outcomes, prerequisites,
  introductions, key results, computational connection, boundary, sources, figures and worked examples, threads) for the match terms
  of every CONCEPTS row and reports any use before the defining section, plus the pointer, appendix, READER and size checks. Re-run
  after every merge.

## Production directives (user, 2026-11-01)
- **Final language-only audit (2026-11-03):** after all chapters are ACCEPTED and before the final figure check, run briefs/reviews/language-audit.md (one reviewer and one reviser per Part).
- **Chapter maps (2026-11-03):** chapters with a tower of approximations open with a Fig.-1.8-style map (see STYLE.md "Chapter maps"); retrofit accepted chapters, and include one in every new chapter.
- **Succinct general background.** "we can be succinct on general background like evidence for DM since that's not the main purpose of the book. Safe to assume reader would already have some basic idea to be interested in the dark detection material." General-background material (evidence for dark matter, cosmology, generic model building) is compressed to what motivates sub-GeV dark matter and solid targets; the detection physics carries the length. 1.1 is cut to ≈ 1,500 words (Fig. 1.2 dropped); the budget saved goes to the core chapters. Sections whose content is standard background for the persona are kept short and cite reviews.
- **Batched review.** "The heavy review rounds should be lumped over multiple writing sessions so each review cover more content and reduce the token burn relative to reviewing a lot of small chunks" Review cadence:
  - per section, cheap and automatic: lint (scripts/lint.py) and the build;
  - per batch of a chapter (or two short chapters): one derivation check, one prose review, two mock readers (personas a and b/c alternating), then one revision pass per section, driven by the batch reports;
  - seam reviews are folded into the batch review (the reviewer reads the batch in order);
  - the chapter review is the batch review.
  The pilot section 1.1 is reviewed together with the rest of Chapter 1.
- **Compute policy (user, 2026-11-02).** In-box QE runs only when a figure needs data no open source tabulates, or a claim cannot otherwise be checked; otherwise cite literature values. Heavier work is deferred (state/DEFERRED-COMPUTE.md). See COMMON.md.
- **Final figure check (user, 2026-11-02).** Once, after the whole book is drafted and every chapter gate has passed, before release. This pass is not repeated per chapter. It inspects the rendered PDF of every figure and limit-box plot for:
  - visual overlaps: annotations, labels, legends and curves colliding or clipped;
  - semantic placement: each label, arrow, bracket and marker sits on the curve, region, edge or threshold it names, and colours and line styles match the legend and the caption.
  Each fix goes to the figure script, the PDF is regenerated, and the reviewer re-renders it to confirm.
- **Read-along PDFs (user, 2026-11-01).** At every passed revision gate (a chapter batch revised and accepted) the editor builds the book and saves it as book/releases/book-gate-<chapter>.pdf, committed and sent to the user. Retention (user, 2026-11-02): keep at most the 2 most recent read-along PDFs in book/releases; delete older ones (git rm) when a new one is added.

## Budget
**Length policy (user, 2026-11-01):** "length is flexible  No hard cap on length. but of course fluff can be trimmed". Word budgets are planning guides, not caps: a section is as long as its content needs and no longer. Reviews cut fluff (repetition, restated material, padding) rather than enforce a target; writers never pad to reach a budget.
Totals (recomputed 2026-11-02 by ledgers/check_concepts.py after SPINE-II-REV; see the change log, fourth entry). Sections are sized at
3,000–6,000 words (1.1 is 2,000 by user directive and is the script's one size exemption); chapters have 4–10 sections, except Chap. 22,
which has three indexed sections by editor decision 8 and review item S25 (the script's one chapter exemption).
- Part I, Chaps. 1–7: 41 sections, 190,500 words, plus 7 chapter openings and summaries (6,300): **196,800 words**, planned in detail.
  By chapter: 1: 20,000 · 2: 21,000 · 3: 30,000 · 4: 41,500 · 5: 31,500 · 6: 26,500 · 7: 20,000.
- Chap. 8 (Part I, the framework): 8 sections, 37,500 words, plus opening and summary (900): **38,400 words**.
- Part II, Chaps. 9–17: 63 sections, 284,500 words, plus 9 chapter openings and summaries (8,100): **292,600 words**.
  By chapter: 9: 15,000 · 10: 45,000 · 11: 42,500 · 12: 29,500 · 13: 42,000 · 14: 27,000 · 15: 18,500 · 16: 38,500 · 17: 26,500.
- Part III, Chaps. 18–22: 26 sections, 120,000 words, plus 5 chapter openings and summaries (4,500): **124,500 words**.
  By chapter: 18: 23,500 · 19: 29,000 · 20: 23,000 · 21: 31,500 · 22: 13,000.
- Appendices A–F: A 8,000 · B 4,000 · C 34,300 · D 93,000 · E 85,000 · F 12,000 = 236,300 words.
- Front matter: about 9,500 words (the limitbox and status explanation and the Parts II–III reading paths add about 1,000).
- **Total: about 898,100 words, roughly 1,800–2,000 typeset pages** (was about 891,800 after SPINE-II; the change log, fourth entry,
  gives the reasons). The editor may trim at briefing.
- **Limitbox comparison plots (figure and compute budget, not words):** 99 limitboxes with 106 comparison panels (109 boxed LIMITS rows;
  amendment rows share their base row's panel), each listed under "Limitbox plots" in its section's figures field and specified in the
  Appendix F map (formula, 𝔗, x-axis, fuller result, shaded validity). By chapter (boxes/panels): 10: 15/17 · 11: 20/21 · 12: 9/9 ·
  13: 2/2 · 14: 8/10 · 15: 3/3 · 16: 17/19 · 17: 1/1 · 18: 7/7 · 19: 8/8 · 20: 7/7 · 21: 2/2. Fifteen panels (14 rows) use STYLE's
  fallback (parameter dependence with the validity boundary marked; "fallback" in the F map); the other 91 need the book's fuller result evaluated on the plot's grid,
  which is figure-script work (book/figscripts) from the specification libraries of App. E and the section's own formulas, not new research.

## Front matter
- Preface (written last).
- How to read this book: the dependency chart of Part I (from ledgers/CONCEPTS.md) and the reading paths below, each at section granularity.
  It also explains, once, the two devices the text uses from Chap. 8 on:
  - **How to read a limitbox.** A box shows a literature formula in the book's notation, with its source in one line, and a comparison
    plot. The x-axis is the parameter that controls the approximation. The curves are the formula and the book's fuller result. The shaded
    region is where the two agree within the stated tolerance (10% unless the box says otherwise). The leading correction is marked where
    the curves separate. One sentence under the plot says what breaks down outside the region. A box that has no cheap fuller result says
    so and plots the formula's dependence on its parameter with the validity boundary marked. The derivation is in the running text;
    the formal record of every box is Appendix F.
  - **How the text marks status.** A result stated plainly is established. An estimate carries its factor ("×/÷10"). A result that has
    been claimed but not checked, and an exact remaining gap, appear only in the open-problems passage of their home section, worded as
    open. A result new in this book says "derived here".
- What you need (half a page): READER.md's assumed list, verbatim, followed by the material the book recaps at first use and where:
  Newtonian gravity (1.1); Galilean frames (1.2) and elastic kinematics (1.3); classical field Lagrangians and static propagators
  (App. C.9, used in 1.4); Poisson statistics (1.5); identical particles and Slater determinants (2.3); the interaction picture and the
  Dyson series (2.4); atomic spin–orbit coupling and fine structure (3.3); the variational principle (3.4); classical small
  oscillations (5.2). The first Born approximation is derived in 2.2. Parts II–III add short primers in Appendix C, each pointed to at
  first use: likelihoods, confidence levels and profiling (C.14; from 8.8); the Dirac equation and the Foldy–Wouthuysen reduction (C.15;
  from 10.6); the quantized radiation field (C.16; 12.6); point and space groups (C.17; 13.9, 14.5); continuum elasticity (C.18; 11.1,
  20.3); magnetic neutron scattering (C.19; 14.4); the spin–boson model and the Frenkel–Kontorova chain (C.20; 16.8, 19.6). Shorter
  recaps sit in the section that needs them: nuclear spin content (8.1), the B − L vector (11.6), scalars coupled to the electron mass or
  to α (11.8), solid helium as a quantum crystal (11.5), Landé g_J and the Kittel mode (14.3), Bloch equations (14.6), the dirty limit
  and Anderson's theorem (15.3), the boosted dark-matter flux (10.9), the Luke effect (19.5) and internal radioactive backgrounds (21.7).
- Conventions and units: a two-page summary; the full conventions are in App. A.
- Symbols and acronyms: one list each, in App. B.1 and B.3; the front matter points to them and does not repeat them.
- **Reading paths and skip lists** (one per persona of READER.md, plus two by role):
  - (a) First-year PhD student (the primary reader): Chaps. 1–7 in order. On a first reading the following can be skimmed and
    returned to when a later section points back: 2.1 and 2.5 (mostly undergraduate material; keep their worked examples), 3.5 beyond
    its first two outcomes, 3.6 (until 6.2 needs it), 5.4, 5.7, and the App. D derivations once their results are accepted.
  - (b) Particle physicist who knows quantum field theory: skip 1.4 except Table 1.2, the in-medium mixing caveat and App. A.9; skip
    2.1–2.4 except the normalization comparison in 2.1 and Table 2.1 in 2.3; in 4.7 and 4.8 skip the zero-temperature parts and read the
    imaginary-time formalism, the self-energy with a boson and the screened interaction W^scr. Read Chaps. 3, 5, 6 and 7 in full; then
    Chap. 8.
  - (c) Condensed-matter theorist who knows DFT and many-body theory: read Chap. 1 in full, then 2.2 (form factors), 2.5 (the named
    transition sum), 4.2 (the rate as S(q, ω)), 4.4 (the S–L relation; electron-only versus total-charge probes), 5.6, 6.1 (the two
    Migdal results), 6.5, then Chap. 8. Skip 2.1, 2.3, 2.4, 3.1–3.6 (consult 3.2 for the conventions of ρ_mn and v_mn and Table 3.1),
    4.1, 4.3 (read the normalization conversion line), 4.5–4.9, 5.1–5.5, 5.7, 6.2–6.4 and Chap. 7 except the targets new to them.
  - Experimentalist: 1.1–1.5, 2.2, 3.3, 4.2, 4.4, 5.3, 6.5, 7.2 (worked example 7.3) and 7.4; then 8.2, 9.1–9.2, the chapter openings
    and limitboxes of Part II, 13.9, 17.1–17.2 and 17.5–17.6, all of Part III, and 21.7 (the guide to next targets) last.
  - Computational scientist: 3.2–3.6, 4.4, 4.6, 4.9, 5.4–5.6, 6.2–6.4 and App. E, with App. D as needed.
- **Reading paths through Parts II–III** (section level; each persona continues the Part I path above):
  - (a) First-year PhD student: Chaps. 8–22 in order. Skim on a first reading 8.6 beyond its first two outcomes, 10.6, 10.9, 11.5,
    12.6, 13.6, 14.5–14.6, 16.2–16.4 and 19.6, and return when a later section points back. Read 9.4 before the first limitbox of
    Chap. 10, and App. C.14 before 8.8.
  - (b) Particle physicist: 8.1 (Table 8.1 and the comparison with the effective-operator literature), 8.4, 8.5, 9.1–9.4, 10.1–10.2,
    10.7, 11.1–11.4, Chap. 12, 13.1–13.3, 13.9, Chap. 17, 21.3–21.4 and 21.7. Skip 8.2 except Fig. 8.1, 10.9 (boosted dark matter),
    14.6 and 21.1, 21.5 (familiar statistics and halo material). App. C.15 is the Dirac-equation primer for 10.6.
  - (c) Condensed-matter theorist: 8.1–8.2, 8.5–8.7, 9.1, 9.4, then the dark-matter-specific part of each channel section: 10.1–10.2,
    10.4, 10.7, 11.1, 11.4, 11.7, 12.1–12.5, Chap. 13 in full, 14.1, 14.3–14.4, 15.1–15.2, 16.1, 17.5–17.6, and Part III (18.1–18.4,
    19.1–19.2, 19.5 and 21.7 are the core). Skip the standard theory recapped in 10.3, 10.5, 13.7, 15.3, 16.2, 16.7–16.8 and 20.3;
    each of those sections states its dark-matter-specific result in its key results.

---
## Part I — Foundations

Part I uses only established physics. Chapters 1–8 are fully planned below (status PLANNED); Chapter 8 was designed in SPINE-II.
**Order (revised, see change log):** 1 Light dark matter → 2 Transition rates → 3 Electrons in crystals → 4 Linear response →
5 Lattice dynamics → 6 Electron–phonon coupling → 7 Further target materials. The order follows the concept DAG in
ledgers/CONCEPTS.md (checked by ledgers/check_concepts.py over every field of every entry: no concept is used before its defining
section) and mirrors Part II: electronic sector, then lattice, then mixed.

**Per-chapter extras (budgeted separately):** an opening paragraph (≈200 words; the chapter's physical question, why it matters and how it links back and forward, never a section list) at the head of each chapter and a closing summary
section (≈700 words) with a "where this is used later" list, written after all sections of the chapter are accepted:
7 × 900 = 6,300 words.

**Boundary with Appendix E.** Chapters teach the physics of a computed quantity and what each approximation does to it; Appendix E
teaches the method (functionals, basis, convergence parameters, cost, pitfalls). Each method-heavy entry says what it hands to which
E section. **Appendix D** holds every long derivation; every "D.n" named in an entry has a matching row in the Appendix D list and
vice versa.

**Source lines.** "Public" sources may be cited in the book. "Writer only" paths are planning material: they are never cited,
named or paraphrased with their labels in the book (BANNED-TERMS.txt). Equation numbers marked "eq. to fix" must be read from the
full text by the writer before citing; no equation number is cited from memory. Journal data marked "to confirm at briefing" were
entered from standard knowledge in the revision and have not been checked against the source.

### Chapter 1. Light dark matter   [PLANNED; 5 sections, 20,000 words]

### 1.1 The master equation of direct detection   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: State the event rate for spin-independent scattering, derived in Chaps. 2–4, as an integral over the product of halo, coupling
  and target response tied by energy conservation, and use that factorization to organize the book; then give, cited and not derived,
  the inputs from cosmology: the evidence, the cosmological abundance, the open mass range and the case for light dark matter and a light mediator.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the schematic rate ℛ as an integral over halo × coupling × target response with an energy-conserving
  delta function, and name each factor and the part of the book that supplies it; (ii) explain why the factorization separates particle physics from
  condensed-matter physics, and name the ways the book generalizes the product (forward, treated in 8, 10–13, 21); (iii) state
  Ω_c h² = 0.120 ± 0.001 and why gravity leaves the particle mass open, and locate the keV–GeV window against the excitations of solids;
  (iv) state the thermal-relic value ⟨σv⟩ ≈ 2–3 × 10⁻²⁶ cm³ s⁻¹ and explain (Lee–Weinberg) why thermal sub-GeV dark matter needs a light
  mediator or a non-thermal history.
- Prerequisites (concept → section): special relativity, statistical mechanics → READER.md "assumed"; natural units → App. A.1;
  reference appendices → App. A.1.
- Introduces: event rate ℛ and target mass density ρ_T (schematic); the rate factorization (halo × coupling × response, energy-conserving
  delta function); dynamic structure factor S(q, ω) (op.: the target factor; precise in 4.2); dark matter; rotation curves and circular
  speed v_c (qualitative, cited); relic density Ω_c h²; light (sub-GeV) dark matter; WIMP (as a historical benchmark); mediator
  (qualitative); freeze-out and freeze-in (qualitative); Lee–Weinberg bound (qualitative).
- Key results (derived / cited): schematic rate [cited: Kahn and Lin 2108.03239, Eq. (11) after the angular average; derived in 2.5];
  Ω_c h² = 0.1200 ± 0.0012 [cited: Planck 2018 VI, 1807.06209, Table 2 and Eq. (23)]; thermal relic ⟨σv⟩ ≈ 2–3 × 10⁻²⁶ cm³ s⁻¹
  [cited: Steigman, Dasgupta and Beacom 1204.3622, abstract and Sect. II C]; Ω ∝ 1/⟨σv⟩ [cited: Lin 1904.07915, Eq. (32)]; heavy-mediator
  scaling and Lee–Weinberg bound [cited: Lin 1904.07915, Eq. (44); Lee and Weinberg, Phys. Rev. Lett. 39, 165 (1977)].
- Sources: Public: Kahn and Lin 2108.03239; Bertone and Hooper 1605.04909; Clowe et al. (2006); Planck 2018 VI 1807.06209; Lin, TASI
  lectures 1904.07915 §§1–2; Steigman, Dasgupta and Beacom 1204.3622; Green 1112.0524. Writer only:
  surveys/dm-solid-state-processes/README.md §1.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 1.1 the mass range of dark-matter candidates with the keV–GeV window and the energy scales of
  solid-state excitations. No worked example.
- Threads (open/advance/close): none.
- Budget (words): 2,000 (user directive 2026-11-02: the rate factorization up front; cosmology cited, not derived).

### 1.2 The local halo   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Give the local dark-matter density and velocity distribution that every rate in the book integrates over.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the standard halo model in the galactic and laboratory frames, with escape-speed
  truncation, using a Galilean change of frame; (ii) compute its normalization N_esc in closed form; (iii) assemble the Earth's velocity
  v_E(t) from the solar and orbital velocities and explain annual modulation; (iv) derive the angle between v_E and a laboratory axis
  over a sidereal day and explain why an anisotropic target shows daily modulation; (v) estimate the dark-matter number flux Φ_χ
  through a detector for a given mass.
- Prerequisites: statistical mechanics → READER.md "assumed"; halo, local density (qualitative) → 1.1; reference appendices → App. A.1,
  A.8, C.8.
- Introduces: Galilean change of frame (recap); local density ρ_χ; velocity distribution f_χ(v); standard halo model (SHM); v₀, v_esc,
  v_⊙, v_⊕(t), v_E(t); N_esc; the reference speed v_E^ref = |v_⊙| and the maximum speed v_max = v_esc + v_E^ref; annual modulation;
  laboratory axes, Earth rotation and daily modulation (the target side is treated in Chap. 10); number flux Φ_χ.
- Key results: SHM parameters ρ_χ = 0.3 GeV cm⁻³, v₀ = 238 km/s, v_esc = 544 km/s, v_⊙ components, v_⊕(t) [cited: Baxter et al.
  2105.00599, recommended values and Eq. (11) for v_⊕(t)]; N_esc closed form, with the error function of App. C.8 [derived in D.1];
  v_max = 794.5 km/s [derived from the cited parameters]; laboratory-frame direction of v_E over a sidereal day [derived; cited: Baxter et
  al. 2105.00599, section to fix].
- Sources: Public: Baxter et al. 2105.00599; Lewin and Smith, Astropart. Phys. 6, 87 (1996); Green 1112.0524 (astrophysical
  uncertainties). Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §8; returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §1–§2 (halo integrals).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 1.1 SHM parameters with sources; Fig. 1.3 laboratory-frame speed distribution in June and
  December; Fig. 1.4 the angle between v_E and a fixed laboratory axis over one sidereal day. Worked example 1.2: N_esc and the mean
  inverse speed ⟨1/v⟩ of the SHM; worked example 1.3: number flux of 100 MeV dark matter through 1 cm².
- Threads: none (prepares T1).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 1.3 Kinematics of slow dark matter   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive what energy and momentum slow dark matter can give a target, and the speed integral that every scattering rate contains.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the energy transfer ω_q = q·v − q²/2m_χ − Δ_χ and the minimum speed v_min(q, ω);
  (ii) derive the threshold mass m_min = 2E_th/v_max² and evaluate it for eV and meV thresholds; (iii) compare typical momentum
  transfers with the electron momentum scale αm_e and the interatomic scale 2π/a, and show why nuclear recoils deposit little energy
  for light dark matter; (iv) define η(v_min) and evaluate it in the standard halo model; (v) explain the kinematic edge of a rate.
- Prerequisites: special relativity → READER.md "assumed"; Galilean change of frame, f_χ, v_E, v_max, N_esc → 1.2; reference appendices →
  App. A.4, A.8, C.8.
- Introduces: elastic two-body kinematics (recap); momentum transfer q (from dark matter to target; never folded); energy transfer ω (to
  the target); ω_q; mass splitting Δ_χ (inelastic, exothermic for Δ_χ < 0); v_min; reduced masses μ_χe, μ_χN, μ_χp; threshold energy E_th;
  threshold mass m_min; kinematic edge; η(v_min); electron momentum scale q₀ = αm_e; interatomic momentum scale 2π/a (qualitative;
  precise in 3.1); nuclear recoil energy E_R = q²/2M; the energy scales of electronic excitations (eV) and lattice vibrations, phonons
  (10–100 meV), each defined in one sentence (precise in 3.3 and 5.3); band gap E_g of an insulator (qualitative: the least energy that
  frees an electron, 1.1 eV in silicon; precise in 3.3).
- Key results: ω_q and v_min [derived]; maximum deposit ½m_χv² for a target that can absorb any momentum [derived]; m_min [derived];
  maximum nuclear recoil energy 2μ_χN²v²/M [derived]; η(v_min) for the SHM in closed form [derived in D.1; cross-check against
  Lewin and Smith (1996), eq. to fix].
- Sources: Public: Lin 1904.07915 §4; Essig, Mardon and Volansky 1108.5383 §2; Kahn and Lin 2108.03239 §2; Baxter et al. 2105.00599.
  Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §3, §8; returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §2 (2.1); returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §2–§3 (η, kinematic edge).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 1.5 the accessible (q, ω) region for m_χ = 0.1, 1, 10, 100 MeV with the electronic,
  optical-phonon and acoustic-phonon scales overlaid; Fig. 1.6 η(v_min) of the SHM. Worked example 1.4: minimum mass to deposit a
  1.1 eV electronic threshold (the silicon gap, ≈ 0.3 MeV) and a 30 meV phonon; worked example 1.5: maximum nuclear recoil energy for
  1 GeV dark matter on silicon.
- Threads: opens T1.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 1.4 Light dark-matter models   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Introduce the interactions light dark matter can have with ordinary matter and the conventions that express their strength.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Yukawa potential from the static field equation of a massive mediator with point
  sources (classical field theory, App. C.9) and its Fourier transform Ṽ(q); (ii) state the definitions of σ̄_e and F_DM(q), and their
  heavy- and light-mediator limits; (iii) remove kinetic mixing by a field redefinition of the classical Lagrangian and show that the
  dark photon then couples to the electromagnetic current with strength ζe; (iv) distinguish scattering from absorption of bosonic dark
  matter (ω ≈ m_φ, |𝐩_φ| ≈ m_φv); (v) relate the nucleon and nucleus cross-sections through coherence.
- Prerequisites: special relativity, classical electromagnetism → READER.md "assumed"; classical field Lagrangians, static Klein–Gordon
  Green's function → App. C.9; q, q₀ = αm_e, Δ_χ, μ_χe → 1.3; reference appendices → App. A.9, C.9.
- Introduces: Yukawa potential; mediator mass m_V; couplings y_χ, y_e, y_N; Fourier potential Ṽ(q); reference cross-section σ̄_e
  (a definition in vacuum at q₀; its Born derivation is precise in 2.2); dark-matter form factor F_DM(q); heavy and light mediators; dark
  photon and kinetic mixing ζ; in-medium mixing (a caveat that the medium changes the effective coupling; forward: 4.4 and Chap. 10);
  axion-like particles (g_aee, g_aγγ) and scalar dark matter, with their non-relativistic absorption couplings stated (treated in
  Chap. 8); absorption of bosonic dark matter φ and its momentum 𝐩_φ; σ_p, σ_N; inelastic dark matter (Δ_χ revisited); a forward note that
  a general non-relativistic operator basis exists (treated in Chap. 8).
- Key results: Yukawa potential and Ṽ(q) = y_χy_e/(q² + m_V²) [derived; the Green's function in App. C.9]; F_DM(q) = (m_V² +
  α²m_e²)/(m_V² + q²) [cited: Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275 Eq. (12)]; σ̄_e = μ_χe²|Ṽ(q₀)|²/π [definition; cited 2101.08275 Eq. (11)
  and 1108.5383]; σ_N = σ_p(μ_χN/μ_χp)²A² [cited: Lewin and Smith (1996), eq. to fix]; dark-photon mixing [derived as a field
  redefinition; cited: Holdom, Phys. Lett. B 166, 196 (1986)]; absorption couplings of axion-like and scalar dark matter [cited, stated:
  Mitridate et al. 2106.12586 Eq. (18) and Table I].
- Sources: Public: Lin 1904.07915; Essig, Mardon and Volansky 1108.5383; Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275; Holdom (1986);
  Hochberg, Lin and Zurek 1608.01994 (in-medium mixing, named); Mitridate et al. 2106.12586; Fitzpatrick et al. 1203.3542 (named only).
  Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §7 (vertex anchor), §9; returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 1.2 interaction types with coupling, target quantity and dominant excitation (in words); Fig. 1.7
  F_DM(q)² for heavy and light mediators against q/q₀. Worked example 1.6: σ̄_e for a heavy dark-photon mediator in terms of ζ, the dark
  coupling and m_V (cited 1108.5383, eq. to fix).
- Threads: none (the in-medium caveat prepares T2).
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 1.5 Detector signals   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Connect an energy deposit to the quanta a detector records and to an exclusion plot, and lay out the chain the book follows.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) state, for each of the four readout channels (charge, phonons/heat, quasiparticles, photons),
  the quantum it counts and the energy per quantum in silicon or aluminium; (ii) read an exclusion plot in (m_χ, σ̄_e) and explain its
  low-mass edge and its slopes from the scaling ℛ ∝ σ̄_e ρ_χ ⟨F_DM²⟩/m_χ (stated heuristically; the rate itself is precise in 4.2);
  (iii) derive the 2.3-event Poisson upper limit at 90% confidence with zero background and turn it into a cross-section limit for a toy
  rate; (iv) list the inputs of a limit and say which are theory and which are measured.
- Prerequisites: σ̄_e, F_DM → 1.4; m_min, η(v_min), kinematic edge, band gap (qualitative) → 1.3; ρ_χ → 1.2; reference appendices →
  App. A.8.
- Introduces: Poisson distribution (recap); phonons as heat carriers and quasiparticles of a superconductor, as readout quanta
  (qualitative; precise in 5.3 and 7.2); electron–hole pair; ionization yield N_eh (qualitative); mean energy per pair ε_eh (quoted);
  spectrum dℛ/dω (ℛ and ρ_T now from 1.1); exposure; exclusion limit; signal chain (response → deposit → quanta →
  sensor); theory uncertainty (qualitative).
- Key results: Poisson 90% upper limit of 2.3 events [derived]; limit scaling σ̄_e ∝ 1/exposure [derived]; ε_eh ≈ 3.6–3.8 eV in silicon
  [cited: Ramanathan and Kurinsky 2004.10709, eq. to fix].
- Sources: Public: Essig et al. 2203.08297; Kahn and Lin 2108.03239 §§5–6; Ramanathan and Kurinsky 2004.10709. Writer only:
  surveys/dm-solid-state-processes/README.md §1–§2, catalog-analysis-and-gaps.md (analysis chain).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 1.8 the signal chain from dark matter to sensor, annotated with the chapters that treat each
  step; Fig. 1.9 schematic exclusion plot with the edge and the light- and heavy-mediator slopes. Worked example 1.7: expected events
  for a toy rate and a 1 kg-year exposure, and the resulting limit.
- Threads: opens T6, T7.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### Chapter 2. Transition rates   [PLANNED; 5 sections, 21,000 words]

### 2.1 Fermi's golden rule   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive transition rates into a continuum from first-order time-dependent perturbation theory, with the δ-function limit made
  precise.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the first-order transition probability and its long-time limit 2πδ(E_f − E_i)t; (ii) state
  the conditions (continuum of final states, time window) under which a rate exists; (iii) derive the rates for a static and a harmonic
  perturbation (absorption and emission); (iv) convert sums over final states with box normalization, compare it with the relativistic
  normalization (2E particles per unit volume) of particle physics, and obtain a cross-section from a rate and a flux.
- Prerequisites: time-dependent perturbation theory, Fourier analysis → READER.md "assumed"; Fourier and box conventions → App. A.3, C.1,
  C.2; reference appendices → App. A.3, C.1, C.2.
- Introduces: transition rate; golden rule; density of final states 𝒟_f(E); box normalization (Σ_p ↔ V∫d³p/(2π)³); relativistic
  normalization (one-paragraph comparison); the δ-function limit; flux; cross-section from a rate.
- Key results: Γ_{i→f} = 2π|⟨f|H′|i⟩|²δ(E_f − E_i) [derived]; harmonic-perturbation rates [derived]; the limit
  sin²(xt/2)/x² → (πt/2)δ(x) [stated; distributional proof in App. C.1].
- Sources: Public: Sakurai and Napolitano, Modern Quantum Mechanics §5.7; Cohen-Tannoudji, Diu and Laloë, Quantum Mechanics Ch. XIII.
  Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 2.1 the function sin²(xt/2)/x² narrowing with t. Worked example 2.1: a harmonic perturbation
  coupled to a flat continuum: linear growth, the rate, and the breakdown time.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 3,000.

### 2.2 Born approximation and form factors   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive dark-matter cross-sections and rates off free and bound targets in the first Born approximation, and make the
  reference cross-sections of Sect. 1.4 precise.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive dσ/dΩ_q = (μ/2π)²|Ṽ(q)|² and dσ/dq; (ii) derive the rate off a bound electron in terms
  of the transition form factor ⟨f|e^{iq·r}|i⟩, and evaluate the hydrogen 1s elastic form factor F_1s(q); (iii) show that σ̄_e reproduces
  the free-electron cross-section at q₀; (iv) derive the coherent dark-matter–nucleus cross-section with a nuclear form factor.
- Prerequisites: golden rule, box normalization, flux → 2.1; Ṽ(q), σ̄_e, F_DM, σ_p → 1.4; q, ω, v_min → 1.3; Fourier sign convention
  e^{+iq·r} → App. A.3; reference appendices → App. A.3, A.4, A.9, C.1, C.3.
- Introduces: first Born approximation (precise); solid angle dΩ_q of the scattered momentum; transition form factor f_{i→f}(q) of a single
  particle; elastic form factor; hydrogen 1s elastic form factor F_1s(q); atomic ionization form factor (named); Helm form factor
  F_Helm(q); coherent enhancement A².
- Key results: Born cross-section [derived]; dσ/dq = σq/(2μ²v²) for a contact interaction [derived]; bound-target rate
  Γ = ∫d³q/(2π)³ |Ṽ(q)|² Σ_f |f_{i→f}(q)|² 2πδ(E_f − E_i − ω_q) [derived]; F_1s(q) = 16/(4 + q²a₀²)² [derived];
  Helm form factor [cited: Helm, Phys. Rev. 104, 1466 (1956); Lewin and Smith (1996), eq. to fix].
- Computational connection: one sentence saying that atomic ionization form factors are computed from self-consistent atomic orbitals
  (App. E.7; the self-consistent-field method is precise in 3.4).
- Sources: Public: Sakurai and Napolitano §6.3; Lewin and Smith (1996); Essig, Mardon and Volansky 1108.5383 (atomic form factor);
  Essig et al. 1509.01598 (named as the crystal analogue, treated in Chap. 10). Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §2.1, §3.2
  (bookkeeping line); CONVENTIONS.md §2, §9.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 2.2 hydrogen 1s form factor against qa₀ with q₀ marked; Fig. 2.3 Helm form factors of Si and Ge.
  Worked example 2.2: the hydrogen 1s elastic form factor; worked example 2.3: dark-matter scattering off a free electron reproduces
  σ̄_e; worked example 2.4: the A² enhancement for silicon.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 2.3 Second quantization   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Introduce Fock space so that many-electron states, and later phonon and magnon states (forward: 5.3, 7.1), and their operators are written in one language.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) build fermionic and bosonic Fock spaces from (anti)symmetrized states and use (anti)commutation
  relations; (ii) write any one-body operator, in particular the density n_e(q) = Σ_i e^{iq·r_i}, as a bilinear in creation and
  annihilation operators in any single-particle basis; (iii) write the Coulomb interaction with v_C(k) = e²/k² in momentum space, with
  the neutralizing background, and convert it to Gaussian and SI units; (iv) change single-particle basis; (v) write the Coulomb
  Hamiltonian of electrons and point nuclei that Sect. 5.1 starts from.
- Prerequisites: linear algebra, harmonic oscillator → READER.md "assumed"; box normalization → 2.1; Fourier and charge conventions →
  App. A.2, A.3; reference appendices → App. A.2, A.3.
- Introduces: identical particles, (anti)symmetrized states and Slater determinants (recap); Fock space; fermion operators c†, c; boson
  operators (generic; phonon and magnon operators are precise in 5.3 and 7.1); field operator ψ̂(r); one- and two-body operators;
  number operator; normal ordering; Heaviside–Lorentz Coulomb kernel v_C(k), with the Gaussian and SI forms given in the text; Coulomb
  Hamiltonian of electrons and nuclei (electron–electron, electron–nucleus and nucleus–nucleus terms).
- Key results: second-quantized one- and two-body operators [derived; full proof in D.2]; Coulomb Hamiltonian in momentum space, with
  k = 0 removed by neutrality [derived]; n_e(q) as Σ_k c†_{k+q}c_k for plane waves [derived]; electron–nucleus Coulomb Hamiltonian
  [derived].
- Sources: Public: Bruus and Flensberg, Many-Body Quantum Theory in Condensed Matter Physics Ch. 1; Fetter and Walecka Ch. 1;
  Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 1. Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §1, §2; returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §1 (1.1).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 2.1 first- and second-quantized forms of the operators used in the book, including n_e(q) for
  plane waves and in a general basis. Worked example 2.5: the homogeneous electron gas Hamiltonian in second quantization, with the
  background and the k = 0 term, extended to electrons and point nuclei.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 2.4 Second-order processes   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive transition amplitudes at second order, with intermediate states and energy denominators, and the regularization they
  need at resonance.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the second-order amplitude Σ_m ⟨f|V|m⟩⟨m|V|i⟩/(E_i − E_m + i0) from the Dyson series
  of the interaction picture; (ii) enumerate the time orderings when a quantum is absorbed and another emitted; (iii) derive exponential
  decay and a Lorentzian line from the Wigner–Weisskopf treatment; (iv) split 1/(x + i0) into principal value and δ parts, and handle a
  double pole with the Hadamard finite part (motivated by the resonant denominators that return in 6.5); (v) explain when a second-order
  process dominates (first order forbidden or kinematically closed).
- Prerequisites: time-dependent perturbation theory → READER.md "assumed"; golden rule → 2.1; Fock space → 2.3; principal value,
  Sokhotski–Plemelj, finite part → App. C.4; reference appendices → App. C.4.
- Introduces: interaction picture and Dyson series (recap); T-matrix (Lippmann–Schwinger, in brief); intermediate (virtual) state;
  energy denominator; time ordering; resonance; finite width Γ_w; Lorentzian (Breit–Wigner) line; principal value (PV); Hadamard finite
  part (fp); on- and off-shell intermediate states.
- Key results: second-order golden rule [derived]; Wigner–Weisskopf decay [derived in D.3; cited: Weisskopf and Wigner, Z. Phys. 63, 54
  (1930)]; Sokhotski–Plemelj [App. C.4].
- Sources: Public: Sakurai and Napolitano §5.7–5.9; Cohen-Tannoudji, Dupont-Roc and Grynberg, Atom–Photon Interactions Ch. I and III.
  Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M4 (resonances; for the writer's orientation only, none of its terminology enters the book).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 2.4 the two time orderings of absorbing one quantum and emitting another; Fig. 2.5 real and
  imaginary parts of a regularized energy denominator. Worked example 2.6: two-photon absorption in a three-level system, on and off
  resonance, with the finite width.
- Threads: none (prepares 6.5).
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 2.5 Thermal states and detailed balance   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Extend transition rates to targets in thermal equilibrium, derive detailed balance, and fix the zero-temperature Fermi gas
  that Chap. 4 uses (forward: 4.4, 4.5).
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the Gibbs density matrix, the grand-canonical state with chemical potential μ_ch, and
  thermal averages; (ii) derive the thermally averaged rate Σ_i p_iΓ_{i→f}; (iii) derive detailed balance for the transition sum
  𝒮_O(ω) = Σ_if p_i|⟨f|O|i⟩|²δ(ω − E_f + E_i), 𝒮_{O†}(−ω) = e^{−βω}𝒮_O(ω), which becomes 𝒮_O(−ω) = e^{−βω}𝒮_O(ω) for an
  inversion- or time-reversal-symmetric target, and explain energy transfers ω < 0 at T > 0; (iv) write the Bose and Fermi occupations of
  a mode in second-quantized form, and take the T → 0 limit of the free Fermi gas: the Fermi sea, k_F = (3π²𝓃_e)^{1/3}, E_F and the
  density of states per spin at the Fermi level 𝒩_F.
- Prerequisites: ensembles, Bose and Fermi distributions → READER.md "assumed"; golden rule → 2.1; Fock space, plane-wave operators →
  2.3.
- Introduces: density matrix ϱ̂; Gibbs weight p_i; partition function 𝒵; β = 1/T; chemical potential μ_ch; thermal average ⟨·⟩;
  stationary state; transition sum 𝒮_O(ω) (op.; identified with the response matrix, precise in 4.2); detailed balance; Bose function
  n_B and Fermi function n_F in second-quantized form; free Fermi gas at T = 0: Fermi sea, mean density 𝓃_e, k_F, E_F = k_F²/2m_e,
  v_F = k_F/m_e, 𝒩_F = m_ek_F/2π².
- Key results: thermal golden rule [derived]; detailed balance [derived]; emission ∝ n + 1, absorption ∝ n for a thermal oscillator
  [derived]; k_F, E_F and 𝒩_F of the free Fermi gas [derived].
- Sources: Public: Bruus and Flensberg Ch. 1; Landau and Lifshitz, Statistical Physics Part 1 §§31, 53–54; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 2
  (free Fermi gas). Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §6 (detailed balance).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 2.6 upward and downward transitions of a thermal two-level system. Worked example 2.7: scattering
  off a thermal harmonic oscillator; the n and n + 1 factors and the ratio of energy-loss to energy-gain rates.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 3,000.

### Chapter 3. Electrons in crystals   [PLANNED; 6 sections, 30,000 words]

### 3.1 Lattices and reciprocal space   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Describe crystal structure and its Fourier dual so that momentum transfers can be measured against crystal scales.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) construct the diamond and zincblende structures (Si, Ge, GaAs) as an fcc lattice with a two-atom
  basis; (ii) construct the reciprocal lattice and the fcc Brillouin zone with its high-symmetry points; (iii) prove the lattice sum
  Σ_R e^{ik·R} = N Σ_G δ_{k,G} under Born–von Karman conditions and count k points; (iv) split any q into q_r + G_q and explain Umklapp;
  (v) compute the basis structure factor and its extinctions.
- Prerequisites: Fourier analysis → READER.md "assumed"; box normalization → 2.1; interatomic momentum scale → 1.3; reference appendices →
  App. A.3, C.2, C.17.
- Introduces: Bravais lattice; primitive vectors 𝐚_i; lattice constant a; basis; lattice vector R; cell volume Ω (the only meaning of bare
  Ω); number of cells N; V = NΩ; reciprocal lattice, 𝐛_i, G; first Brillouin zone (BZ); high-symmetry points Γ, X, L, K; crystal momentum;
  Born–von Karman (BvK) conditions; reduced momentum q_r and G_q; Umklapp; basis structure factor; the static lattice (nuclei fixed at
  equilibrium) as an explicit working assumption for Chaps. 3–4, justified in 5.1.
- Key results: lattice sum and k-point counting [derived]; BZ volume (2π)³/Ω [derived]; q = q_r + G_q decomposition [definition].
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin, Solid State Physics Ch. 4, 5, 8 (BvK); Kittel, Introduction to Solid State Physics Ch. 1–2.
  Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §1, §2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 3.1 the diamond structure; Fig. 3.2 the fcc Brillouin zone with high-symmetry points; Fig. 3.3 a
  momentum transfer reduced to the first zone (Umklapp). Worked example 3.1: in silicon (a = 5.431 Å) 2π/a ≈ 2.3 keV, compared with q for
  m_χ = 1, 10, 100 MeV; worked example 3.2: the diamond basis structure factor and the absent (200) reflection.
- Threads: advances T1 (q against the zone scale).
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 3.2 Bloch states and band structure   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the form of one-electron states in a periodic potential and the matrix elements of e^{iq·r} and of the velocity between
  them, which every electronic dark-matter rate and every optical rate contains.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) prove Bloch's theorem; (ii) derive the nearly-free-electron gap 2|U_G| at a zone boundary;
  (iii) set up the LCAO overlap problem, derive a tight-binding band and explain sp³ bonding qualitatively; (iv) derive the
  crystal-momentum selection rule for ρ_mn(k, q) = ⟨ψ_{m,k+q}|e^{iq·r}|ψ_{nk}⟩, with the Umklapp vector kept in the plane wave, and its
  normalization ρ_nn(k, 0) = 1; (v) define the momentum and velocity matrix elements p_mn(k) and v_mn(k), show that v_nn(k) is the group
  velocity, and derive the optical limit ρ_mn(k, q) ≈ q·v_mn(k)/(ε_mk − ε_nk) for m ≠ n (phase convention of App. A.5).
- Prerequisites: reciprocal lattice, BZ, Umklapp, BvK → 3.1; transition form factor → 2.2; Fock space (Bloch basis) → 2.3; reference
  appendices → App. A.5, C.2, C.17.
- Introduces: Bloch's theorem; ψ_nk (normalized over V), u_nk (normalized over Ω); band index n; band energy ε_nk; periodic gauge;
  nearly-free electrons; tight binding, LCAO overlap, hopping t_hop; band structure; group velocity v^g_n(k); momentum and velocity
  matrix elements p_mn(k), v_mn(k); optical limit of the density vertex; density vertex ρ_mn(k, q); occupation f_nk.
- Key results: Bloch's theorem [derived; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 8]; nearly-free-electron gap [derived; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 9];
  tight binding [derived; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 10]; selection rule and plane-wave expansion of ρ_mn(k, q) [derived]; v_nn = v^g_n and
  the optical limit of ρ_mn [derived].
- Computational connection: one paragraph saying that ε_nk and u_nk are computed with density-functional theory (forward: 3.4) and that
  ρ_mn and v_mn are evaluated from plane-wave coefficients (App. E.7).
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 8–10; Harrison, Electronic Structure and the Properties of Solids Ch. 3 (sp³ bonding);
  Yu and Cardona, Fundamentals of Semiconductors Ch. 2 (momentum matrix elements); Essig et al. 1509.01598 (crystal form factor, as the
  use made in Chap. 10). Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 3.4 nearly-free-electron bands in one dimension with gaps; Fig. 3.5 tight-binding band of an fcc
  s-band. Worked example 3.3: the one-dimensional gap; worked example 3.4: ρ_mn(k, q) between the two lowest nearly-free bands: its
  O(q) suppression at small q through v_mn, and its Umklapp part.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### 3.3 Silicon, germanium and gallium arsenide   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Introduce the band structures of the book's running materials and the features that set electronic thresholds.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) locate on the band structures of Si, Ge and GaAs the indirect and direct gaps and the valley
  minima, and state their values; (ii) derive the effective-mass tensor from k·p theory with the velocity matrix elements of 3.2;
  (iii) derive the spin–orbit splitting Δ_SO of the valence-band maximum from atomic spin–orbit coupling; (iv) define the minimum pair
  energy E_min(q) and explain why, in an indirect-gap material, a vertical (q ≈ 0) excitation at the gap needs a phonon or a momentum near
  k_c; (v) compare E_min(q) with the kinematic reach of Sect. 1.3.
- Prerequisites: Bloch states, ε_nk, ρ_mn, v_mn → 3.2; BZ points → 3.1; band gap (qualitative), kinematic reach, m_min → 1.3; spin,
  hydrogen atom → READER.md "assumed".
- Introduces: atomic spin–orbit coupling and the fine structure of hydrogen (recap); valence and conduction bands (band subscripts v, c
  only); valence-band maximum, conduction-band minimum; indirect gap E_g (precise); minimum direct gap E_dir; CBM momentum k_c; valleys;
  effective-mass tensor m*; k·p theory; heavy, light and split-off holes; Δ_SO; E_min(q); intrinsic carrier density (brief); the
  phonon-assisted window (qualitative).
- Key results: k·p effective mass [derived]; gap, valley and Δ_SO values for Si, Ge, GaAs [cited: Yu and Cardona, Fundamentals of
  Semiconductors, Ch. 2 tables, table no. to fix]; E_min(q) [definition].
- Computational connection: one paragraph: the bands and masses of Table 3.1 are computed with density-functional theory and
  quasiparticle corrections (precise in 3.4; App. E.1, E.3) and checked against measured gaps and cyclotron masses.
- Sources: Public: Yu and Cardona Ch. 2; Chelikowsky and Cohen, Phys. Rev. B 14, 556 (1976); Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 28. Writer only:
  returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §3 (thresholds); returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M1, M3 (onset kinematics; orientation only); CONVENTIONS.md §8 (reference
  speeds).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 3.6 silicon bands with E_g, E_dir and k_c marked; Fig. 3.7 Ge and GaAs bands; Table 3.1 gaps,
  valleys, effective masses and Δ_SO for Si, Ge, GaAs. Worked example 3.5: the momentum to reach the silicon conduction-band minimum (≈ 0.85
  of Γ–X) against the q available to 1 MeV dark matter; worked example 3.6: m_min for the direct gaps of Si and Ge and the indirect gap of
  Ge (the silicon indirect gap was worked example 1.4).
- Threads: opens T5 (seed); advances T1.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 3.4 Many electrons and density-functional theory   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Explain how the one-electron bands of a real crystal are obtained from the many-electron problem, and what is wrong with them.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Hartree–Fock equations from the variational principle, interpret exchange, and state
  Koopmans' theorem; (ii) state and prove the Hohenberg–Kohn theorems and derive the Kohn–Sham equations; (iii) explain what an
  exchange–correlation approximation (LDA, GGA, hybrid) is; (iv) explain the band-gap problem and its effect on thresholds; (v) derive how a
  rigid scissor shift Δ_scis changes ε_nk and rescales the interband v_mn by (ε_ck − ε_vk + Δ_scis)/(ε_ck − ε_vk), and state why
  quasiparticle gaps exceed Kohn–Sham gaps (screened exchange, qualitative).
- Prerequisites: Fock space, Coulomb Hamiltonian → 2.3; Bloch states, v_mn → 3.2; gaps of Si, Ge, GaAs → 3.3; reference appendices → App. C.17.
- Introduces: variational principle (recap); Slater determinant (in the many-body context); Hartree–Fock; exchange; Koopmans' theorem;
  electron density 𝓃(r); Hohenberg–Kohn theorems; Kohn–Sham equations, V_KS, v_xc, E_xc; LDA, GGA, hybrid functionals (named); Kohn–Sham
  eigenvalues versus quasiparticle energies; band-gap problem; scissor shift Δ_scis; quasiparticle gap corrections (qualitative: the
  screened-exchange picture; the GW self-energy is precise in 4.8).
- Key results: Hartree–Fock equations and Koopmans' theorem [derived in D.4]; Hohenberg–Kohn theorems and Kohn–Sham equations [derived
  in D.5; cited: Hohenberg and Kohn, Phys. Rev. 136, B864 (1964); Kohn and Sham, Phys. Rev. 140, A1133 (1965)]; scissor rescaling of v_mn
  [derived]; LDA and quasiparticle gaps of Si [cited: Hybertsen and Louie, Phys. Rev. B 34, 5390 (1986), table no. to fix; the GW method
  is precise in 4.8].
- Boundary with App. E: this section teaches what Kohn–Sham and quasiparticle energies are and what the gap problem does to a
  threshold. App. E.1 (functionals, k-point sampling, smearing, convergence, cost) and E.3 (scissor and quasiparticle corrections in
  practice) carry the method.
- Sources: Public: Martin, Electronic Structure Ch. 6–9; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 17; Hybertsen and Louie (1986); Aryasetiawan and
  Gunnarsson, Rep. Prog. Phys. 61, 237 (1998). Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §1 (approximation ladder, scissor).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 3.8 silicon bands near the gap in LDA, with quasiparticle corrections and from experiment.
  Worked example 3.7: the shift of the silicon m_min from the LDA gap to the corrected gap.
- Threads: advances T1 and T7: the threshold moves with the gap (T1); the gap problem is a theory uncertainty (T7).
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### 3.5 Plane waves, pseudopotentials and core states   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Explain how Bloch states are represented in practice and why the region near the nuclei matters at large momentum transfer.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) expand u_nk in plane waves and choose the cutoff E_cut needed for a given q; (ii) state the
  norm-conservation conditions and explain what a pseudopotential removes; (iii) write the projector-augmented-wave transformation and the
  all-electron reconstruction of ρ_mn(k, q); (iv) identify the core and semicore states of Si and Ge and the q at which they matter;
  (v) estimate relativistic corrections for core electrons as (Zα)² and explain core spin–orbit splitting.
- Prerequisites: Kohn–Sham equations → 3.4; ρ_mn(k, q) → 3.2; G vectors → 3.1; atomic spin–orbit coupling and hydrogen fine structure →
  3.3; reference appendices → App. C.3, C.15.
- Introduces: plane-wave basis; kinetic cutoff E_cut; pseudopotential; norm conservation; core radius r_c; nonlocal pseudopotential;
  projector augmented waves (PAW), with projectors p̃_j and partial waves φ_j^AE, φ_j^PS; all-electron reconstruction; core and semicore
  states; scalar-relativistic treatment; (Zα)² estimate.
- Key results: norm-conservation conditions [cited: Hamann, Schlüter and Chiang, Phys. Rev. Lett. 43, 1494 (1979)]; PAW transformation
  [cited: Blöchl, Phys. Rev. B 50, 17953 (1994), eq. to fix]; E_cut ≳ (q_max + G_needed)²/2m_e estimate [derived]; (Zα)² for Si 1s, Ge 1s,
  Ge 3d [derived estimate].
- Boundary with App. E: physics of the representation here; pseudopotential choice, PAW datasets, reconstruction recipes and their
  convergence in E.2 and E.7.
- Sources: Public: Martin Ch. 11–13; Hamann 1306.4707; Blöchl (1994); Griffin et al. 2105.05253 (all-electron form factors for dark
  matter). Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §5; returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §1.2 (relativistic estimate).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 3.9 all-electron and pseudo wavefunctions of silicon near a nucleus; Fig. 3.10 schematic weight
  of core, semicore and valence states against q. Worked example 3.8: E_cut needed for q up to 30 keV; worked example 3.9: (Zα)² for Si 1s,
  Ge 1s and Ge 3d.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 3.6 Wannier functions   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Introduce localized Wannier functions and the interpolation they allow, because electron–phonon and phonon-assisted rates
  (Chap. 6 and Part II) need k and Q grids far finer than a direct calculation can afford.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define Wannier functions from Bloch states and explain the gauge freedom U(k); (ii) state the
  spread functional Ω_W, with the periodic position-operator subtlety, and the condition for maximal localization; (iii) derive Wannier
  interpolation of bands from a finite-range real-space Hamiltonian H^W(R); (iv) explain why every interpolated quantity must share one
  gauge.
- Prerequisites: Bloch states, periodic gauge → 3.2; lattice sums → 3.1; Kohn–Sham bands → 3.4; reference appendices → App. A.5.
- Introduces: Wannier function w_n(r − R); gauge matrix U(k); disentanglement; maximally localized Wannier functions; spread functional
  Ω_W; real-space Hamiltonian H^W(R); Wannier interpolation.
- Key results: Wannier transform and its inverse [derived]; exactness of interpolation for finite-range H^W(R) [derived]; spread
  functional [cited: Marzari and Vanderbilt, Phys. Rev. B 56, 12847 (1997); Marzari et al. 1112.5411, eq. to fix].
- Boundary with App. E: the idea and its exactness here; disentanglement windows, projections, convergence and pitfalls in E.5.
- Sources: Public: Marzari et al. 1112.5411; Marzari and Vanderbilt (1997); Souza, Marzari and Vanderbilt, Phys. Rev. B 65, 035109 (2001).
  Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §4 (one gauge for all interpolated tensors).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 3.11 an sp³ Wannier function of silicon; Fig. 3.12 interpolated and directly computed bands.
  Worked example 3.10: a one-dimensional two-band model interpolated from a coarse mesh.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### Chapter 4. Linear response   [PLANNED; 9 sections, 41,500 words]
Outline paragraph: it warns that this is the densest chapter of Part I and gives its internal dependency chart:
4.1 → 4.2 → 4.3 → 4.4 → 4.5 → 4.6 (the response chain: Kubo, structure factor, sum rules, screening, the electron gas, crystals);
4.1 → 4.7 → 4.8 (Green's functions and diagrams); 4.6 and 4.8 → 4.9 (excitons need both local fields and the ladder).

### 4.1 The Kubo formula   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the linear response of a many-body system to a weak external field.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Kubo formula for H_ext = O_b φ_ext(t); (ii) define the retarded response R_ab(q, ω)
  with the book's Fourier and sign conventions; (iii) derive its Lehmann representation; (iv) show that R is analytic in the upper half
  ω-plane and explain why.
- Prerequisites: time-dependent perturbation theory → READER.md "assumed"; interaction picture, Dyson series → 2.4; Gibbs state, thermal
  averages → 2.5; Fock space, n_e(q) → 2.3; temporal Fourier convention → App. A.3; correspondence with the literature's χ → App. B.2;
  reference appendices → App. A.3, A.7, B.2, C.4, C.5.
- Introduces: external potential φ_ext(t); linear response; retarded response function R_ab(q, ω) (the letter χ is reserved for the
  dark-matter particle; App. B.2 gives the correspondence); Kubo formula; Lehmann representation; causality; analyticity in Im ω > 0.
- Key results: Kubo formula [derived; cited: Kubo, J. Phys. Soc. Jpn. 12, 570 (1957)]; Lehmann representation [derived].
- Sources: Public: Giuliani and Vignale, Quantum Theory of the Electron Liquid Ch. 3; Bruus and Flensberg Ch. 6. Writer only:
  returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §3 (3.2); CONVENTIONS.md §2, §6.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.1 poles of R in the complex ω-plane. Worked example 4.1: response of a driven harmonic
  oscillator; worked example 4.2: response of a two-level system.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 4.2 Correlations and the dynamic structure factor   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Define the dynamic structure factor and show that it is the only target quantity in a density-coupled scattering rate.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define S_ab(q, ω) = (2π/V)Σ_{if} p_i⟨i|O_a(q)†|f⟩⟨f|O_b(q)|i⟩δ(ω − E_f + E_i) and identify its
  diagonal with the transition sum 𝒮_O of Sect. 2.5; (ii) derive the Born-approximation rate per dark-matter particle
  Γ = ∫d³q/(2π)³ |Ṽ(q)|² S(q, ω_q) for any target (Van Hove); (iii) derive the fluctuation–dissipation theorem relating S to R; (iv) show
  that S is a positive semidefinite matrix; (v) recover the single-particle rate of Sect. 2.2 as the independent-particle limit.
- Prerequisites: R_ab, Lehmann representation → 4.1; transition sum 𝒮_O, detailed balance → 2.5; Fermi sea, k_F → 2.5; bound-target
  rate, transition form factor → 2.2; ω_q → 1.3; Ṽ(q) → 1.4; reference appendices → App. A.4, A.7, C.7.
- Introduces: response matrix S_ab(q, ω) (precise form of the transition sum); dynamic structure factor S(q, ω) ≡ S_ee; Van Hove
  correlation function 𝒢_VH(r, t); static structure factor S^st(q); fluctuation–dissipation theorem (FDT); positivity of S;
  independent-particle S.
- Key results: Van Hove formula [derived; cited: Van Hove, Phys. Rev. 95, 249 (1954)]; single-operator rate [derived; cited: Knapen,
  Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275 Eqs. (4), (6)]; FDT (1 − e^{−βω})S_aa = −2 Im R_aa [derived]; positivity [derived].
- Sources: Public: Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 3–4; Pines and Nozières, The Theory of Quantum Liquids Vol. I Ch. 2; Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin
  2101.08275. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §3 (3.1), (3.3), §4 (4.1), (4.4); CONVENTIONS.md §6.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.2 S(q, ω) of the non-interacting electron gas with its particle–hole continuum. Worked example
  4.3: the independent-particle S of the electron gas and the boundaries of its continuum; worked example 4.4: the rate formula applied to
  a hydrogen atom reproduces Sect. 2.2.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 4.3 Causality and sum rules   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the exact constraints that causality and commutators place on response functions.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Kramers–Kronig relations; (ii) derive the moments of S from commutators; (iii) derive
  the f-sum rule ∫_{−∞}^{∞}dω ω S(q, ω) = π𝓃_e q²/m_e from [H, n_e(q)] = q·j_e(q) for local potentials, and convert it in one line to
  the per-particle ∫₀^∞ normalization of the standard textbooks; (iv) explain why nonlocal pseudopotentials and relativistic corrections
  modify it, and state its species-resolved form; (v) define oscillator strengths and use the Thomas–Reiche–Kuhn rule as a check on atomic
  data.
- Prerequisites: analyticity, Lehmann representation → 4.1; S, FDT → 4.2; mean density 𝓃_e → 2.5; nonlocal pseudopotential, core
  states → 3.5; contour integrals → READER.md "assumed"; reference appendices → App. A.7, C.5.
- Introduces: Kramers–Kronig (KK) relations; spectral representation; first moment 𝓜¹; f-sum rule; paramagnetic current j_e(q);
  plasma frequency ω_p; oscillator strength f^osc_n0; Thomas–Reiche–Kuhn rule; species-resolved f-sum (electrons and nuclei, D.6);
  mean kinetic energy K̄; sum-rule violation by nonlocal potentials; sum rules as consistency checks.
- Key results: KK [derived]; first-moment identity for any stationary state [derived]; f-sum rule with normalization π over the whole real
  line, and its species-resolved form [derived; commutator algebra in D.6; cited: Giuliani and Vignale §3.4, eq. to fix]; relativistic
  mass-velocity correction to the f-sum, of relative order K̄/m_e [derived estimate].
- Sources: Public: Giuliani and Vignale §3.2–3.4; Pines and Nozières Ch. 2–3; Bethe and Salpeter, Quantum Mechanics of One- and Two-Electron
  Atoms §61 (oscillator strengths). Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §4 (4.2), (4.5), (4.6), (4.6′); CONVENTIONS.md §6 (moments, commutator).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.3 the contour behind the Kramers–Kronig relations; Fig. 4.3b (fig4_3b) the Thomas–Reiche–Kuhn sum
  accumulated with energy for hydrogen and silicon. Worked example 4.5: the Thomas–Reiche–Kuhn sum
  for hydrogen from tabulated oscillator strengths; worked example 4.6: the f-sum of the free-electron S of worked example 4.3; worked
  example 4.7: the f-sum of silicon, four valence electrons per atom against ten core electrons, and the energy range in which each part
  saturates.
- Threads: opens T3.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 4.4 Screening and the dielectric function   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Separate the long-range Coulomb interaction from the rest of the response, define the dielectric function, the loss function
  and the optical conductivity, and express the dynamic structure factor through them.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive R = R̃ + R̃ v_C R as an algebraic geometric series and the definitions ε = 1 − v_C R̃,
  ε^{-1} = 1 + v_C R; (ii) define the dielectric matrix ε_GG′(q, ω) for any proper response R̃ and derive the macroscopic
  ε_mac = 1/(ε^{-1})_00 (D.7); (iii) derive S(q, ω) = q² L(q, ω)/(2πα(1 − e^{−βω})) with L = Im[−(ε^{-1})_00], state when it holds, and
  explain the difference between a probe that couples to electrons only and one that couples to the total charge; (iv) derive the f-sum
  rules for L and ε₂, the optical conductivity σ₁ = ωε₂ (Heaviside–Lorentz units), and the equality of transverse and longitudinal
  dielectric functions as q → 0; (v) derive Thomas–Fermi screening.
- Prerequisites: R, Kubo → 4.1; S, FDT → 4.2; f-sum → 4.3; v_C(k), Coulomb Hamiltonian → 2.3; G, q = q_r + G_q → 3.1; Bloch states
  → 3.2; k_F, 𝒩_F → 2.5; classical electromagnetism → READER.md "assumed"; reference appendices → App. A.2, A.3, A.7, C.7.
- Introduces: Coulomb-proper (irreducible) response R̃; improper response R; dielectric function ε(q, ω), ε₁, ε₂; inverse dielectric
  function; dielectric matrix ε_GG′; local-field effects (formal definition; their physics in the random-phase approximation is precise in
  4.6); macroscopic dielectric function ε_mac; electronic high-frequency dielectric constant ε_∞ (static lattice); loss function L; the
  plasmon as the peak of L where ε₁ crosses zero (qualitative; precise in 4.5); Thomas–Fermi wavevector k_TF; statically screened
  Coulomb interaction ε^{-1}v_C; electron-only versus total-charge probes; optical conductivity σ_cond(ω) = σ₁ + iσ₂; transverse
  dielectric function ε^T; dielectric stopping power −dE/dx.
- Key results: proper/improper Dyson relation [derived; matrix inversion and macroscopic average in D.7]; S–L relation [derived; cited:
  Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275 and Hochberg et al. 2101.08263, eq. to fix]; ∫₀^∞dω ω L = (π/2)ω_p² and ∫₀^∞dω ω ε₂ = (π/2)ω_p² at
  T = 0 [derived]; σ₁ = ωε₂ and ε^T → ε as q → 0 [derived; cited: Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 3, eq. to fix]; Thomas–Fermi [derived];
  dielectric stopping −dE/dx as an integral of ωL [derived; cited: Lindhard (1954), eq. to fix].
- Computational connection: one paragraph: ε_GG′ and L are computed from Kohn–Sham states (App. E.6); measured optical and energy-loss
  data constrain them at small q.
- Sources: Public: Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 3, 5; Onida, Reining and Rubio, Rev. Mod. Phys. 74, 601 (2002); Hochberg et al. 2101.08263;
  Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275; Lindhard (1954) (stopping). Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §5; returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §1;
  CONVENTIONS.md §6, §7.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.4 a schematic of the geometric series, proper parts joined by Coulomb lines (labeled as a
  schematic; its Feynman-rule meaning is given in 4.8, forward: 4.8); Fig. 4.5 measured ε₂(ω) and L(ω) of silicon at q ≈ 0 (source to fix;
  the comparison of the loss peak with ω_p is made once, in worked example 4.10). Worked example 4.8: the Thomas–Fermi length of the
  silicon valence electrons, with k_F from 2.5; worked example 4.9: S(q, ω) from L for a single damped-oscillator dielectric function,
  with its f-sum and the stopping power −dE/dx of a slow charged particle.
- Threads: opens T2; advances T3.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 4.5 The homogeneous electron gas   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Work out the response of the homogeneous electron gas, where the independent-particle bubble and the random-phase
  approximation take closed forms, as the reference model for valence electrons, plasmons and screening.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the independent-particle bubble R⁰ of the gas (the Lindhard function) from the Lehmann
  representation and identify its particle–hole continuum; (ii) derive the random-phase approximation ε = 1 − v_C R⁰, the plasmon
  dispersion to order q² and the Landau-damping onset q_c; (iii) derive the Mermin relaxation-time dielectric function and say why it
  conserves particle number; (iv) check the f-sum and perfect-screening rules on these models.
- Prerequisites: Lehmann representation → 4.1; independent-particle S → 4.2; f-sum → 4.3; R̃, ε, L, k_TF → 4.4; n_F, k_F, v_F, E_F →
  2.5; reference appendices → App. A.7, C.8.
- Introduces: density parameter r_s; independent-particle bubble R⁰ (of the gas); random-phase approximation (RPA, R̃ ≈ R⁰); Lindhard
  dielectric function ε_Lin; plasmon and its dispersion ω_pl(q) (precise); Landau damping, q_c; relaxation rate γ_rel; Mermin dielectric
  function ε_Mer; perfect screening.
- Key results: Lindhard function at T = 0 [derived in D.8; cited: Lindhard, K. Dan. Vidensk. Selsk. Mat.-Fys. Medd. 28, no. 8 (1954);
  Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 4, eq. to fix]; RPA dielectric function of the gas [derived]; plasmon dispersion [derived]; Mermin function
  [derived in D.9; cited: Mermin, Phys. Rev. B 1, 2362 (1970)].
- Sources: Public: Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 4–5; Pines and Nozières Ch. 3–5; Lindhard (1954); Mermin (1970); Hochberg et al. 2101.08263
  (use of these models for dark matter). Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §4 (homogeneous gas and plasmons).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.6 L(q, ω) of the electron gas at r_s = 2 with the plasmon dispersion meeting the continuum.
  Worked example 4.10: the silicon valence electrons as an electron gas: ω_p ≈ 16.6 eV against the measured loss peak of Fig. 4.5.
- Threads: advances T2, T3.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 4.6 Local fields and exchange–correlation kernels   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Carry the random-phase approximation from the electron gas to crystals, explain local-field effects, and add the
  exchange–correlation kernels of time-dependent density-functional theory.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the independent-particle bubble R⁰_GG′(q, ω) of a crystal from Kohn–Sham states
  (Adler–Wiser) and its optical limit through v_mn; (ii) derive the RPA dielectric matrix and explain local-field effects (the average of
  the inverse against the inverse of the average) through their effect on the ε₂ of silicon; (iii) write the time-dependent
  density-functional Dyson equation R = R⁰ + R⁰ f_Hxc R, say what the adiabatic local-density kernel misses, and relate the static
  local-field factor G_LF(q) of the electron gas to f_xc; (iv) order the levels IP → RPA → RPA + local fields → TDDFT and use their spread as
  an uncertainty estimate (the last level is added in 4.9, forward: 4.9).
- Prerequisites: ε_GG′, ε_mac, local fields (formal) → 4.4; R⁰, RPA, Lindhard function → 4.5; Kohn–Sham states, scissor → 3.4; ρ_mn,
  v_mn, f_nk → 3.2; reference appendices → App. A.7, C.7.
- Introduces: crystal bubble R⁰_GG′ (Adler–Wiser); RPA with local fields; exchange–correlation kernel f_xc; Hartree–xc kernel
  f_Hxc = v_C + f_xc; adiabatic LDA kernel; TDDFT; static local-field factor G_LF(q); hierarchy of approximations IP → RPA → RPA + local
  fields → TDDFT.
- Key results: Adler–Wiser R⁰ [derived; cited: Adler, Phys. Rev. 126, 413 (1962); Wiser, Phys. Rev. 129, 62 (1963)]; TDDFT Dyson
  equation [cited: Runge and Gross, Phys. Rev. Lett. 52, 997 (1984); Petersilka, Gossmann and Gross, Phys. Rev. Lett. 76, 1212 (1996)];
  f_xc of the gas from G_LF(q) [derived].
- Boundary with App. E: physics of each level here; basis sizes, local-field cutoffs, kernel choices, k-point convergence and cost in E.6.
- Sources: Public: Onida, Reining and Rubio (2002); Botti et al., Rep. Prog. Phys. 70, 357 (2007); Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 5 (local-field
  factor). Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §1, §3; CONVENTIONS.md §11 (the level list; book uses plain words, no labels).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.7 ε₂ of silicon in IP, RPA, RPA + local fields and experiment (source to fix). Worked example
  4.11: the local-field reduction of ε_∞ of silicon (numbers cited, source to fix).
- Threads: advances T2, T7.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 4.7 Green's functions   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Define the single-particle Green's function at zero and finite temperature, and the self-energy that later chapters use for
  quasiparticle energies and widths.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define retarded and time-ordered single-particle Green's functions 𝖦 at T = 0 and the spectral
  function 𝖠, and derive their Lehmann representations; (ii) define the imaginary-time (Matsubara) Green's function at T > 0 with its
  fermionic and bosonic Matsubara frequencies, and obtain retarded functions from it by analytic continuation (D.10); (iii) derive the
  Dyson equation and interpret the self-energy as an energy shift and a width: quasiparticle energy, residue z_qp and lifetime; (iv) write
  the density response as an imaginary-time correlation function and recover the retarded R of 4.1 by continuation.
- Prerequisites: Fock space → 2.3; Gibbs state, thermal averages, μ_ch → 2.5; Kubo, Lehmann, analyticity → 4.1; widths, Lorentzian →
  2.4; contour integrals → READER.md "assumed"; reference appendices → App. C.5.
- Introduces: single-particle Green's function 𝖦 (retarded, advanced, time-ordered); spectral function 𝖠; imaginary time τ; Matsubara
  Green's function 𝖦(k, iω^F_j); Matsubara frequencies ω^F_j, ω^B_j; analytic continuation; self-energy Σ; Dyson equation;
  quasiparticle, residue z_qp, lifetime.
- Key results: Lehmann representations of 𝖦 and 𝖠 [derived]; Matsubara frequency sums and analytic continuation [derived in D.10];
  Dyson equation [derived]; R as a continued imaginary-time correlation function [derived].
- Sources: Public: Bruus and Flensberg Ch. 8–11 (chapter numbers to confirm at briefing); Mahan, Many-Particle Physics Ch. 2–3; Fetter and
  Walecka Ch. 3, 7. Writer only: none.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.8 the Matsubara contour and its deformation onto the real axis; fig4_8b the spectral function of
  the level coupled to a flat band against a Lorentzian. Worked example 4.12: 𝖦 and 𝖠 of
  a single level coupled to a flat continuum: a Lorentzian spectral function whose width is Im Σ, the Wigner–Weisskopf result of 2.4
  regained.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 4.8 Feynman diagrams   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the diagram rules from Wick's theorem and evaluate the bubble, self-energy, screened-interaction and ladder diagrams
  that Chaps. 4–7 use.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) state Wick's theorem at T = 0 and in imaginary time (proof in D.11) and derive the Feynman rules
  for the Coulomb interaction and for the exchange of a generic boson; (ii) evaluate the bubble with a Matsubara sum, show that it equals
  the R⁰ of 4.5 and 4.6, and sum the RPA chain; (iii) evaluate step by step the second-order self-energy of an electron coupled to an
  Einstein boson at T > 0, with its thermal factors n_B + 1 − n_F and n_B + n_F; (iv) write the GW self-energy Σ = i𝖦W^scr with
  W^scr = ε^{-1}v_C and obtain the electron–electron lifetime from Im Σ; (v) derive the cutting rule: the imaginary part of a diagram is a
  sum over on-shell intermediate states, a golden-rule rate.
- Prerequisites: Matsubara 𝖦, self-energy, Dyson equation, analytic continuation → 4.7; R⁰, RPA → 4.5, 4.6; ε^{-1} → 4.4; second-order
  amplitudes → 2.4; golden rule → 2.1; Fock space → 2.3; n_B, n_F → 2.5; reference appendices → App. C.5.
- Introduces: Wick's theorem; Feynman diagrams and rules; bubble; RPA chain; second-order self-energy of an electron coupled to a boson;
  screened interaction W^scr; GW self-energy; electron–electron lifetime; ladder diagram and the two-particle kernel (the Bethe–Salpeter
  equation is precise in 4.9); cutting rule.
- Key results: Wick's theorem and the Feynman rules [derived in D.11]; bubble = R⁰ [derived]; second-order self-energy with a boson at
  finite T [derived]; GW self-energy [cited: Hedin, Phys. Rev. 139, A796 (1965)]; cutting rule for the bubble and the second-order
  self-energy [derived].
- Computational connection: one paragraph: G₀W₀ quasiparticle energies and lifetimes are computed as in App. E.3.
- Sources: Public: Bruus and Flensberg Ch. 10–13; Mahan Ch. 2–3; Fetter and Walecka Ch. 3, 7; Aryasetiawan and Gunnarsson (1998)
  (GW). Writer only: none.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.9 the bubble, the RPA chain, the ladder and the second-order self-energy; Fig. 4.10 the cut of
  the bubble; fig4_9b the self-energy of an electron coupled to an Einstein boson. Worked example 4.13: the cut of the bubble reproduces the golden-rule sum of 4.2; worked example 4.14: the second-order
  self-energy of an electron coupled to an Einstein boson, which Sect. 6.3 reuses.
- Threads: advances T2 (the screened interaction).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 4.9 Excitons and the Bethe–Salpeter equation   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Add the electron–hole interaction to the response, derive excitons from the ladder, and complete the hierarchy of
  approximations.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the Bethe–Salpeter equation as an electron–hole eigenvalue problem in the transition basis,
  with a direct kernel from W^scr and an exchange kernel from the local-field part of v_C, and justify it from the ladder of 4.8 (D.12);
  (ii) solve the Wannier–Mott limit, E_x^b = μ_xα²/(2ε_∞²), with its hydrogenic envelope; (iii) write the exciton contribution to ε₂ and to
  S(q, ω) at finite q through E_λ(q) and the exciton oscillator strength; (iv) complete the hierarchy IP → RPA → RPA + local fields →
  TDDFT → BSE and use the differences between levels as an uncertainty estimate.
- Prerequisites: ladder diagram, W^scr → 4.8; RPA with local fields, hierarchy → 4.6; ε_∞ → 4.4; S and the rate → 4.2; oscillator
  strength → 4.3; Kohn–Sham states, scissor → 3.4; ρ_mn → 3.2; effective masses → 3.3; hydrogen atom → READER.md "assumed"; reference
  appendices → App. A.7, C.7.
- Introduces: Bethe–Salpeter equation (BSE); exciton; exciton envelope A^λ_{cvk}, energy E_λ; direct and exchange kernels; exciton
  binding E_x^b, radius a_x, reduced mass μ_x; Wannier–Mott exciton; hydrogenic envelope function (reused in 7.4); finite-momentum excitons
  E_λ(q); the full hierarchy of approximations.
- Key results: BSE from the ladder [derived in D.12; cited: Onida, Reining and Rubio (2002), eq. to fix; Rohlfing and Louie, Phys. Rev. B
  62, 4927 (2000)]; Wannier–Mott binding [derived; a forward note says that 5.5 adds the lattice contribution, which matters when E_x^b is
