  small against optical-phonon energies, so that the ions follow the pair and ε₀ replaces ε_∞]; exciton contribution to S(q, ω) [derived].
- Boundary with App. E: physics of the kernels here; transition-basis sizes, k-point convergence and cost in E.6.
- Sources: Public: Onida, Reining and Rubio (2002); Rohlfing and Louie (2000); Botti et al. (2007); Yu and Cardona Ch. 6 (Wannier–Mott).
  Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §1, §3; CONVENTIONS.md §11.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 4.11 ε₂ of silicon in IP, RPA + local fields, BSE and experiment (source to fix). Worked example
  4.15: the Wannier–Mott exciton of GaAs from μ_x and ε_∞.
- Threads: advances T2, T7.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### Chapter 5. Lattice dynamics   [PLANNED; 7 sections, 31,500 words]

### 5.1 The Born–Oppenheimer approximation   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Separate electronic and nuclear motion, define the energy surface on which nuclei move, and identify the small parameter.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the adiabatic factorization from the full Coulomb Hamiltonian of electrons and nuclei;
  (ii) derive by scaling that zero-point amplitudes over bond lengths are of order κ_BO = (m_e/M)^{1/4} and phonon over electronic energies
  of order κ_BO²; (iii) identify the terms neglected (named non-adiabatic couplings, treated in 6.1); (iv) state the clamped-nuclei
  approximation used in Chaps. 3–4; (v) derive the Hellmann–Feynman force F_κ.
- Prerequisites: Coulomb Hamiltonian of electrons and nuclei → 2.3; Kohn–Sham total energy → 3.4; Fock space → 2.3; band gap E_g → 3.3.
- Introduces: Born–Oppenheimer (adiabatic) approximation; potential-energy surface E^ad(R); clamped nuclei; equilibrium positions;
  small parameter κ_BO; non-adiabatic coupling (named); Hellmann–Feynman force F_κ.
- Key results: adiabatic expansion and its order counting [derived in D.13; cited: Born and Oppenheimer, Ann. Phys. 84, 457 (1927);
  Born and Huang, Dynamical Theory of Crystal Lattices App. VIII]; Hellmann–Feynman theorem [derived].
- Sources: Public: Born and Huang; Ziman, Electrons and Phonons Ch. 5; Giustino 1603.06965 §III. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §1 (1.1),
  §6 (orientation only); returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §1; returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §1.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 5.1 energy and length scales of electrons and nuclei with their ratios. Worked example 5.1: κ_BO,
  κ_BO² and κ_BO³ for silicon, compared with ω_ph/E_g and ⟨u²⟩^{1/2}/a.
- Threads: opens T4.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 5.2 The harmonic crystal   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the normal modes of a crystal from the second-order expansion of the energy surface.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define the force constants Φ^{(2)} and the dynamical matrix D^dyn(Q); (ii) derive the phonon
  eigenproblem, branches and polarization vectors with their normalization; (iii) derive the acoustic sum rule from translation
  invariance and the linear acoustic dispersion with sound speeds c_ν; (iv) interpret the measured phonon dispersion and density of states
  of silicon, and fit to its acoustic part a Debye model with Debye temperature Θ_D.
- Prerequisites: energy surface, equilibrium positions → 5.1; lattice, basis, BZ → 3.1; harmonic oscillator, linear algebra →
  READER.md "assumed".
- Introduces: classical small oscillations and normal modes (recap); displacement u_Rκ; species index κ; masses M_κ, M_cell; force
  constants Φ^{(2)}; dynamical matrix D^dyn(Q); phonon wavevector Q; branch ν; frequency ω_Qν; polarization e_κν(Q); acoustic and optical,
  longitudinal and transverse branches; acoustic sum rule; sound speed c_ν; phonon density of states g_ph(ω); Debye model, Debye
  temperature Θ_D and frequency ω_D.
- Key results: harmonic equations of motion and dispersion [derived; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 22]; acoustic sum rule [derived]; long-wave
  acoustic limit [derived]; Debye density of states [derived; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 23].
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 22–23; Born and Huang Ch. V; Nilsson and Nelin, Phys. Rev. B 6, 3777 (1972) (silicon dispersion).
  Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §5.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 5.2 silicon phonon dispersion and density of states, with the Debye fit. Worked example 5.2: the
  one-dimensional diatomic chain; worked example 5.3: silicon sound speeds from elastic constants, and the Debye temperature they imply.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 5.3 Phonons   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Quantize the normal modes and derive the displacement operator in the form used throughout the book.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive u_Rκ = Σ_{Qν}(2NM_κω_Qν)^{-1/2}e_κν(Q)e^{iQ·R}(b_Qν + b†_{−Qν}); (ii) compute zero-point
  and thermal mean-square displacements and the tensor B_κ; (iii) write the phonon emission and absorption matrix elements of u_Rκ, with
  the n + 1 and n factors taken from Sect. 2.5; (iv) define the zero-point length ℓ_Qν.
- Prerequisites: normal modes, polarizations, Debye model → 5.2; boson operators → 2.3; Bose function, thermal averages, n and n + 1
  factors → 2.5; reference appendices → App. A.6, C.6.
- Introduces: phonon (precise); operators b_Qν, b†_Qν; displacement field B_Qν = b_Qν + b†_{−Qν}; occupation n_Qν; zero-point length ℓ_Qν;
  displacement tensor B_κ = ⟨u_κu_κ⟩; zero-point motion.
- Key results: phonon expansion of u_Rκ [derived; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 23]; ⟨u²⟩ at T and at T = 0 [derived].
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 23; Ziman Ch. 1. Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §5 (normalization, ℓ_Qν with N outside).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 5.1 representative phonon energies and ⟨u²⟩ for Si, Ge, GaAs (sources to fix). Worked example 5.4:
  ⟨u²⟩ of silicon at 0 K and 300 K in the Debye model of 5.2.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 5.4 Phonons from first principles   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Explain how force constants and phonons are computed from density-functional theory.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive force constants from finite differences of Hellmann–Feynman forces in a supercell (the
  frozen-phonon method); (ii) derive the first-order Kohn–Sham (Sternheimer) equation of density-functional perturbation theory;
  (iii) state the 2n + 1 theorem and what it buys; (iv) explain the Fourier interpolation of force constants from a coarse Q grid and the
  enforcement of the acoustic sum rule.
- Prerequisites: Kohn–Sham equations → 3.4; static crystal bubble R⁰_GG′ → 4.6; dynamical matrix, acoustic sum rule → 5.2; plane waves →
  3.5; Hellmann–Feynman force → 5.1.
- Introduces: frozen phonon; supercell; density-functional perturbation theory (DFPT); Sternheimer equation; first-order density
  response; 2n + 1 theorem; force-constant interpolation.
- Key results: Sternheimer equation [derived; cited: Baroni et al., Rev. Mod. Phys. 73, 515 (2001), eq. to fix]; 2n + 1 theorem [cited:
  Gonze, Phys. Rev. A 52, 1096 (1995)].
- Boundary with App. E: the linear-response idea and its equations here; Q and k grids, convergence, self-consistency, cost and pitfalls in
  E.4.
- Sources: Public: Baroni et al. (2001); Giustino 1603.06965 §IV. Writer only: none needed.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 5.3 computed and measured silicon dispersion (sources to fix). Worked example 5.5: the Γ optical
  frequency of silicon from a frozen-phonon energy difference (numbers cited).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 5.5 Polar crystals   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the long-range electric fields of optical phonons in polar crystals.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define the Born effective charge Z*_κ as the force on atom κ per unit uniform field, ∂F_κ/∂ℰ
  (equivalently the polarization per unit displacement, with the polarization of a periodic solid defined by the Berry-phase theory), and
  derive its charge neutrality; (ii) derive the nonanalytic term of the dynamical matrix and the LO–TO splitting; (iii) derive the
  Lyddane–Sachs–Teller relation ε₀/ε_∞ = ω_L²/ω_T²; (iv) derive the coupling Z*_κ·ℰ of a polar mode to a uniform electric field, as a
  dark photon will use it in Chap. 11.
- Prerequisites: ε_∞ (microscopic) → 4.4; DFPT → 5.4; dynamical matrix → 5.2; phonons → 5.3; Hellmann–Feynman force F_κ → 5.1;
  classical electromagnetism → READER.md "assumed"; reference appendices → App. A.2.
- Introduces: macroscopic electric field ℰ and polarization 𝐏; the modern (Berry-phase) theory of polarization (named, with its
  computation in App. E.4); Born effective charge Z*_κ; static dielectric constant ε₀ (lattice included); nonanalytic term; LO and TO
  frequencies ω_L, ω_T; LO–TO splitting; Lyddane–Sachs–Teller (LST) relation; phonon polariton (qualitative).
- Key results: nonanalytic term and LST relation [derived in D.14; cited: Lyddane, Sachs and Teller, Phys. Rev. 59, 673 (1941); Gonze and Lee,
  Phys. Rev. B 55, 10355 (1997), eq. to fix]; Berry-phase polarization [cited: King-Smith and Vanderbilt, Phys. Rev. B 47, 1651 (1993), to
  confirm at briefing].
- Computational connection: Z*_κ and ε_∞ are DFPT outputs (App. E.4).
- Sources: Public: Born and Huang Ch. II; Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 27; Gonze and Lee (1997); King-Smith and Vanderbilt (1993); Knapen, Lin,
  Pyle and Zurek 1712.06598 (use for dark matter, named). Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 5.4 GaAs phonon dispersion with the LO–TO splitting. Worked example 5.6: the LST relation for GaAs
  with measured ε₀, ε_∞, ω_T (sources to fix).
- Threads: advances T2.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 5.6 Displacement correlations   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the thermal averages of exponentials of displacements that control every lattice response at finite momentum.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive ⟨e^{iq·u}⟩ = e^{−W} for a harmonic crystal (Bloch identity) with 2W_κ(q) = q·B_κ·q;
  (ii) derive the time-dependent displacement correlation 𝒞_κκ′(t) and the generating formula
  ⟨e^{−iq·u(t)}e^{iq·u(0)}⟩ = e^{−2W}e^{q·𝒞(t)·q}; (iii) derive Franck–Condon factors for a displaced oscillator and the Poisson phonon
  distribution with Huang–Rhys factor S_HR.
- Prerequisites: phonon operators, B_κ → 5.3; thermal averages → 2.5; Gaussian integrals and harmonic-oscillator identities → App. C.6;
  reference appendices → App. C.6.
- Introduces: Debye–Waller factor e^{−2W} (only this meaning; a one-line note says that the second-order electron–phonon term of the
  same name is a different object, treated in 6.3); W_κ(q); displacement correlation 𝒞_κκ′(t); Franck–Condon factor; Huang–Rhys factor S_HR;
  Poisson phonon distribution; zero-phonon line and phonon sideband (generic, displaced-oscillator sense).
- Key results: Bloch identity [derived in D.15; cited: Mermin, J. Math. Phys. 7, 1038 (1966)]; Poisson Franck–Condon distribution
  [derived; cited: Huang and Rhys, Proc. R. Soc. A 204, 406 (1950)].
- Computational connection: one paragraph: W_κ(q) and 𝒞_κκ′(t) are computed from DFPT phonons on a Q grid (App. E.4).
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin App. N; Lovesey, Theory of Neutron Scattering from Condensed Matter Vol. 1 Ch. 3–4; Huang and Rhys
  (1950). Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §2–§3; CONVENTIONS.md §10 (terminology).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 5.5 Franck–Condon progressions for S_HR = 0.5, 2, 5. Worked example 5.7: 2W for silicon at q = 10
  and 50 keV; worked example 5.8: the displaced oscillator.
- Threads: none (prepares Chap. 11).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 5.7 Anharmonicity   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the cubic and quartic terms of the energy surface and estimate their size.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define third- and fourth-order force constants; (ii) derive the three-phonon decay rate and
  linewidth γ^anh_Qν of a phonon with the golden rule; (iii) estimate the anharmonic small parameter in powers of κ_BO; (iv) state the
  self-consistency condition of the self-consistent harmonic approximation, and what the quasi-harmonic approximation and the
  temperature-dependent effective potential each fit.
- Prerequisites: energy surface, κ_BO → 5.1; phonons → 5.3; golden rule → 2.1; detailed balance → 2.5.
- Introduces: Φ^{(3)}, Φ^{(4)}; three-phonon processes; phonon linewidth γ_Qν and its anharmonic part γ^anh_Qν; anharmonic small
  parameter η_anh; quasi-harmonic approximation; self-consistent harmonic approximation (SCHA) and temperature-dependent effective
  potential (TDEP).
- Key results: three-phonon linewidth [derived; cited: Maradudin and Fein, Phys. Rev. 128, 2589 (1962)]; η_anh ~ κ_BO [derived estimate];
  SCHA self-consistency condition [stated; cited: Errea, Calandra and Mauri, Phys. Rev. B 89, 064302 (2014), to confirm at briefing].
- Computational connection: SCHA, TDEP and related methods in App. E.8.
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 25; Maradudin and Fein (1962); Klemens, Phys. Rev. 148, 845 (1966); Menéndez and Cardona,
  Phys. Rev. B 29, 2051 (1984). Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §6.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 5.6 three-phonon decay channels. Worked example 5.9: Klemens decay of the silicon Γ optical phonon
  against the measured linewidth.
- Threads: advances T4.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### Chapter 6. Electron–phonon coupling   [PLANNED; 5 sections, 26,500 words]

### 6.1 Nonadiabatic coupling   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the couplings neglected by the Born–Oppenheimer approximation and the electron–phonon Hamiltonian, and set up the adiabatic
  and sudden limits.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the first- and second-derivative non-adiabatic coupling operators; (ii) obtain the
  electron–phonon Hamiltonian as the expansion of the electronic potential in displacements; (iii) estimate non-adiabatic corrections in
  powers of κ_BO, the power counting behind Migdal's adiabatic theorem (precise in 6.3); (iv) compute the probability |F_1s(m_e v_N)|² that
  a suddenly kicked hydrogen atom stays in its ground state, and say when the sudden limit applies.
- Prerequisites: Born–Oppenheimer approximation, κ_BO → 5.1; phonon operators → 5.3; hydrogen 1s form factor F_1s → 2.2; second-order
  amplitudes → 2.4; reference appendices → App. C.6.
- Introduces: non-adiabatic coupling operators; electron–phonon Hamiltonian H_ep (general form); adiabatic and sudden limits; nuclear
  velocity after a kick v_N; recoil-induced ionization (the Migdal effect; named, treated in Chap. 12); a paragraph that separates the two
  results carrying Migdal's name: the 1941 sudden-kick ionization treated here and in Chap. 12, and the 1958 adiabatic theorem on vertex
  corrections (precise in 6.3).
- Key results: non-adiabatic expansion [derived in D.13]; sudden kick of a hydrogen atom: ground-state survival |F_1s(m_e v_N)|²
  [derived; cited: Migdal, J. Phys. USSR 4, 449 (1941)].
- Sources: Public: Born and Huang App. VIII; Giustino 1603.06965 §III; Ibe et al. 1707.07258 §2 (sudden picture, named). Writer only:
  returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §1–§2 (orientation only); returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §6.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 6.1 adiabatic and sudden responses of an electron to a moving nucleus. Worked example 6.1: ground-state
  survival of a hydrogen atom whose nucleus is kicked to velocity v_N.
- Threads: advances T4.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 6.2 Electron–phonon matrix elements   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Define the electron–phonon vertex g, derive its main physical forms, and say how it is obtained from first principles.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write H_ep = N^{-1/2}Σ g_mnν(k, Q)c†_{m,k+Q}c_nk B_Qν with g defined from the screened potential
  derivative; (ii) derive deformation-potential coupling for acoustic phonons and the optical deformation potential; (iii) derive the
  Fröhlich coupling of LO phonons from the macroscopic polarization; (iv) explain why the vertex is screened by the electrons and why the
  acoustic vertex vanishes at Q → 0 (acoustic sum rule); (v) write g from DFPT as ⟨ψ_{m,k+Q}|∂_{κ,Q}V_KS|ψ_nk⟩ with mass and frequency
  factors, explain why its Wannier interpolation works (the vertex is short-ranged in the Wannier basis of 3.6), and why the polar
  long-range part g^L is subtracted before interpolation and restored analytically.
- Prerequisites: H_ep general form → 6.1; screening, ε_∞ → 4.4; Born charges, ε₀, LO–TO → 5.5; Bloch states → 3.2; acoustic sum rule → 5.2;
  phonons, ℓ_Qν → 5.3; DFPT, Sternheimer equation → 5.4; Wannier functions, one gauge → 3.6; reference appendices → App. A.2, A.5, A.6.
- Introduces: electron–phonon vertex g_mnν(k, Q); deformation-potential tensor D^n; optical deformation potential d^n; Fröhlich coupling
  C_F and constant α_F; piezoelectric coupling (its form stated); screened vertex; short- and long-range parts g^S, g^L; potential
  perturbation ∂_{κ,Q}V_KS; Wannier interpolation of g; long-range subtraction; coarse and fine grids.
- Key results: deformation-potential coupling [derived; cited: Bardeen and Shockley, Phys. Rev. 80, 72 (1950)]; Fröhlich vertex [derived in
  D.14; cited: Fröhlich, Adv. Phys. 3, 325 (1954); Verdi and Giustino 1510.06373, eq. to fix]; g_nn(k, 0) = 0 for acoustic modes [derived];
  DFPT expression of g [cited: Giustino 1603.06965, eq. to fix]; Wannier interpolation of g [cited: Giustino, Cohen and Louie, Phys. Rev. B
  76, 165108 (2007); Poncé et al. 1604.03525].
- Boundary with App. E (the first-principles passage that replaces the former method section): the objects, and why interpolation works,
  here; workflows, grid sizes, matrix-element and memory counts, convergence tests, cost and pitfalls in E.4 and E.5.
- Sources: Public: Giustino 1603.06965 §§III–V; Yu and Cardona Ch. 3; Verdi and Giustino 1510.06373; Poncé et al. 1604.03525; Giustino,
  Cohen and Louie (2007). Writer only: CONVENTIONS.md §4–§5 (shared gauge, normalization of g); returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §4 (4.8)(i).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 6.2 |g| against |Q| for an acoustic branch of Si and the LO branch of GaAs; Fig. 6.3 the chain from
  DFT and DFPT to interpolated g. Worked example 6.2: the Fröhlich constant of GaAs.
- Threads: advances T2.
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### 6.3 Electron self-energy from phonons   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive how phonons shift and broaden electronic levels, including at zero temperature, and state when vertex corrections can
  be dropped.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the second-order Fan–Migdal self-energy and the Allen–Heine term from the expansion of the
  electronic potential to second order in displacements, with the diagram rules of 4.8; (ii) explain the rigid-ion approximation, the
  translational-invariance relation between the two terms, and why the Allen–Heine term is not the Debye–Waller factor e^{−2W} of 5.6;
  (iii) compute a zero-point renormalization of a gap in an Einstein model and compare with silicon; (iv) obtain phonon-limited lifetimes
  from Im Σ; (v) state Migdal's adiabatic theorem (vertex corrections suppressed by ω_ph/E_F in metals), justify it from the power counting
  of 6.1 and these diagrams, and state its form for insulators, where ω_ph/E_g is the small ratio.
- Prerequisites: g → 6.2; Green's functions, self-energy, Matsubara frequencies → 4.7; diagrams, cutting rule, second-order self-energy
  with a boson → 4.8; κ_BO power counting → 6.1; second-order amplitudes → 2.4; phonon occupations → 5.3; Debye–Waller factor → 5.6;
  E_F → 2.5; E_g → 3.3; reference appendices → App. C.4.
- Introduces: Fan–Migdal self-energy Σ^FM; Allen–Heine self-energy Σ^AH and vertex g^AH; rigid-ion approximation; zero-point
  renormalization (ZPR); Allen–Heine–Cardona theory (AHC); renormalized band energy ε^ren_nk; phonon-limited lifetime; Migdal's adiabatic
  theorem (statement, justification and scope); special displacements (named; the method in App. E.8).
- Key results: AHC self-energy and rigid-ion relation [derived in D.16; cited: Allen and Heine, J. Phys. C 9, 2305 (1976); Allen and Cardona,
  Phys. Rev. B 23, 1495 (1981)]; Migdal's adiabatic theorem [derived at the level of power counting; cited: Migdal, Sov. Phys. JETP 7, 996
  (1958)]; silicon gap ZPR [cited: Poncé et al. 1504.05992, table no. to fix]; special displacements [cited: Zacharias and Giustino
  1604.02394].
- Computational connection: AHC and special-displacement calculations in App. E.8.
- Sources: Public: Giustino 1603.06965 §VI; Poncé et al. 1504.05992; Zacharias and Giustino 1604.02394. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md
  Part II §5; CONVENTIONS.md §10 (Debye–Waller versus Allen–Heine).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 6.4 the temperature dependence of the silicon gap, measured and computed (sources to fix). Worked
  example 6.3: the Einstein-model ZPR and its temperature dependence, built on worked example 4.14.
- Threads: advances T4, T7.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 6.4 Phonon self-energy and the Eliashberg function   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive how electrons shift and broaden phonons, and define the Eliashberg function and the coupling constant that
  superconductivity and phonon-mediated rates use.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the lowest-order phonon self-energy Π_ν(Q, ω), the electron bubble with two vertices g,
  and the phonon Dyson equation; (ii) derive the electron–phonon linewidth γ^ep_Qν from Im Π (Allen's formula) and show that it vanishes
  for ω_Qν < E_g in an undoped insulator at T = 0; (iii) define the Eliashberg function α²F(ω) and the coupling λ_ep = 2∫dω α²F(ω)/ω, and
  relate λ_ep to the linewidths; (iv) state the phonon-side Migdal theorem (vertex corrections to Π suppressed by ω_ph/E_F), and explain
  why DFPT phonons already contain the static (adiabatic) part of Π, so that only the nonadiabatic remainder may be added.
- Prerequisites: g, screened vertex → 6.2; Migdal's adiabatic theorem, electron self-energy → 6.3; Matsubara 𝖦, Dyson equation → 4.7;
  bubble, cutting rule → 4.8; phonons, ω_Qν, n_Qν → 5.3; DFPT → 5.4; anharmonic linewidth γ^anh_Qν → 5.7; 𝒩_F, E_F → 2.5; E_g → 3.3;
  reference appendices → App. C.4.
- Introduces: phonon self-energy Π_ν(Q, ω); phonon Dyson equation; electron–phonon linewidth γ^ep_Qν; Eliashberg function α²F(ω);
  coupling constant λ_ep; adiabatic and nonadiabatic phonon renormalization; Kohn anomaly (named); phonon-side Migdal theorem.
- Key results: phonon self-energy and Allen's linewidth formula [derived in D.17; cited: Allen, Phys. Rev. B 6, 2577 (1972), to confirm at
  briefing]; α²F and λ_ep [derived in D.17; cited: Eliashberg, Sov. Phys. JETP 11, 696 (1960), to confirm at briefing; Giustino
  1603.06965, section to fix]; phonon-side Migdal theorem [cited: Migdal (1958)]; adiabatic versus nonadiabatic phonons [cited: Calandra,
  Profeta and Mauri, Phys. Rev. B 82, 165111 (2010), to confirm at briefing].
- Computational connection: one paragraph: Π, γ^ep_Qν and α²F are computed from Wannier-interpolated g on fine grids (App. E.5); Eliashberg
  gaps from α²F (App. E.9).
- Sources: Public: Giustino 1603.06965; Allen (1972); Grimvall, The Electron–Phonon Interaction in Metals (1981), to confirm at briefing;
  Calandra, Profeta and Mauri (2010). Writer only: none needed.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 6.5 the phonon self-energy bubble beside the electron self-energy of 6.3; Fig. 6.6 α²F(ω) of
  aluminium with its phonon density of states (source to fix). Worked example 6.4: λ_ep of aluminium from a two-peak (TA, LA) Einstein
  model of α²F, against the cited value (source to fix).
- Threads: advances T4.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 6.5 Phonon-assisted optical absorption   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the classic theory of indirect-gap absorption, the first composite process of the book.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the second-order amplitude for photon absorption with phonon emission or absorption, with both
  time orderings, the velocity matrix elements of 3.2 and their energy denominators; (ii) derive the absorption edge
  α_abs(ω) ∝ (ω ∓ ω_Qν − E_g)² of Hall, Bardeen and Blatt; (iii) write the first-principles ε₂^PA and identify its sums over intermediate
  states, phonon modes and k (their convergence is treated in App. E.5 and E.8); (iv) explain the temperature dependence through n and
  n + 1; (v) explain why the same structure reappears when the photon is replaced by dark matter.
- Prerequisites: second-order amplitudes, PV and widths → 2.4; detailed balance → 2.5; v_mn → 3.2; indirect gap, k_c → 3.3; ε₂, σ₁, f-sum →
  4.4; phonons → 5.3; g, Wannier interpolation of g → 6.2; renormalized bands → 6.3; reference appendices → App. A.6, C.4.
- Introduces: phonon-assisted (indirect) absorption; ε₂^PA; absorption coefficient α_abs(ω); emission and absorption thresholds
  E_g ± ω_Qν; the velocity form of the optical vertex.
- Key results: Hall–Bardeen–Blatt edge [derived in D.18; cited: Hall, Bardeen and Blatt, Phys. Rev. 95, 559 (1954)]; first-principles ε₂^PA
  [cited: Noffsinger et al. 1202.3454, eq. to fix]; measured silicon edge [cited: Macfarlane et al., Phys. Rev. 111, 1245 (1958)].
- Computational connection: ε₂^PA from Wannier-interpolated g (App. E.5) or from special displacements (App. E.8).
- Sources: Public: Yu and Cardona Ch. 6; Noffsinger et al. 1202.3454; Zacharias and Giustino 1604.02394; Bassani and Pastori Parravicini,
  Electronic States and Optical Transitions in Solids Ch. 5. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §4 (phonon-assisted absorption),
  M1–M3 (orientation only; none of its labels enter the book).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 6.7 the two time orderings; Fig. 6.8 √α_abs against ω for silicon with the phonon thresholds.
  Worked example 6.5: silicon thresholds E_g ± ω_TA, E_g ± ω_TO and the slopes of √α_abs.
- Threads: advances T5.
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### Chapter 7. Further target materials   [PLANNED; 4 sections, 20,000 words]

### 7.1 Magnetic order and spin waves   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive spin waves in ordered magnets, the excitations of the magnetic targets of Chap. 14.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the Heisenberg model from exchange and derive the superexchange estimate J^ex ~ t_hop²/U_H
  for a correlated insulator such as NiO (Anderson's mechanism, qualitative); (ii) apply the Holstein–Primakoff transformation and derive
  linear spin-wave dispersions of a ferromagnet; (iii) diagonalize the antiferromagnet with a Bogoliubov transformation and derive its linear
  dispersion; (iv) write the spin-density response and the magnetic form factor; (v) estimate the NiO magnon velocity and zone-boundary
  energy from the cited exchange constants.
- Prerequisites: Hartree–Fock exchange → 3.4; tight binding, hopping t_hop → 3.2; boson operators → 2.3; Kubo, S_ab → 4.1, 4.2;
  lattices → 3.1; spin → READER.md "assumed"; Bogoliubov transformations (matrix form) → App. C.7; reference appendices → App. C.7, C.19.
- Introduces: exchange constants J^ex_ij; Heisenberg model; superexchange and the on-site repulsion U_H (qualitative); ionic spin 𝐒_j;
  ferromagnet, antiferromagnet; Holstein–Primakoff transformation; magnon operators a_kν; linear spin-wave theory (LSWT); Bogoliubov
  transformation; magnon Bogoliubov coefficients u^M_k, v^M_k; magnetic form factor F^mag(q); spin density s_e(q) and its response.
- Key results: superexchange estimate [cited: Anderson, Phys. Rev. 115, 2 (1959), to confirm at briefing]; Holstein–Primakoff [cited:
  Holstein and Primakoff, Phys. Rev. 58, 1098 (1940)]; LSWT of the antiferromagnet [derived in D.19]; NiO exchange [cited: Hutchings and
  Samuelsen, Phys. Rev. B 6, 3447 (1972)].
- Computational connection: exchange constants from DFT + U or spin-spiral calculations (App. E.9).
- Sources: Public: Ashcroft and Mermin Ch. 32–33; White, Quantum Theory of Magnetism Ch. 6; Anderson (1959); Trickle, Zhang and Zurek
  1905.13744 (use for dark matter, named). Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §1–§2 (reference states, magnetic targets; orientation only).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 7.1 NiO magnetic structure and magnon dispersion. Worked example 7.1: the ferromagnetic chain;
  worked example 7.2: NiO magnon velocity from J^ex.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 7.2 Superconductivity   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the BCS ground state from the phonon-mediated attraction, its quasiparticles and a charge-conserving density response.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the retarded phonon-mediated attraction from the vertex g of 6.2 and the coupling λ_ep of 6.4,
  reduce it to the BCS model interaction with cutoff ω_D, and derive the BCS gap equation and the weak-coupling result Δ_BCS = 1.764 T_c;
  (ii) derive Bogoliubov quasiparticles E_k = (ξ_k² + Δ_BCS²)^{1/2} and the coherence factors for density and spin perturbations; (iii) show
  that the bare quasiparticle bubble violates the f-sum rule and derive the conserving response that restores it, with the Nambu density
  vertex and the Ward identity stated here (D.21); (iv) evaluate the pair-breaking threshold 2Δ_BCS in aluminium and its kinematic reach.
- Prerequisites: Fock space → 2.3; g → 6.2; λ_ep, α²F → 6.4; Debye frequency ω_D → 5.2; 𝒩_F → 2.5; Bogoliubov transformation → 7.1;
  f-sum → 4.3; R, S → 4.1, 4.2; Matsubara Green's functions → 4.7; diagrams → 4.8; m_min → 1.3; reference appendices → App. C.7.
- Introduces: phonon-mediated (retarded) attraction and the BCS model interaction; Cooper pair; BCS state; gap Δ_BCS; normal-state energy
  ξ_k; Bogoliubov energy E_k; coherence factors u^B_k, v^B_k (case I, case II); Bogoliubov quasiparticles and their density of states;
  pair-breaking threshold 2Δ_BCS; Nambu formalism; the density Ward identity (stated locally; the general translation Ward identities are
  treated in Chap. 8); conserving (gauge-invariant) response; Mattis–Bardeen conductivity (named).
- Key results: phonon-mediated attraction [derived from one-phonon exchange; cited: Bardeen and Pines, Phys. Rev. 99, 1140 (1955), to
  confirm at briefing]; BCS gap equation and coherence factors [derived in D.20; cited: Bardeen, Cooper and Schrieffer, Phys. Rev. 108,
  1175 (1957)]; conserving response and the f-sum check [derived in D.21; cited: Anderson, Phys. Rev. 112, 1900 (1958); Nambu, Phys. Rev.
  117, 648 (1960)]; aluminium gap ≈ 0.18 meV from T_c = 1.18 K [derived from the weak-coupling ratio].
- Computational connection: gaps from Eliashberg theory with the α²F of 6.4 (App. E.9), named only.
- Sources: Public: Tinkham, Introduction to Superconductivity Ch. 3; Schrieffer, Theory of Superconductivity; Hochberg et al. 1512.04533 (use for
  dark matter, named). Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §4 (BCS response; orientation only); CONVENTIONS.md §13 row 33 (bulk versus film gap).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 7.2 the BCS quasiparticle density of states. Worked example 7.3: the aluminium gap, 2Δ_BCS and m_min
  ≈ 0.1 keV.
- Threads: advances T1, T3.
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### 7.3 Dirac and narrow-gap materials   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the low-energy theory of Dirac materials and their screening.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive a Dirac Hamiltonian from k·p near a band crossing and add a gap; (ii) derive its density of

  cutoff Λ_D; degeneracy g_deg; Weyl nodes (named).
- Key results: Dirac polarization function [derived in D.22; cited: Hochberg et al. 1708.08929, eq. to fix]; ZrTe₅ parameters [cited: Geilhufe,
  Kahlhoefer and Winkler 1910.02091 Eq. (1.1)].

### 7.4 Defects and dopants   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Introduce point defects and dopants as targets and as final states.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) apply the hydrogenic envelope solution of 4.9 to shallow donors, adding only the anisotropic valley
  masses and the central-cell correction, and compare with silicon donors; (ii) describe a color center with a configuration-coordinate
  diagram and compute its zero-phonon fraction e^{−S_HR}, for the silicon T center and, as the contrast, the nitrogen–vacancy center of
  diamond; (iii) define displacement threshold energies and Frenkel pairs from the energy surface; (iv) say which of these can serve as final
  states or readouts.
- Prerequisites: effective masses, valleys → 3.3; hydrogenic envelope, Wannier–Mott exciton → 4.9; ε₀ → 5.5; Franck–Condon, S_HR → 5.6;
  energy surface → 5.1; hydrogen atom → READER.md "assumed".
- Introduces: donor, acceptor; effective-mass theory; envelope function (with anisotropic masses); effective Bohr radius a*; donor binding
  E_b^D; central-cell correction; defect density 𝓃_D; color center; zero-phonon line energy E_ZPL; displacement threshold energy E_d;
  vacancy, interstitial, Frenkel pair; stored energy (qualitative).
- Key results: effective-mass donor levels [derived in D.23; cited: Kohn and Luttinger, Phys. Rev. 98, 915 (1955)]; silicon T-center ZPL and
  Huang–Rhys factor [cited: Bergeron et al., PRX Quantum 1, 020301 (2020), to confirm at briefing]; nitrogen–vacancy center parameters [cited:
  Doherty et al. 1302.3288]; silicon E_d [cited: Holmström, Kuronen and Nordlund, Phys. Rev. B 78, 045202 (2008)].
- Computational connection: supercell defect calculations and threshold-displacement molecular dynamics (App. E.10).
- Sources: Public: Yu and Cardona Ch. 4; Kohn and Luttinger (1955); Bergeron et al. (2020); Doherty et al. 1302.3288; Holmström et al. (2008).
  Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §6 (dopant final states; orientation only); surveys/dm-solid-state-processes/catalog-processes.md
  (defect channels).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 7.4 hydrogenic donor levels in silicon; Fig. 7.5 configuration-coordinate diagram of the silicon
  T center, with the nitrogen–vacancy center as the contrast. Worked example 7.5: the effective-mass binding of Si:P against the measured
  45 meV; worked example 7.6: the zero-phonon fractions of the T center and the nitrogen–vacancy center.
- Threads: advances T6.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### Chapter 8. The response framework   [PLANNED; 8 sections, 37,500 words]
Opening: Chapters 2–7 each computed one kind of rate in one kind of material, and a reader who meets a new dark-matter model or a new
target should not have to start again. The question of this chapter is what all those calculations have in common. The answer is short:
one rate formula built from the response functions of Chap. 4, a handful of constraints that no approximation may violate, and one
expansion with named small parameters of which every model of Part II is a truncation, so that its error can be estimated rather than
guessed. The chapter closes Part I by turning its separate results into the framework that Part II applies channel by channel, and it
ends where Part III begins, with the final state handed to a detector.
**Status tags (writer-side; defined here because Chap. 8 is their first user, and they apply to every later entry).** REF = refereed;
VER = claimed and verified once: both are presented as established. EST = an estimate (an [E] number: order of magnitude or model value,
whatever its verification), presented as an estimate with its stated factor and, where a conclusion depends on it, stated as open. CL =
claimed only; GAP = exact remaining gap: both are presented as open, in the open-problems passage of their home section, never as
established. "new" marks a result to our knowledge new: the text says "derived here" and never implies literature consensus; a new result
that is only claimed is CL. "Open problems (exact gaps)" fields carry one label per item (LE-n from state/LOOSE-ENDS.md, or XG-n for an
open item that LOOSE-ENDS does not list); each label has exactly one home, and Table 22.2 indexes them. Limitboxes may be used from 8.1
on: the front matter explains how a box is read, and 9.4 builds the plots.

### 8.1 Dark-matter couplings to a target   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Reduce every non-relativistic dark-matter interaction to a short list of target operators per species, so that any model
  enters the book through the same response functions.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the non-relativistic effective operators 𝒪_i built from q, the transverse velocity v^⊥
  and the spins, and state which target operator each one needs; (ii) build the target operators per species: electron density n_e(q),
  nuclear density n_κ(q), electron spin density s_e(q), paramagnetic current j_e(q), kinetic-energy density 𝒯_e(q) and nuclear spin
  density s_κ(q), with the proton and neutron spin content of a nucleus; (iii) reduce 𝒪₂ = (v^⊥)² to density, current and
  velocity-squared rows; (iv) write the absorption operators of vector, axion-like and scalar dark matter, the fermionic absorption
  operators (a dark fermion absorbed with a light fermion emitted), and the inelastic and exothermic kinematics through Δ_χ.
- Prerequisites (concept → section): Ṽ(q), F_DM, σ̄_e, axion-like and scalar couplings, absorption of bosonic dark matter → 1.4; Δ_χ,
  ω_q → 1.3; n_e(q), one-body operators, Coulomb Hamiltonian → 2.3; j_e → 4.3; s_e(q), magnetic form factor → 7.1; species index, M_κ →
  5.2; spin → READER.md "assumed"; reference appendices → App. A.9, C.15.
- Introduces: operator basis 𝒪_i (non-relativistic effective theory; the general basis of 1.4 made precise); transverse velocity v^⊥;
  target operators per species, nuclear density operator n_κ(q), nuclear spin density s_κ(q), kinetic-energy density 𝒯_e(q);
  dark-matter-side tensor 𝔇_ab (contracts the target response; one per model); spin-dependent and nuclear-spin couplings (precise);
  absorption operators of vector, pseudoscalar and scalar bosons; fermionic absorption operators (op.); two-body Coulomb-energy row
  (named); nuclear spin expectation values ⟨S_p⟩, ⟨S_n⟩ (recap).
- Key results (derived / cited): the operator list and its velocity rows, 𝒪₂ → n, j, v² (Table 8.1; a key result, not a limitbox:
  a list has no controlling parameter to plot) [derived in D.24; cited: Fitzpatrick et al. 1203.3542 Eq. (19); Trickle, Zhang, Zurek
  and Lin 2009.13534 Table III; REF for the list, VER for the 𝒪₂ row]; reduction of every 𝒪_i to target operators per species [derived
  in D.24; REF]; scalar absorption through the kinetic-energy density at next-to-leading order [cited: Mitridate et al. 2106.12586
  Eq. (18); VER].
- Sources: Public: 1203.3542; 2009.13534; Catena et al. 2105.02233; 2106.12586; Lin 1904.07915. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §1–§2,
  §15; returns/R16/CW3.md §2 (velocity-squared row); CONVENTIONS.md §7 (with its R16 and R17 amendments).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 8.1 the operator list, the absorption and fermionic absorption operators, with the target
  operator each needs and the sector it excites. Worked example 8.1: a dark photon with a heavy mediator reduces to the density row;
  worked example 8.2: 𝒪₂ for electron scattering in silicon. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 8.2 The master rate formula   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive one rate formula, at finite temperature, for scattering, absorption and exothermic transitions off any target.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the scattering rate Γ = ∫d³q/(2π)³ Σ_ab 𝔇_ab(q, v) S_ab(q, ω_q) from the golden rule with the
  response matrix of 4.2; (ii) derive the absorption rate of a non-relativistic bosonic field from the same matrix at q = 𝐩_φ, ω = m_φ;
  (iii) show that ω < 0 terms describe energy given up by a warm target or by exothermic dark matter, with detailed balance; (iv) state the
  conditions (first Born approximation, plane waves, Gibbs target, unpolarized dark matter) and when the coherent classical-field regime
  replaces the golden rule (forward: 14.6).
- Prerequisites: golden rule → 2.1; Born approximation → 2.2; detailed balance, transition sum → 2.5; S_ab, FDT, Van Hove → 4.2; Kubo,
  R_ab → 4.1; operator basis, 𝔇_ab → 8.1; η(v_min), f_χ → 1.2, 1.3; reference appendices → App. A.4, A.8.
- Introduces: master rate formula (scattering, absorption, exothermic); multi-operator response contraction; master-formula conditions;
  Gaussian field average for absorption (named).
- Key results: the master formula, whose one-row case is the single-operator rate Γ = ∫d³q/(2π)³|Ṽ|²S(q, ω_q) already derived in 4.2
  (cited back, not re-derived and not boxed) [derived in D.25; cited: Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275 Eqs. (4), (6); 1910.08092;
  REF]; absorption rate Γ_abs ∝ Im R(𝐩_φ, m_φ) [derived in D.25; REF]; exothermic and thermal terms [derived; REF].
- Sources: Public: 2101.08275; 1910.08092; 2009.13534; Mitridate et al. 2106.12586. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §3 ((3.1)–(3.3)),
  §9.1; CONVENTIONS.md §6.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 8.1 the (q, ω) plane with scattering (ω_q curve), absorption (point) and exothermic (ω < 0)
  supports. Worked example 8.3: a two-operator model (density and spin couplings) contracted with the 2 × 2 response matrix of the
  free-electron gas, showing the cross term that a single-operator rate misses. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T6.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 8.3 Exact relations   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Collect the constraints that hold for every target without approximation, so that every later model can be tested against
  them.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) apply the positivity of the response matrix (proved in 4.2) to bound the interference between two
  couplings; (ii) build the moment matrices from commutators, starting from the species-resolved f-sum rules of 4.3 (cited, not
  re-derived); (iii) derive the third-moment mixed electron–nucleus sum rule and its q → 0 limit; (iv) derive the translation Ward
  identities linking the nuclear densities to the momentum density 𝐠(q), and the soft acoustic limit they imply.
- Prerequisites: S_ab, positivity, FDT → 4.2; Kramers–Kronig, f-sum, species-resolved f-sum, 𝓜¹ → 4.3; master rate formula → 8.2; target
  operators per species → 8.1; acoustic sum rule → 5.2; Born–Oppenheimer approximation → 5.1; reference appendices → App. A.7, C.7.
- Introduces: moment matrices 𝓜^k; mixed electron–nucleus third moment (named result); translation Ward identities (precise; named for
  translation invariance, and one line in the text separates them from the gauge Ward identities of field theory); momentum density
  𝐠(q); soft acoustic limit; Cauchy–Schwarz bound on interference.
- Key results: the interference bound from positivity [derived; REF]; species-resolved f-sum rules [from 4.3 and D.6; REF; not
  re-derived]; mixed third-moment sum rule 𝓜³_eκ ∝ q²Z_κ𝓃_κ[𝓃_e^{(κ)} + 2𝓃_e]/(m_eM_κ), with 𝓃_e^{(κ)} the electron density at
  nucleus κ [derived in D.26; REF; new: derived here; priority to be checked at briefing against the two-component-plasma literature on
  third frequency moments]; translation Ward identities Σ_s m_sS_{Bs} = (q/ω)·S_{B𝐠} [derived in D.26; REF; new in this form: derived
  here]; soft acoustic limit [derived; REF].
- Sources: Public: Giuliani and Vignale Ch. 3; Pines and Nozières Ch. 2. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §4 ((4.1)–(4.9)); returns/R3/X1.md
  (soft limit); state/NOVELTY.md N1, N2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 8.2 the exact relations, with the sections that use each as a check. Worked example 8.4: the
  mixed third moment of silicon at q = 10 keV, valence and core parts (the silicon f-sum split is worked example 4.7 and is not
  repeated); worked example 8.5: the Ward identity for a monatomic harmonic chain. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T3.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 8.4 Screening for every coupling   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Fix, for each external coupling (electron-only, total charge, nucleon number, spin), which part of the response is screened
  and when the loss function gives the rate exactly.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define the Coulomb-proper response for a channel of reciprocal vectors and derive the screened
  rate for any external coupling with the total dielectric matrix of electrons and nuclei; (ii) prove for a general coupling that the loss
  function gives the rate exactly only for a probe coupled to the electron density at static lattice (4.4 stated the electron-only case;
  10.2 applies the result by reference); (iii) classify Coulomb lines so that no screening is counted
  twice when lattice and electronic sectors combine; (iv) explain the in-medium mixing of a dark photon as the proper/improper distinction.
- Prerequisites: R̃, ε, L, S–L relation, electron-only versus total-charge probes → 4.4; RPA + local fields → 4.6; Z*, ε₀ → 5.5; master rate
  formula → 8.2; target operators per species → 8.1; in-medium mixing → 1.4; reference appendices → App. A.2, A.7.
- Introduces: external-field type (electron-only, total charge, nucleon number); total dielectric matrix of electrons and nuclei;
  Coulomb-line classes and the no-double-counting rule; in-medium mixing (precise).
- Key results: screened rate for every coupling [derived in D.27; REF]; exactness condition of the loss-function form [derived; REF];
  Coulomb-line classes [derived in D.27 at random-phase level; REF]; in-medium mixing as the improper response [derived; cited: Hochberg,
  Lin and Zurek 1608.01994; REF].
- Sources: Public: 2101.08275; Hochberg et al. 2101.08263; 1608.01994. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §5 ((5.1)–(5.7′)); returns/R3/X2.md §3–§4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 8.3 external-field types against the screening factor each takes. Worked example 8.6: the nucleon-
  number coupling of a heavy scalar in GaAs, screened by the Born charges. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T2.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 8.5 The expansion hierarchy   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Show that every model of Part II is a truncation of one expansion, with stated small parameters, so that each approximation has
  a defined next order.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) list the small parameters (κ_BO, the off-shell parameter θ_ep, η_anh, T/ω_Qν, the recoil parameter
  x_R, the suddenness ratio, ξ_D) with their silicon values, each with the Part I section that introduced the physics and the chapter
  where it becomes a number (κ_BO 5.1 → Chaps. 12–13; θ_ep 6.2 → Chap. 13; η_anh 5.7 → 11.5; T/ω_Qν 2.5 → 13.7; x_R 5.6 → 11.3–11.4;
  suddenness 6.1 → Chap. 12; ξ_D 2.2 → 10.6, 12.2); (ii) write the truncation label 𝔗 = (n_B, n_g, n_A, 𝒳, n_T, 𝒦, 𝔐, n_D) component
  by component, each illustrated by a Part I result at its two lowest values (𝒳: the independent-particle and RPA responses of 4.5–4.6;
  𝒦: one-phonon and multiphonon terms of 5.6; n_g = 0 and 2: direct and phonon-assisted absorption of 6.5; 𝔐: the sudden kick of 6.1),
  and the base point 𝔗₀ against which Appendix F lists only the components that differ; (iii) explain why the many-body level 𝒳 is not
  an expansion in a small number and how its error is estimated; (iv) read the lattice regimes one-phonon → multiphonon → impulse from
  x_R and 2W.
- Prerequisites: κ_BO, Born–Oppenheimer approximation → 5.1; η_anh → 5.7; hierarchy of approximations → 4.6, 4.9; W_κ, Debye–Waller →
  5.6; electron–phonon vertex → 6.2; second-order amplitudes → 2.4; sudden limit, Migdal effect → 6.1; ε₂^PA → 6.5; master rate formula
  → 8.2.
- Introduces: expansion hierarchy (precise); truncation label 𝔗 and its components; base point 𝔗₀ = (n_B = 0, n_g = 0, n_A = 0,
  𝒳 = IP, n_T = 0; 𝒦, 𝔐, n_D not applicable) and the deviation notation of Appendix F; off-shell parameter θ_ep; recoil parameter x_R;
  suddenness ratio; multipole parameter ξ_D; lattice regime label 𝒦; Migdal regime label 𝔐.
- Key results: small-parameter table with silicon values [derived estimates; REF]; the label, its base point and the deviation notation
  [definition; REF]. The placement of the literature formulas of Part II on 𝔗 is done once, in 9.4.
- Sources: Public: Giustino 1603.06965 §III. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §6 (Table 6.1, truncation multi-index); CONVENTIONS.md §11.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 8.4 small parameters with silicon values and the chapter where each becomes concrete. Worked
  example 8.7: the label of phonon-assisted optical absorption (6.5): n_g = 2, n_T = 1, 𝒳 = IP, read off its assumptions. Limitbox
  plots: none.
- Threads: advances T4.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 8.6 The decomposition theorem   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Split every response into electron-following, zero-phonon electronic and phonon-dressed electronic final-state sectors, and
  fix the bookkeeping that prevents double counting.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) build the sector projectors 𝒫_ad, 𝒫_0, 𝒫_+ from the adiabatic transformation and show that they
  partition the final states; (ii) derive the leading-order operators in each sector (electrons following the nuclei; the zero-phonon
  line; phonon-dressed electronic states) and their partial sum rules; (iii) state the Dyson convention that makes the split unique outside
  the off-shell window, and why the totals do not depend on it; (iv) explain why the theorem needs a gap and what replaces it for metals,
  superconductors and semimetals.
- Prerequisites: Born–Oppenheimer approximation, non-adiabatic coupling → 5.1, 6.1; zero-phonon line, Franck–Condon → 5.6; f-sum → 4.3;
  expansion hierarchy, θ_ep → 8.5; exact relations, moment matrices → 8.3; Dyson equation → 4.7; reference appendices → App. C.4, C.7.
- Introduces: decomposition theorem; electron-following sector (𝒫_ad); zero-phonon electronic sector (𝒫_0); phonon-dressed electronic
  sector (𝒫_+); partial sum rules; Dyson convention; gapless targets and the sector construction for them; Fano interference with a
  continuum (named; one line in the text separates it from the Fano factor of the pair number, which is unrelated: forward, 18.2).
- Key results: the decomposition and its partial sum rules [derived in D.28; REF]; uniqueness of the 𝒫_0/𝒫_+ split inside the window to
  O(θ_ep) [derived; REF]; the Dyson convention and convention independence of the total [derived; REF]; sectors for gapless targets
  [derived in D.28; REF]; Fano term vanishes for inversion- and time-reversal-symmetric targets with an even electron number [derived; VER].
- Sources: Public: Born and Huang App. VIII; Giustino 1603.06965. Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §6.2; returns/R5/O4.md; returns/R8/WP4-v3.md
  §8 (gapless targets); returns/R16/CW3.md §1 (rigour); CONVENTIONS.md §10.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 8.5 the sector table (densities of electrons and nuclei, and their cross terms, against the three
  sectors) with the literature formulas each cell recovers. Worked example 8.8: a two-level electron coupled to one oscillator, split
  exactly into the three sectors. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T4, T3.
- Open problems (exact gaps): convention-free sector labels without the gap hypothesis (LE-73); uniformity of the bound in the number of
  nuclei (LE-74); existence and bounds of the dressing transformation for gapless targets (LE-82). This passage is their only statement;
  Table 22.2 indexes them.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 8.7 Final-state-resolved response   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Resolve the response by the class of its final state, so that what the target absorbs can be handed to the detector chain of
  Part III.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define orthogonal projectors P_Λ on prompt final-state classes and the resolved response S(q, ω; Λ);
  (ii) write its independent-particle and screened pair-resolved forms; (iii) explain why the hand-off must keep q, v and the operator
  structure inside the final-state sum; (iv) state what Part III needs from Part II for each sector.
- Prerequisites: decomposition theorem, sectors → 8.6; master rate formula → 8.2; ρ_mn, density vertex → 3.2; screening → 4.4; signal chain
  → 1.5.
- Introduces: final-state class Λ and projectors P_Λ; final-state-resolved response S(q, ω; Λ); seeding (named; precise in 18.3).
- Key results: the resolved response and its independent-particle and screened forms [derived; REF]; the hand-off conditions [derived;
  REF].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §7 ((7.1)–(7.3)); returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §1.3.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 8.3 the chain from S(q, ω; Λ) to quanta, annotated with Part III chapters. Worked example 8.9: the
  pair-resolved response of the silicon valence band at one q. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T6.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 8.8 Uncertainty structure   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Turn each truncation of 8.5 into a nuisance parameter with a computed size, so that theory uncertainty is reported, not guessed.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define the nuisance of a truncation as the difference to its next order and say when that
  difference is computable; (ii) impose the exact relations of 8.3 as constraints on the nuisances; (iii) classify nuisances by how they
  act (normalization, tilt, threshold shift); (iv) state how the nuisances enter the likelihood of one exposure (likelihoods, confidence
  levels and profiling are recapped in App. C.14; the limit itself is set in 21.4).
- Prerequisites: expansion hierarchy, truncation label → 8.5; exact relations → 8.3; theory uncertainty, Poisson, exclusion → 1.5;
  hierarchy of approximations → 4.9; reference appendices → App. C.14.
- Introduces: nuisance parameter ϑ_k of a truncation; nuisance types (normalization, tilt, threshold); constrained nuisances; likelihood
  and profile likelihood (recap, App. C.14).
- Key results: nuisance per truncation and its constraints [derived as definitions; REF]; the nuisance terms of the likelihood [derived;
  REF; profiling and the limit are in 21.4].
- Sources: Public: Cowan et al. 1007.1727 (profile likelihood). Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §8; returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §5.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 8.6 nuisance types with an example for each sector. Worked example 8.10: the nuisance of the
  random-phase level in silicon from the local-field difference. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T7.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

---
## Part II — Channels

Part II is planned in full (SPINE-II, 2026-11-01; revised after its reviews, SPINE-II-REV). The status tags (REF, VER, EST, CL, GAP,
"new") are defined at the head of Chap. 8 and apply here. "Limit N.Mx" names a `limitbox`; Appendix F carries every one, with its
formula in the book's notation, its 𝔗 point and its plot specification, and each section lists its plots under "Limitbox plots".
"Open problems (exact gaps)" fields name, writer-side, the open items each passage must state, one home per label; DC-n items are the
computations App. E specifies. The chapter template of the design principles holds: (a)–(g) is a checklist, the narrative sets the
order; (f) is woven into the text at first appearance; (g) is the open-problems passage of each section plus one nuisance table per
chapter, never a restatement of gaps stated at home.

### Chapter 9. Channel atlas   [PLANNED; 4 sections, 15,000 words]
Opening: Part I gave the machinery; Part II must now use it on some twenty kinds of excitation and six kinds of dark-matter coupling, and
the danger is that a channel is explained twice or not at all. This chapter answers a simple question before any rate is computed: for
each excitation of a solid and each way dark matter can couple to it, which section treats the pair, what mass can it reach and what
energy does it leave behind? The answer is a map that the rest of Part II fills cell by cell and that Chap. 17 reads along the other axis.
It also shows how the literature formulas that Part II recovers sit on the expansion of Chap. 8, so that every Limit Check that follows
can be read as one point of that expansion. (Table 9.1 below is the printed Table 9.1 of 9.1, tab:9-atlas, as accepted at the Chapter 9
merge; the written opening motivates the chapter by three hazards: double counting of a dark photon in GaAs, the silicon phonon-assisted
mass window 0.333–0.405 MeV, and formulas used outside their range.)
**Atlas (excitation × interaction → home section; one home per cell; "—" = no channel at leading order; "○" = allowed at leading
order, computed in no section).** Columns, with the target operators of 8.1: electron density n_e (scattering); nuclei n_κ (scattering);
spin and current s, j, 𝒥; vector (dark-photon) absorption j_e, j_κ; axion-like (ALP) and scalar absorption s_e, 𝒥^{αα}_e, 𝒯_e;
inelastic, exothermic and other kinematics (rows of q, Δ_χ). Sector: the final-state sector of eq:8.6-partition (ad electron-following;
0 zero-phonon electronic; + phonon-dressed electronic; ad^s electron-following on the spin manifold of a magnet; qp the
quasiparticle-number sectors of a gapless target; (2,0) pair breaking, (2,m) pair breaking with m phonons). Counts (atlas9_1.py): 144
cells, 57 filled (columns 14/6/8/14/10/5), 8 ○ (1 density, 7 spin), 79 empty.

| Excitation | Sector | e density | nuclei | spin / current | vector absorption | ALP / scalar absorption | inelastic, exothermic, other |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Electron–hole pairs, band to band (3.2, 4.2) | 0 | 10.1 | — | 10.4 | 10.7 | 10.7 | 10.9 |
| Electron-gas continua and plasmons (4.5) | 0; qp | 10.3 | — | — | 10.7 | — | 17.3 |
| Excitons (4.9) | 0 | 10.5 | — | 10.5 | 10.7 | 10.7 | — |
| Core and semicore electrons (3.5) | 0 | 10.6 | — | ○ | 10.7 | — | — |
| Single phonons (5.3, 5.5) | ad | 11.2 | 11.1 | 11.6 | 11.8 | 11.8 | 17.3 |
| Multiphonons (5.6) | ad | 11.3 | 11.3 | 11.6 | — | — | — |
| Free nuclear recoil (1.3, 5.6) | ad | ○ | 11.4 | 11.6 | — | — | 11.4 |
| Quantum crystal: solid helium | ad | — | 11.5 | — | — | — | — |
| Migdal effect: electrons with a nuclear recoil (6.1) | 0, + | — | 12.3 | ○ | — | 12.6 | — |
| Phonon-assisted electron recoil (6.5) | + | 13.3 | — | ○ | 13.8 | 13.8 | — |
| Magnons (7.1) | ad^s | — | — | 14.1 | 14.3 | 14.3 | 17.3 |
| Two-magnon continua, magnon–phonon modes | ad^s | — | — | 14.2 | — | — | — |
| Chiral and nonreciprocal modes: crystals without inversion or time reversal | ad, ad^s | — | — | 14.5 | 14.5 | 14.5 | — |
| Precessing nuclear spins in a coherent field | ad | — | — | — | — | 14.6 | — |
| Superconducting quasiparticles (7.2) | (2,0), (2,m) | 15.1 | — | ○ | 15.4 | 15.4 | — |
| Dirac and narrow-gap pairs (7.3) | qp; 0 | 16.1 | — | ○ | 16.1 | — | — |
| Plasmons of sheets, wires and layered stacks | 0; qp | 16.2 | — | — | — | — | — |
| Graphene, nanotubes, quantum dots: confined electrons | 0; qp | 16.3 | — | — | 16.3 | — | — |
| Frenkel excitons, localized on one molecule | 0 | 16.4 | — | ○ | — | — | — |
| Dopant transitions (7.4) | 0 | 16.5 | — | ○ | 16.5 | — | — |
| Color centers and broken bonds (7.4) | ad | — | 16.6 | — | — | — | — |
| Two-level systems: tunneling atoms of a glass | ad | 16.7 | — | — | 16.7 | — | — |
| Mobile defects: defectons and crowdions, kinetics only (19.6) | ad | — | — | — | — | — | — |
| Acoustic modes of the whole body | ad | — | — | — | 11.8 | 11.8 | — |

*Notes under the table (printed: the caption of tab:9-atlas and the paragraphs after it).* The density cell of single phonons is the
coupling to charge through the Born charges; for a coupling to electrons alone, the acoustic peak of the electrons that follow the nuclei
is computed in 10.2. The absorption cells of excitons rest on measured optical data. An entry names the one section that computes the
rate (the density cell of the pair row is the crystal form factor of 10.1, screened in 10.2; the nuclear cell of the Migdal row is the
loss-function probability of 12.3, with 12.2 and 12.4 its neighbors). Coherent scattering off a whole random medium and the absorption of
a dark photon by the vibrations of a glass are not single excitations and have no row (16.7); processes second order in the coupling
(two insertions: pair absorption, dark Compton scattering φe → γe; 10.8) have no row either. The three 17.3 cells of the last column are
one formula for exothermic or inelastic scattering into a collective mode. Couplings to the nuclear spin and to B − L change from site to
site and reach the lattice through the random couplings of 11.6, which holds the spin entries of the lattice rows (one phonon to free
recoil) and the absorption of such bosons by phonons; the single-phonon absorption cells keep their home 11.8. The eight ○ cells (core
spin flip, spin-dependent Migdal, phonon-assisted × spin, spin-coupled pair breaking, Dirac spin, Frenkel × spin, dopants × spin, and the
electron density on a freely recoiling atom through its following cloud) are listed in the text of 9.1 with the reason each is allowed,
and form one grouped row of tab:22-gaps (22.2, class unsized). Mobile defects keep a row, empty by construction, because they set
delayed signals through the defect kinetics of 19.6 (sec:19.6-defecton, sec:19.6-crowdion), not because dark matter excites them.

### 9.1 The excitation–interaction matrix   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Map every pairing of a target excitation with a dark-matter interaction to the one section that treats it, so that no channel
  is explained twice or missed.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) locate any channel in Table 9.1 and name the target operator of 8.1 it needs; (ii) say, for each
  row built on a Part I excitation, which sector of the decomposition theorem holds its final states (the other rows are forward
  mentions, each glossed in the table with the section that treats it); (iii) explain why some cells are empty at leading order
  (selection rules, operator content, absorption kinematics, order in the coupling) and which cells are allowed but computed in no
  section (○); (iv) list, for a given dark-matter model, the cells it populates and the sections that compute them.
- Prerequisites: operator basis, target operators per species → 8.1; master rate formula → 8.2; decomposition theorem, sectors → 8.6;
  phonons → 5.3; magnons → 7.1; BCS → 7.2; Dirac Hamiltonian → 7.3; donor, color center → 7.4; exciton → 4.9; plasmon → 4.5.
- Introduces: channel atlas (Table 9.1); excitation axis and interaction axis; leading-order selection of a cell; the ○ mark (allowed at
  leading order, computed in no section); phonon-assisted electron recoil (op.: electron–hole pairs created together with a phonon;
  precise in 13.2).
- Key results: Table 9.1 (tab:9-atlas), 24 rows × 6 columns with a Sector column: of 144 cells, 57 hold one home section each, 8 are ○
  and 79 are empty at leading order, counted by figscripts/ch09/atlas9_1.py [definition; checked against the accepted tex of Chaps. 10–17
  and 19; the coverage is checked by the spine script]; the operator and sector of each row [from 8.1 and 8.6; REF]; the empty cells by
  four reasons: operator content (Born–Oppenheimer orthogonality empties the nuclear column of the electronic rows; n_e commutes with the
  total spin, so no magnon without spin–orbit coupling; no spin plasmon), absorption kinematics (an acoustic phonon at q = m_φv costs
  c_νm_φv ~ 10⁻⁸ m_φ; a free nucleus absorbs no massive boson, m_φ² + 2E_φM = 0; at q → 0 a vector or ALP vertex linear in the nuclear
  velocities creates exactly one phonon in a harmonic crystal), order in the coupling (two insertions, 10.8), and no new response for
  inelastic kinematics; material zeros: Si Z* = 0 (no dark-photon single optical phonon), a clean free-electron metal or superconductor
  absorbs only with a third body (15.4) [derived; REF].
- Sources: Writer only: surveys/dm-solid-state-processes/catalog-processes.md and catalog-analysis-and-gaps.md (catalog coverage: 91 rows);
  returns/R12/WP7-B1-v2.md §0, WP7-B2-v2.md §0, WP7-D-v2.md §0, WP7-T-v2.md §0 (placement of the extended catalog).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 9.1 the atlas with its notes (caption key: sectors, operators, entry, ○, —). Worked example 9.1
  (we:9-darkphoton): the dark photon along its column: scattering through the total charge and in-medium mixing (loss function, 10.2),
  polar optical phonons through Z* (empty in Si), the multiphonon charge vertex, nuclear recoil with weight Z_κ² beyond the core-electron
  momenta (11.4), color centers (16.6), the helium crystal of 11.5 via Z_κ − f_κ(q), Migdal; absorption on electrons (10.7; 13.8 in Si between
  1.17 and 3.4 eV), on the plasmon (10.7), core L and K edges (10.7), GaAs LO phonons (11.8), magnons through B′ (14.3), molecular
  magnets (14.5), body modes of a piezoelectric (11.8), superconductors only with an impurity or a phonon (15.4); 37 of the 57 filled
  cells (all 14 density, all six nuclear, all 14 vector absorption, three of the last column: 10.9 and two 17.3). Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 9.2 The kinematic map   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Draw, for every channel, the lowest dark-matter mass it can reach against the energy it deposits, so that targets can be
  compared by kinematics before rates.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the minimum mass of each channel from its threshold and dispersion (gap, sound speed,
  optical phonon, magnon gap, 2Δ_BCS); (ii) explain why acoustic phonons reach keV masses and why absorption reaches m_φ ≈ ω; (iii) place
  silicon, germanium, GaAs, aluminium and a Dirac material on one map; (iv) explain how the edge moves with the reference speed and the
  escape speed.
- Prerequisites: m_min, kinematic edge, v_max → 1.3; E_min(q), indirect gap → 3.3; sound speed c_ν → 5.2; ω_L, ω_T → 5.5; Δ_BCS,
  pair-breaking → 7.2; magnons → 7.1; Dirac Hamiltonian, Δ_D → 7.3; channel atlas → 9.1.
- Introduces: kinematic map (Fig. 9.1); channel minimum mass (the tex: lightest mass of a channel) m_min^ch = min_q q²/[2(qv_max −
  E_ch(q))] (eq:9.2-mch), with E_ch(q) and the minimizing momentum q̄; the floor 2ω_th/v_max² (eq:1.3-mmin) below every channel; five
  shapes (flat onset, gapless linear branch of speed c_ch, gapped linear branch, offset minimum, quadratic branch) and absorption;
  acoustic edge m_min^ac(ω_th); absorption line m_φ = ω (eq:9.2-abs); magnon gap from single-ion anisotropy (recap; 7.1 treats isotropic
  exchange); halo exponent (eq:9.2-halo, in words).
- Key results: eq:9.2-mch, eq:3.3-allowed read as a mass bound [derived; REF]; acoustic edge m_min^ac = ω_th/[2c_ν(v_max − c_ν)] at
  small thresholds, above the floor by ≈ v_max/4c_ν (21.5 for Si [111]) [derived; REF]; c_ch ≥ v_max closed to elastic scattering (Si
  bulk plasmon, phase speed falling to 9.2×10⁻³ = 3.5 v_max where its line ends at 2.24 keV; III–V Dirac cones v_D ≈ 1.8 v_max)
  [derived; REF]; channel edges of Table 9.2 (tab:9-edges) for the running materials: floors Ge 0.212, Si 0.333, GaAs 0.433 MeV;
  phonon-assisted onsets Si 0.3332/0.3374, Ge 0.2119–0.2141, GaAs 0.433–0.436 MeV; zero-phonon Si 0.405–0.408 MeV, Ge ≈ 0.32 MeV
  (estimate, bounds in neither direction), GaAs L valleys 0.517–0.560 MeV; Al pairs 0.10 keV; acoustic edges at 1 meV Si 6.1, Al 8.6
  (c_LA,max 6.67 km/s along [111], 0 K constants and density of 20.3; computed here), Ge 10.2, GaAs 10.3–12.3 keV (computed tensors);
  optical phonons GaAs 10.35, Si 18.3 keV; ZrTe₅ lightest mass 7.1 keV (slow axis; 9.0 and 9.6 keV along the others; isotropic cone
  8.0 keV, floor 6.72 keV; computed here), NiO magnon edge 7.3 keV at 5 meV (c_M = 1.36×10⁻⁴), gapped NiO mode 1.30 keV; random-site
  channels and magnon pairs on the floor (0.285 keV at 1 meV); core shells Ge 3d 8.5–8.7, Si 2p 28.6 MeV (computed here); free-recoil
  impulse onsets Si 17.5, Ge 21.3 MeV (estimate); a free Si nucleus takes up the gap from 46.8 MeV (reduced mass); absorption Si
  1.1728 eV, GaAs 36.35 meV, Al 0.36 meV, ZrTe₅ 23.6 meV, 2.85×10⁵ below scattering at equal energy [derived; computed, fig9_1.log;
  quoted from the home sections]; halo exponent d ln m/d ln v_max = −q̄v_max/[q̄v_max − E_ch(q̄)]: −2 flat, −1.012 Si acoustic, −1 free
  nucleus, −2.37 ZrTe₅ (−2.12 to −2.74 by axis), beyond −2 only at a stationary minimum (q̄v = 2E − q̄E′); v_esc ± 25 km/s moves the floor
  6.3 %, the Si acoustic edge 3.2 %, the free-recoil edges 3.1 %, ZrTe₅ 7.4 % [derived; computed, fig9_1.log].
- Sources: Public: Kahn and Lin 2108.03239 §2; Essig et al. 2203.08297 §2. Writer only: returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §3; returns/R16/WP8-F-v3.md §2.3.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 9.1 (fig:9-kinmap) the kinematic map (mass against threshold) for all channels, panel (b) the

### 9.3 Excitations on the momentum–energy plane   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Place every excitation of Table 9.1 on the (q, ω) plane with its sector, so that the overlaps of channels, and the few places
  where amplitudes interfere, are visible before Part II computes them.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) draw the support of each excitation in (q, ω) for silicon (pair continuum, plasmon, phonon branches,
  multiphonon band, impulse line, phonon-assisted region); (ii) mark the sector of each region; (iii) find the regions where channels
  overlap and say why an overlap is not an interference (sectors add, and so do phonon numbers) and where an interference term of 8.6
  does live: the cross cells S^A_eκ of a coupling to both species, and the Fano term in the edge band at the boundary of the two
  electronic sectors; (iv) overlay the kinematic reach of 1.3 for given m_χ.
- Prerequisites: decomposition theorem, sectors → 8.6; accessible (q, ω) region → 1.3; independent-particle S → 4.2; plasmon dispersion →
  4.5; phonon dispersion → 5.2; Debye–Waller → 5.6; kinematic map → 9.2.
- Introduces: support of a response; (q, ω) overlay of excitations (Fig. 9.2); overlap regions and where amplitudes interfere: the cross
  cells within a sector and the Fano term at the P_0/P_+ edge band (op.; precise in 17.4; the planned "cross-sector interference" is not
  used, since sectors add).
- Key results: support of each excitation in silicon, by sector (Fig. 9.2: one phonon fills 0–64 meV at every q beyond the zone,
  ⟨u_x²⟩ = 2.45×10⁻³ Å², 2W = 0.016/0.063/1.0 at 5/10/40 keV; free recoil crosses E_g at 247 keV, band edge 212 keV, needs m_χ ≥ 46.8 MeV;
  E_min(q) 3.17 eV at q → 0 (scissor-shifted PBE, below the measured direct gap 3.4 eV), valley images 1.94, 2.63, 3.25, 4.96 keV…;
  phonon-assisted onset ≤ 1.23 eV at every q; the plasmon line of the quadratic dispersion ends at 2.24 keV, the Lindhard zero at 2.60 keV)
  [derived; computed, fig9_2.log]; the overlaps: one and two phonons below 64 meV, the impulse band and the pair continuum above 212 keV
  and 1.17 eV, the phonon sideband and the zero-phonon continuum above E_min(q); in silicon the phonon-assisted region (ω ≥ 1.18 eV) does
  not overlap the multiphonon band, which reaches the gap only above 212–247 keV; four interference regions (tab:9-overlaps): S^ad_eκ
  (the following cloud cancels the nuclear charge at long wavelength, 14 − 14 = 0; the one place where a cross term decides a rate at
  leading order), S^0_eκ and S^+_eκ (5.47×10⁻⁴ of the electronic rate for a dark photon), the Fano coefficients c^F_ν (zero in Si and Ge
  at one-body level; a few percent at most of first emissions in GaAs, 13.6) [derived; REF 8.6, 13.6, 17.4]; reach of 0.3, 1, 10 MeV
  on the plane [derived; computed]; supports of the non-silicon rows (tab:9-supports) [quoted from their homes].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §6.2 (sector table); returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §1.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 9.2 (fig:9-qomega) the (q, ω) overlay for silicon with sectors shaded and m_χ = 0.3, 1, 10 MeV
  reach curves, drawn on the axes of Fig. 1.5 so that the step from kinematics to excitations is visible; Table 9.3 (tab:9-overlaps) the
  four interference regions; Table 9.4 (tab:9-supports) the supports of the other rows. Worked example 9.3 (we:9-open): at q = 5 keV and
  ω = 2 eV (m_χ ≥ 1.11 MeV), which excitations of silicon are open: electron-following closed; zero-phonon closed along [100]/[110]/[111]

### 9.4 Limit Checks and the truncation label   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Place each widely used formula of Part II on the truncation label once, and show how the comparison plot of its Limit Check
  follows from that placement.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) build and read the comparison plot of a Limit Check: the formula (written in the book's notation,
  with a one-line citation), the fuller result it is compared with (one step 𝔗 → 𝔗_k⁺ further along one axis, or in a parameter the label
  records in words), the controlling parameter on the x-axis, the leading correction as a third curve, the gray band where the two agree
  to the stated tolerance, and the one sentence on what fails outside it (the reading of a box is given here in full, since the front
  matter has none; the chain is in the running text, the record in App. F); (ii) place a formula on 𝔗 from its stated assumptions, as a
  deviation from the base point 𝔗₀ of 8.5; (iii) predict its leading correction, and hence the plot's x-axis, from the axis of the
  largest step of the label, or from its qualifier; (iv) use Appendix F to find every formula's home, its plot specification and whether
  its leading correction is sized.
- Prerequisites: truncation label 𝔗, expansion hierarchy → 8.5; decomposition theorem → 8.6; channel atlas → 9.1; reference appendices →
  App. F.1.
- Introduces: Limit Check comparison plot (precise; reading and construction); controlling parameter of a box; fuller result; approximation
  chain; leading correction; qualifiers of 𝔗 sorted in four kinds (long wavelength; kinematics of halo and signal; dark-matter side and
  coupling order; target model and method); unsized correction (App. F usage); established to the stated tolerance inside the band, an
  estimate of the failure point when the fuller result is a model, nothing shaded = fails over the plotted range (the book has no
  separate status words; open items go to 22.2).
- Key results: the plot construction, worked on the Limit Check of 1.3 (free recoil against the Einstein well), and the map to Appendix F
  [definition]; the placement of every Part II literature formula on 𝔗 by the axis of its leading correction (Fig. 9.3 and Table 9.5;
  done here and nowhere else; counts from figscripts/ch09/fig9_3.py, which parses the tex): 75 Part II Limit Checks (Ch10 15, Ch11 20,
  Ch12 9, Ch13 2, Ch14 8, Ch15 3, Ch16 17, Ch17 1), 25 with the leading correction on a component of 𝔗 (𝒳 11, 𝒦 6, n_A 2, 𝔐 2, n_D 2,
  n_B 1, n_T 1, n_g 0) and 50 on qualifiers (long wavelength 5, kinematics and halo 9, dark-matter side 5, target model 31); on the label
