### Chapter 10. Electronic excitations   [PLANNED; 10 sections, 45,000 words]
Opening: An electron–hole pair is the signal most sub-GeV searches look for, and silicon's gap of about 1.1 eV is why they reach MeV
masses. The question of this chapter is how faithfully the rate of making such pairs can be computed in a real crystal: what the band
structure gives on its own, what screening and the electron–hole attraction change, which couplings beyond the density matter, and what
happens when the dark matter is absorbed rather than scattered, or carries a mass splitting or a relativistic speed. Everything here is
at static lattice, the electronic sector of Chap. 8; the lattice enters in Chaps. 11–13, and the pairs made here are followed into the
detector in Chap. 18.

### 10.1 The crystal form factor   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the electron-recoil rate of a crystal at independent-particle level and recover the crystal form factor used by
  experiments.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the rate from the master formula with the density row and Bloch states, as a sum of
  |ρ_mn(k, q)|² over pairs; (ii) define the crystal form factor and convert between its normalizations; (iii) explain the q-dependence of
  the silicon form factor near q₀ and at the zone scale; (iv) say what a calculation of it needs (bands, plane-wave vertices, Umklapp
  vectors) and what it leaves out (screening, excitons, the lattice).
- Prerequisites: master rate formula → 8.2; ρ_mn, density vertex, Bloch → 3.2; Umklapp → 3.1; indirect gap, E_min → 3.3; F_DM, σ̄_e →
  1.4; η(v_min) → 1.3; band-gap problem, scissor → 3.4; reference appendices → App. E.7.
- Introduces: crystal form factor f_crys(q, ω); independent-particle electron-recoil rate; normalization conversions between the
  common conventions.
- Key results: Limit 10.1a: crystal form-factor rate [derived in D.29; cited: Essig et al. 1509.01598 Eqs. (3.13), (3.17), eq. to fix
  (check at briefing whether this is the equation that the box of 21.1 cites as Eq. (17)); REF].
- Computational connection: f_crys is computed from Kohn–Sham bands with a scissor and plane-wave vertices (App. E.7); grids in k and q and
  the all-electron correction at large q (App. E.2).
- Sources: Public: 1509.01598; Griffin et al. 2105.05253; Trickle et al. 2210.14917. Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §1, §7 (L1);
  returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §9.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.1 the silicon crystal form factor in (q, ω). Worked example 10.1: the silicon rate at m_χ = 10 MeV
  for heavy and light mediators, from the tabulated form factor. Limitbox plots: 10.1a (one panel).
- Threads: advances T1.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 10.2 Screening and the loss-function rate   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Add screening and local fields to the electronic rate, show when the loss function gives it exactly, and derive the sum-rule
  bounds on it.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the loss-function rate from the S–L relation for a density-coupled probe, applying the
  exactness condition proved in 8.4 by reference; (ii) compare independent-particle, RPA and RPA + local-field rates and their ratios in silicon; (iii) derive
  the upper bounds on the rate from the f-sum and the static step 1 − ε^{-1}(q, 0); (iv) explain why the static-step bound fails for a
  relaxed lattice with an electron-only coupling as q → 0.
- Prerequisites: S–L relation, loss function, ε → 4.4; RPA + local fields → 4.6; screening for every coupling, external-field type →
  8.4; crystal form factor → 10.1; f-sum → 4.3; Z*, ε₀ → 5.5; reference appendices → App. E.6.
- Introduces: loss-function rate; static step 1 − ε^{-1}(q, 0) and its bound; relaxed-lattice correction to the step; level ratios.
- Key results: Limit 10.2a: loss-function rate [derived; cited: Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2101.08275; Hochberg et al. 2101.08263 (eq. to
  fix); REF]; Limit 10.2b: sum-rule bounds on the loss function, with the relaxed-lattice term that grows as (c_LA q)⁻², with c_LA the longitudinal sound speed (c_ν of 5.2 for the LA branch) [derived; cited:
  2110.01587, 2501.18261, 2608.05282 (eqs. to fix); REF for the clamped bound, VER for the relaxed term]; the pointwise screening factor
  1/|ε|² is not the rate ratio (0.117/0.451 pointwise against 1.4–2.5 in rate) [derived; REF].
- Computational connection: L(q, ω) with local fields from Kohn–Sham states (App. E.6); measured optical and energy-loss data at q ≈ 0;
  the relaxed-lattice static step is specified in App. E.16.
- Sources: Public: 2101.08275; 2101.08263; 2110.01587. Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §1, §7 (L2, L17); returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §9.2;
  returns/R16/CW3.md (CW3.4).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.2 IP, RPA and RPA + local-field rates of silicon. Worked example 10.2: the f-sum bound on the
  silicon rate at m_χ = 1 MeV. Limitbox plots: 10.2a, 10.2b (one panel each).
- Threads: advances T2, T3.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 10.3 Electron-gas models and plasmons   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the free-electron models of the loss function and the production of plasmons by dark matter.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the Lindhard and Mermin rates as the homogeneous-gas limit of 10.2 and say when they fit a
  semiconductor; (ii) derive the plasmon contribution from the pole of 1/ε and its Landau-damping cutoff; (iii) show that plasmon
  production by halo dark matter is kinematically suppressed in silicon and estimate by how much; (iv) state what a plasmon decays into
  (pairs, phonons) and where that is treated.
- Prerequisites: Lindhard function, Mermin function, plasmon dispersion, q_c → 4.5; loss-function rate → 10.2; kinematic reach → 1.3;
  f-sum → 4.3.
- Introduces: electron-gas loss-function rate; plasmon production rate; plasmon branching into pairs and phonons (named).
- Key results: Limit 10.3a: Lindhard and Mermin loss-function rates [derived; cited: 2101.08275 Eqs. (17)–(24); 2104.12786; REF]; Limit
  10.3b: plasmon production from the pole of 1/ε, q_c ≈ 2.24 keV in silicon, the often-quoted enhancement (5/1.9)² refuted (≈ 1.4)
  [derived; cited: Kurinsky et al. 2002.06937; Kozaczuk and Lin 2003.12077; REF].
- Sources: Public: 2002.06937; 2003.12077; 2101.08275. Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §4, §7 (L10, L11); returns/R16/CW1.md §14
  (plasmon branching).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.3 the silicon plasmon pole against the halo reach. Worked example 10.3: the plasmon rate in
  silicon at the highest halo speed. Limitbox plots: 10.3a, 10.3b (one panel each).
- Threads: advances T2.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 10.4 Spin and current couplings   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Extend the electronic rate to couplings beyond the density, and state which responses must be computed for each.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) build the minimal response table: the independent responses of n_e, s_e, j_e and the
  velocity-squared row that cover all operators of 8.1; (ii) show which of them are screened and which are not; (iii) apply the
  Cauchy–Schwarz bound of 8.3 to a mixed (density–current) response and show that one ratio, r_IP, decides the band.
- Prerequisites: operator basis, target operators per species → 8.1; positivity, interference bound → 8.3; screening for every coupling
  → 8.4; crystal form factor → 10.1; s_e(q) → 7.1; j_e → 4.3; reference appendices → App. E.7.
- Introduces: minimal response table (the independent electronic responses); spin and current crystal responses; mixed-block band
  [(√r_IP − 1)², (√r_IP + 1)²] with the independent-particle ratio r_IP.
- Key results: Limit 10.4a: crystal responses of the effective operators, with the screening of the density row only [derived in D.30;
  cited: Catena et al. 2105.02233; 2009.13534; 2210.14917; 2510.25835 (eqs. to fix); REF]; mixed-block band [derived in D.30; VER];
  the in-medium spin–current ratio is at least 12 at r_IP ≳ 20 [derived bound; REF; the ratio itself is open, below].
- Computational connection: the response tables (twelve real functions per material, with the exported proper responses) are specified
  in App. E.16.
- Sources: Public: 2105.02233; 2009.13534; 2210.14917. Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §2, §7 (L3); returns/R16/CW1.md §11–§12, §15.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 10.1 the minimal response table with each operator's row. Worked example 10.4: the band on the
  mixed silicon response for a given r_IP. Limitbox plots: 10.4a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): local-field screening of the current row and the in-medium spin–current ratio itself (LE-35; App. E.16).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 10.5 Excitons at finite momentum   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Add the electron–hole interaction to the electronic rate at finite q and state the thresholds and selection rules it brings.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the BSE rate at finite q through E_λ(q) and the exciton form factor; (ii) explain why
  excitons shift the onset below E_min(q) and redistribute weight; (iii) state the selection rules for the lowest excitons of silicon at
  the zone boundary; (iv) estimate the error of the static, resonant-only treatment; (v) explain the triplet (spin) channel of excitons
  and what is known of it.
- Prerequisites: BSE, exciton, E_λ → 4.9; Wannier–Mott, μ_x → 4.9; loss-function rate → 10.2; E_min, k_c → 3.3; minimal response table →
  10.4; reference appendices → App. C.17.
- Introduces: exciton rate at finite q; exciton form factor; resonant-only (Tamm–Dancoff) and coupled forms; bound exciton lines at the
  threshold; triplet channel.
- Key results: Limit 10.5a: BSE exciton rate [derived; cited: 2507.14345 (eq. to fix); REF]; the resonant-only form of the real part,
  L^res/L ≈ [ε₁/ε₁^res]² [derived; VER], overstates L by about 2 at |q| ≈ 0.7 Å⁻¹ and ω ≈ 1.3 eV (the q range of the phonon-assisted
  window), the factor falling to 1 as q grows [EST]; retarded resonant-only coupling error ≈ 10% on ε₂ [derived; VER].
- Computational connection: finite-q BSE in the transition basis with a scissor (App. E.6); the beyond-resonant run is specified in App. E.15.
- Sources: Public: 2507.14345; Rohlfing and Louie (2000). Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §3, §7 (L14); returns/R16/CW1.md §5–§6.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.4 the silicon loss function with and without excitons at q = 1 keV. Worked example 10.5: the
  exciton line of silicon below the pair onset. Limitbox plots: 10.5a (one panel).
- Threads: advances T2.
- Open problems (exact gaps): finite-q BSE beyond the resonant-only form with bound lines (LE-28; App. E.15); the triplet channel beyond
  independent particles (LE-34); Frenkel and self-trapped excitons in NaI (LE-32; the Frenkel exciton is made precise in 16.4, forward).
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 10.6 Core and relativistic states   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the contributions of core and semicore electrons at large q and the relativistic corrections they need.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) estimate the q above which core shells contribute in Si and Ge; (ii) write the core contribution
  with all-electron vertices; (iii) bound the relativistic correction to the response by the Foldy–Wouthuysen expansion to O(1/m_e²);
  (iv) explain the factorization of core and valence responses and when it fails.
- Prerequisites: core states, semicore, (Zα)², PAW, all-electron → 3.5; crystal form factor → 10.1; f-sum, species-resolved → 4.3;
  ionization form factor → 2.2; reference appendices → App. E.2, C.15.
- Introduces: core and semicore responses; Foldy–Wouthuysen corrections (the Dirac equation and the reduction to O(1/m_e²) are
  recapped in App. C.15); high-momentum tail of the rate; core–valence factorization.
- Key results: core onset estimates for Si and Ge [derived; REF]; Foldy–Wouthuysen bound ≈ 10⁻³, ≤ 2% for Ge 3d, next order ≈ 10⁻⁶
  [derived; VER]; orbit–orbit and mass–velocity shifts of the f-sum [derived; REF].
- Computational connection: all-electron reconstruction (App. E.2); the four-component finite-q response is specified in App. E.15, and
  the orbit–orbit and mass–velocity f-sum shifts in App. E.16.
- Sources: Public: Griffin et al. 2105.05253; 2509.14534. Writer only: returns/R8/WP1-v3.md §5; returns/R16/CW1.md §10; returns/R16/CW3.md
  (orbit–orbit shift).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.5 valence, semicore and core shares of the Ge response against q. Worked example 10.6: the
  Ge 3d onset in q. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the four-component finite-q response with no-pair projection (LE-33; App. E.15); spin–orbit mixing near the
  Ge edge (LE-36; App. E.15).
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 10.7 Absorption on electrons   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the absorption of dark photons, axion-like particles and scalars by electrons, with in-medium mixing.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the dark-photon absorption rate ∝ σ₁ with the in-medium mixing of 8.4, and its transverse
  and longitudinal parts; (ii) derive axion-like absorption through the axioelectric coupling and, in a magnetic field, through
  B̂₀·Im[−ε^{-1}]·B̂₀; (iii) derive scalar absorption through the kinetic-energy density and its in-medium mixing; (iv) state why measured
  σ₁ near the silicon indirect edge must be transferred to millikelvin before it is used (forward: the transfer and its size are 13.5).
- Prerequisites: σ₁, ε^T, optical conductivity → 4.4; in-medium mixing, external-field type → 8.4; absorption operators, 𝒯_e(q) → 8.1;
  master rate formula → 8.2; axion-like and scalar couplings → 1.4; reference appendices → App. A.9.
- Introduces: in-medium dark-photon absorption rate; axioelectric absorption; ALP–photon absorption in a static field 𝐁₀; scalar
  absorption via 𝒯_e.
- Key results: Limit 10.7a: absorption ∝ σ₁ with in-medium mixing (panel 1), and the ALP–photon form in a field (panel 2) [derived;
  cited: Hochberg, Lin and Zurek 1608.01994; An et al. 1412.8378; Mitridate et al. 2106.12586; 2305.05681 Eq. (6); REF, the tensor form in
  a field VER]; Limit 10.7b: scalar absorption through 𝒯_e, sum rule for one-body regular potentials only [derived; cited: 2106.12586
  Eq. (18); VER].
- Computational connection: σ₁ from measured data or from the BSE (App. E.6); the kinetic row Im R_𝒯𝒯 is specified in App. E.16.
- Sources: Public: 1608.01994; 1412.8378; 2106.12586; 2305.05681. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md §4; returns/R8/WP0-v3.md (2.9);
  returns/R16/CW3.md §3; returns/R16/CW7.md (scalar row).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.6 dark-photon, ALP and scalar absorption rates of silicon against mass. Worked example 10.7:

- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 10.8 Two-insertion processes   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the processes in which a bosonic dark-matter field acts on the target twice: the absorption of two quanta and the
  scattering of the field off electrons (dark Compton scattering).
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the rate of a process with two insertions of the field from the second-order amplitudes of
  2.4 and the master formula; (ii) derive pair absorption for scalar, pseudoscalar, vector and axial couplings; (iii) derive dark Compton
  scattering of a vector, show that its tree-level form is exact and that its non-relativistic form holds through O(x²) with x = m_φ/m_e;
  (iv) show that the axion-like form misses the factor (m_φ + 2m_e)/(m_φ + m_e).
- Prerequisites: second-order amplitudes → 2.4; absorption on electrons → 10.7; master rate formula → 8.2; absorption operators → 8.1;
  axion-like couplings → 1.4.
- Introduces: two-insertion processes (pair absorption, dark Compton scattering).
- Key results: Limit 10.8a: pair absorption [derived; cited: 2507.14287 Eqs. (3), (5)–(8); REF]; Limit 10.8b: dark Compton scattering
  (panel 1: 2109.08168 Eq. (2), exact at tree level, against its non-relativistic form, first difference at O(x³); panel 2: the
  axion-like Eq. (3) against the corrected form with (m_φ + 2m_e)/(m_φ + m_e)) [derived; cited: 2109.08168; REF for Eq. (2), VER for the
  exactness and the correction].
- Computational connection: the two-insertion responses are specified in App. E.26.
- Sources: Public: 2507.14287; 2109.08168. Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-D-v2.md §3; returns/R16/CW3.md §4; returns/R16/CW1.md §22–§23.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 10.7 pair-absorption and dark Compton rates in silicon against m_φ. Worked example 10.8: the size
  of the factor (m_φ + 2m_e)/(m_φ + m_e) at m_φ = 100 keV. Limitbox plots: 10.8a (one panel), 10.8b (two panels).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 3,500.

- Purpose: Apply the master formula to dark matter with a mass splitting, a relativistic flux or a fermionic absorption channel in the

  splittings; (ii) write the relativistic density row for boosted dark matter and bound the ratio to the non-relativistic form;

### Chapter 11. Lattice excitations   [PLANNED; 9 sections, 42,500 words]
Opening: Below about 10 MeV, a dark particle that couples to nucleons cannot give a nucleus enough energy to free it from the lattice,
and the energy goes into phonons instead. This chapter asks what that rate is, from a single phonon at the lowest masses to the free
nuclear recoil that standard searches assume at the highest, and which few material numbers set it: sound speeds, Born charges,
Debye–Waller exponents. It reuses the harmonic crystal and the displacement correlations of Chap. 5 inside the master formula of
Chap. 8, adds what lies beyond the harmonic picture, and derives the daily modulation that a crystal's direction dependence imprints on
both the electronic rates of Chap. 10 and the lattice rates here. The two mixed sectors that follow, the Migdal effect and
phonon-assisted electron recoil, start from this electron-following response.

### 11.1 Acoustic phonons   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the single-acoustic-phonon rate of a nucleon-coupled dark particle and reduce it to the few material numbers that set
  sensitivity.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the one-phonon term of the nuclear response from the harmonic crystal and the master
  formula; (ii) derive its long-wavelength form, a structure-factor coefficient set by the sound speeds; (iii) show that the light-mediator
  rate per unit mass is target-independent above the edge, and derive its leading correction 1 − (1 + κ_η)m_min^ac/m_χ, κ_η = 3.14;
  (iv) compute the sphere-averaged sound speed from an elastic tensor (continuum elasticity, Voigt notation and the Christoffel problem
  are recapped in App. C.18) and the Debye temperature it implies. How the heavy-mediator rate ranks two materials is a figure-of-merit
  question and is treated once, in 17.5 (forward).
- Prerequisites: phonons, ℓ_Qν, B_Qν → 5.3; sound speed c_ν, acoustic sum rule, Debye model → 5.2; master rate formula → 8.2; nuclear
  density operator → 8.1; Debye–Waller → 5.6; acoustic edge, kinematic map → 9.2; η(v_min) → 1.3; reference appendices → App. C.18.
- Introduces: single-phonon nuclear response; structure-factor coefficient of the acoustic branches; light-mediator target independence
  and its correction constant κ_η; sphere-mean longitudinal speed c̄_LA (Christoffel average); Rayleigh bound on c_LA from the bulk
  modulus B (c_LA the longitudinal sound speed).
- Key results: Limit 11.1a: long-wavelength acoustic structure factor [derived; cited: 2205.02250 Eq. (30) with Eq. (27); prior art
  Campbell-Deem et al. 1911.03482 Eq. (10); REF; its range of validity, corrected in the final research state, VER]; Limit 11.1b:
  light-mediator rate ∝ 1/ω_th, target-independent, with the correction factor above [derived; cited: 1910.10716 Eq. (35); VER];
  Limit 11.1c: sphere-mean c̄_LA from the Christoffel problem with a Voigt-ordered tensor [derived; cited: 1910.10716 App. A3; VER]; the
  elastic Debye temperature in the Anderson convention (a key result, not a limitbox: it fixes a convention and has no controlling
  parameter) [derived; tested against 2008.08560 and 1901.07569; REF]; c_LA ≥ √(B/ρ_T) [derived; VER].
- Computational connection: sound speeds from elastic tensors (DFPT or finite strain, App. E.4); the shear-row ordering of database tensors
  is a known pitfall (App. E.4).
- Sources: Public: 2205.02250; 1911.03482; 1910.10716 (its Table I sound speeds with the inconsistencies named in 17.5).
  Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §1–§2; returns/R16/WP8-F-v3.md §2.3; returns/R17/WP8-errata.md §6–§7, §12; state/NOVELTY.md N12.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 11.1 the single-phonon rate per mass for Si, Ge, diamond, against m_χ, light and heavy
  mediators. Worked example 11.1: c̄_LA of silicon from its elastic constants; worked example 11.2: the light-mediator correction at 3 m_min^ac.
  Limitbox plots: 11.1a, 11.1b, 11.1c (one panel each).
- Threads: advances T1.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 11.2 Optical phonons in polar crystals   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the single-optical-phonon rate of a dark photon (or any total-charge coupling) in a polar crystal, through Born
  charges and dielectric constants.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the polar one-phonon rate from the total-charge row of 8.4 with Z*_κ and ε_∞; (ii) derive
  its per-mass band integral from the Kramers–Kronig relation of 1/ε at ω = 0 and the LST relation; (iii) state the validity (rank-1 ε,
  ω ≪ E_g, interlacing modes per axis) and the leading corrections; (iv) extract Z*/ε_∞ of GaAs from its LO–TO splitting; (v) explain why
  the rate order of polar targets is reversed in their reach.
- Prerequisites: Z*, ε₀, LO–TO, LST → 5.5; ε_∞ → 4.4; Fröhlich → 6.2; external-field type, total dielectric matrix → 8.4; single-phonon
  nuclear response → 11.1; Kramers–Kronig → 4.3.
- Introduces: polar single-phonon rate; polar band integral 𝓘_pol; per-axis interlacing condition; LO–TO extraction of Z*/ε_∞.
- Key results: Limit 11.2a: single-phonon and Born-charge rates [derived; cited: Knapen, Lin, Pyle and Zurek 1712.06598; Griffin et al.
  1807.10291; 1910.08092; 2009.13534; REF]; Limit 11.2b: the polar matrix element and band integral [derived; cited:
  1807.10291 v1 Eqs. (21)–(22), (24); prior art for the formula; REF]; Limit 11.2c: ε₀ − ε_∞ = Z*²e²/(Ωμ_redω_T²) for one TO branch
  [derived; checked on 1910.10716 Table I; REF]; Limit 11.2d: Z*/ε_∞ of GaAs from LO–TO, using the LST relation of 5.5 by reference
  [derived; LST, Born and Huang; VER].
- Computational connection: Z*, ε_∞ and phonons from DFPT (App. E.4).
- Sources: Public: 1712.06598; 1807.10291; 1910.08092; Born and Huang Ch. II. Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §4; returns/R16/WP8-F-v3.md
  §2.5, §8; returns/R16/CW2.md §20 (CW2.24); state/NOVELTY.md N13.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 11.2 the GaAs dark-photon single-phonon rate. Worked example 11.3: 𝓘_pol/ρ_T for GaAs and LiF.
  Limitbox plots: 11.2a, 11.2b, 11.2c, 11.2d (one panel each).
- Threads: advances T2.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 11.3 Multiphonon response   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the nuclear response of a harmonic crystal at all phonon orders and the controlled approximations to it.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the exact harmonic response from the displacement correlations of 5.6 at finite T, with
  coherent and incoherent parts; (ii) derive the incoherent approximation and its Poisson tail and state when they hold; (iii) bound the
  coherent remainder with the exact ω-integrated weight and a certified pointwise band; (iv) treat the second-order thermal diffuse term
  near a reciprocal vector.
- Prerequisites: 𝒞_κκ′, Debye–Waller, Franck–Condon, Poisson → 5.6; single-phonon nuclear response → 11.1; recoil parameter x_R,
  lattice regime label → 8.5; master rate formula → 8.2; phonon density of states → 5.2; reference appendices → App. C.6, E.4.
- Introduces: harmonic multiphonon response (coherent and incoherent parts); incoherent approximation; Poisson phonon tail; coherent
  weight and its certified band; second-order thermal diffuse scattering.
- Key results: Limit 11.3a: multiphonon incoherent approximation and Poisson tail [derived in D.31; cited: Campbell-Deem et al.
  1911.03482; 2205.02250; 2011.09477; REF]; exact coherent weight and pointwise band [derived in D.31; VER]; Limit 11.3b: second-order
  thermal diffuse scattering ∝ 1/|q − G| [derived as a bound; classical result, Warren (1969), to confirm at briefing; VER].
- Computational connection: the multiphonon library is specified in App. E.17 (phonon stack, tables in q, q̂, ω).
- Sources: Public: 1911.03482; 2205.02250; 2011.09477. Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §2–§3; returns/R16/CW2.md §1–§3, §8.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 11.3 one-, two- and many-phonon parts of the silicon response at q = 10 keV. Worked example 11.4:
  the Poisson tail weight above 3ω_D in silicon. Limitbox plots: 11.3a (one panel), 11.3b (one panel; STYLE's fallback: the
  1/|q − G| dependence with the validity boundary T ≫ ω of the soft pair marked, since the full coherent two-phonon term is not computed).
- Threads: advances T3.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the decay of the valence charge deformation at q ≫ q_BZ, the zone-boundary momentum (LE-51; App. E.17).
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 11.4 Impulse limit and free nuclear recoil   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Show how the multiphonon response becomes the free nuclear recoil of the standard rate, with its corrections, and treat
  fermionic absorption on nuclei.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the impulse limit of the nuclear response (q²/2M ≫ ω_Qν) and its Gaussian width from the
  kinetic energy of the nuclei; (ii) recover the standard nuclear-recoil rate with the Helm form factor; (iii) state where the impulse
  approximation fails in silicon and germanium and the onset energies; (iv) derive fermionic absorption on nuclei and the m_χ²(1 − m_χ/M)
  factor from continuity.
- Prerequisites: multiphonon response → 11.3; Helm, σ_N, A² → 2.2; nuclear recoil, E_R → 1.3; master rate formula → 8.2; fermionic
  absorption → 10.9; K̄ → 4.3.
- Introduces: impulse approximation (precise); Doppler width of the impulse peak; free-recoil limit; fermionic absorption on nuclei.
- Key results: Limit 11.4a: free nuclear recoil with the Helm form factor as the impulse limit [derived in D.32; cited: Lewin and Smith
  (1996); REF]; impulse onset 91.5/112 keV and its Si/Ge values [derived; REF]; Limit 11.4b: fermionic absorption on nuclei [derived;
  cited: 1905.12635 Eqs. (1), (3), (4); REF].
- Computational connection: the Doppler width needs the nuclear momentum distribution ⟨p²⟩ of each species, specified in App. E.17.

### 11.5 Anharmonic and quantum-crystal corrections   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Add anharmonicity to the nuclear response, with bounds that hold beyond perturbation theory, and treat quantum crystals.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the first anharmonic correction to the multiphonon response; (ii) bound the anharmonic
  remainder of the cumulative response by the Markov–Krein band of its exact moments and explain why the band is absolute, not relative;
  (iii) treat solid helium with self-consistent phonons and the quasi-harmonic limit; (iv) say where perturbative anharmonicity fails
  (soft modes, light atoms).
- Prerequisites: Φ^{(3)}, anharmonic, SCHA, quasi-harmonic → 5.7; multiphonon response → 11.3; impulse approximation → 11.4; moment
  matrices → 8.3; reference appendices → App. C.10.
- Introduces: anharmonic correction to the nuclear response; Markov–Krein moment band; quantum crystal (solid helium; recap, one
  paragraph: a zero-point amplitude comparable to the lattice spacing makes the harmonic expansion fail at its lowest order).
- Key results: Limit 11.5a: anharmonic nuclear-recoil corrections [derived; cited: 2309.10839; REF]; Limit 11.5b: solid helium response,
  quasi-harmonic limit plus SCHA correction [derived; cited: 2409.02439 Eqs. (2), (4)–(5); REF]; Limit 11.5c: the Markov–Krein band
  [derived; classical moment theory, Akhiezer (1965), to confirm at briefing; VER].
- Computational connection: anharmonic force constants and moments by SCHA, TDEP, PIMD (App. E.8); the template is specified in App. E.19.
- Sources: Public: 2309.10839; 2409.02439; Krein and Nudel'man (1977), to confirm at briefing. Writer only: returns/R8/WP2-v3.md §6;
  returns/R12/WP7-B2-v2.md §4; returns/R16/CW2.md §5–§7.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 11.5 the Markov–Krein band around a silicon cumulative response. Worked example 11.6: the band at
  the peak, ϱ_MK = 2/3 for a Gaussian. Limitbox plots: 11.5a, 11.5b (one panel each), 11.5c (one panel; STYLE's fallback: the band
  around a model distribution with the same first five moments, since no exact anharmonic response is computed).
- Threads: advances T4.

### 11.6 Nuclear-spin and isotope couplings   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat spin-dependent nuclear couplings in the lattice sector and the channel opened by isotope disorder.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the incoherent one-phonon response of a spin-dependent coupling with unpolarized nuclear
  spins; (ii) derive its correction from correlations between nuclear mass and vertex; (iii) derive the isotope-disorder absorption of a

  nucleus feels through its neutron number); isotope-disorder (B − L) absorption; mass-defect local density of states.

  function [derived; classical result, to confirm at briefing; VER].

### 11.7 Anisotropy and daily modulation   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the direction dependence of the electronic and nuclear responses of a crystal and the daily modulation it produces, in
  one place for both sectors.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) expand the response of a cubic crystal in cubic harmonics and show that the first anisotropic
  term is ℓ = 4, for the loss function and for the nuclear response with direction-dependent Debye–Waller exponents; (ii) relate the
  modulation amplitude to the laboratory axes of 1.2 and show why cubic multiphonon responses are nearly isotropic; (iii) explain why a
  crystal that breaks both inversion and time reversal can show odd ℓ; (iv) estimate the daily modulation of sapphire and SiC, where sites are
  not cubic, and the combined electronic and lattice amplitude of silicon and GaAs.

  11.3; W_κ, B_κ → 5.6, 5.3; master rate formula → 8.2; reference appendices → App. C.3.

### Chapter 12. The Migdal effect   [PLANNED; 6 sections, 29,500 words]
Opening: When a nucleus is kicked, its electrons do not follow at once, and a small fraction of nuclear recoils leaves the atom ionized
or the crystal with an electron–hole pair. That fraction, the Migdal effect, matters because electrons are far easier to see at low
energy than the recoil itself, so it carries nuclear-coupling searches to masses well below where the recoil alone is visible. The
question here is how large the effect is in a crystal rather than an isolated atom, and how to compute it without counting the same
energy twice, once in the nuclear response of Chap. 11 and again in the electronic response of Chap. 10. This is the first of the two
mixed sectors of the decomposition theorem; Chap. 13 treats the second, and 17.4 the interference between them.

### 12.1 The joint electron–lattice response   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Define the response that resolves the energy given to the lattice and to the electrons separately, and derive from it every
  form of the Migdal effect.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define the joint response S(q; ω_l, ω_e) of the nuclear density and state its sector content;
  (ii) write its explicit entries at leading non-adiabatic order through the boosted electronic response; (iii) derive the
  no-double-counting rules between it, the multiphonon response and the electronic sector; (iv) state what the formula needs outside the
  gap hypothesis of 8.6.
- Prerequisites: non-adiabatic coupling, Migdal effect, v_N → 6.1; decomposition theorem, sectors → 8.6; multiphonon response → 11.3;
  loss-function rate → 10.2; Born–Oppenheimer approximation → 5.1; nuclear density operator → 8.1.
- Introduces: joint response S(q; ω_l, ω_e) (lattice and electronic energy transfers ω_l, ω_e); boost of the electronic state by the
  recoiling nucleus; electron drag mass δM_κ (named; precise in 12.5).
- Key results: joint response and its explicit leading entries [derived in D.33; REF]; no-double-counting corollaries [derived; REF].
- Sources: Public: Ibe et al. 1707.07258; Knapen, Kozaczuk and Lin 2011.09496. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §1.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 12.1 the (ω_l, ω_e) plane of the joint response for silicon at q = 50 keV. Worked example 12.1:
  the joint response of a single displaced oscillator coupled to one electronic level. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T4.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 12.2 The atomic limit   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Recover the atomic (sudden) Migdal effect and its bound-state version as truncations of the joint response.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the isolated-atom ionization probability with the boost by the factor m_e/m_A from the
  joint response; (ii) state the conditions (free recoil, isolated atom, sudden) and their leading corrections; (iii) derive the excitation
  of bound final states without ionization and why its size estimate is a lower bound; (iv) estimate the probability for xenon, germanium
  and silicon atoms.
- Prerequisites: joint response → 12.1; sudden, 1s form factor → 6.1, 2.2; ionization form factor → 2.2; impulse approximation → 11.4.
- Introduces: atomic Migdal probability; boost mass m_A; bound-state (excitation) Migdal channel.
- Key results: Limit 12.2a: atomic Migdal effect [derived in D.33; cited: Ibe et al. 1707.07258; REF]; Limit 12.2b: Migdal excitation of
  bound final states, a lower bound [derived; cited: 2604.26773 Eqs. (1), (6); REF].
- Sources: Public: 1707.07258; 2604.26773. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §2; returns/R12/WP7-B1-v2.md §8.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 12.2 atomic Migdal spectra of Si and Ge. Worked example 12.2: the ionization probability of a Ge
  atom at E_R = 1 keV. Limitbox plots: 12.2a, 12.2b (one panel each).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 12.6 Bremsstrahlung and Migdal-type absorption   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat photon emission by a recoiling nucleus in a crystal and the absorption of scalars coupled to nucleons through the same

### Chapter 13. Phonon-assisted electron recoil   [PLANNED; 9 sections, 42,000 words]
Opening: Silicon's band gap is indirect: the lowest pair has a crystal momentum near the X point, far more than light dark matter, with
a few keV of momentum, can supply. Below a mass of about 0.4 MeV such dark matter can still create a pair, but only by creating a phonon
with it to carry the missing momentum. The question of this chapter is how large that phonon-assisted rate is, how to compute it without
double-counting the zero-phonon rate of Chap. 10, and what it means for silicon detectors: over which masses it would be the only
electron-recoil channel, and what about the edges of that window is still open. It is the second mixed sector of Chap. 8 and the one
where the book's own derivations carry most weight; its optical limit ties it to the absorption data of 6.5, and the computation it
needs is woven into each section, with the method in Appendix E.

### 13.1 The indirect-gap window   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Show that in an indirect-gap crystal light dark matter can create electron–hole pairs only by also creating a phonon, over a
  mass window below the zero-phonon threshold.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) compare the kinematic reach q ≲ 2m_χv_max with the momentum k_c needed at the gap; (ii) derive the
  zero-phonon threshold mass from E_min(q) and the phonon-assisted onset from E_g(T) − ω_Qν and the phonon momentum; (iii) explain why the
  window exists in Si and Ge and not, at the same masses, in a direct-gap crystal; (iv) say which window edges kinematics alone fixes and
  which need the rates of 13.3 and the excitons with phonons of 13.8 (forward; the numbers and their status are in 13.9).
- Prerequisites: indirect gap, k_c, E_min, phonon-assisted window → 3.3; m_min, kinematic reach → 1.3; kinematic map → 9.2; phonons, ω_Qν →
  5.2; phonon-assisted electron recoil (op.) → 9.1; zero-phonon electronic sector → 8.6.
- Introduces: zero-phonon threshold mass m_min^ZP; phonon-assisted onset mass m_min^PA; the silicon phonon-assisted window (precise
  kinematics).
- Key results: window kinematics [derived; REF]. The computed edges are stated once, in 13.9.
- Sources: Public: Yu and Cardona Ch. 2; Hall, Bardeen and Blatt (1954). Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M1, M3–M4; returns/R16/CW1.md §1;
  returns/R15/COMP-PH.md §0–§2; returns/R15/COMP-PAER.md §4; state/SOTA.md "R17 final state" (window table).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 13.1 E_min(q) of silicon against the reach of 0.3, 0.4 and 0.5 MeV dark matter. Worked example 13.1:
  the phonon-assisted onset from E_g(T → 0) − ω_TA and the TA momentum. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T5, T1.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 13.2 The phonon-assisted amplitude   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the second-order amplitude for creating an electron–hole pair and a phonon, with all its paths, and prove that the band
  sum over intermediate states is unrestricted.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the four paths (phonon emitted by the electron or the hole, before or after the density
  vertex) with their energy denominators; (ii) prove that Pauli-blocked paths return as exchange partners, so the intermediate band sum
  runs over all bands; (iii) show there is no interference with the zero-phonon line at this order; (iv) derive the limit in which the
  amplitude reduces to a derivative of the zero-phonon vertex: the adiabatic (Herzberg–Teller) form, the derivative of the frozen-phonon
  transition density, with error of order ω_Qν/δ_off.
- Prerequisites: second-order amplitudes, energy denominator, time ordering → 2.4; electron–phonon vertex → 6.2; density vertex → 3.2;
  phonon-assisted electron recoil (op.) → 9.1; phonon-dressed electronic sector → 8.6; DFPT, Sternheimer → 5.4; Wannier interpolation of
  g → 6.2; θ_ep, δ_off → 8.5; reference appendices → App. C.4.
- Introduces: phonon-assisted electron recoil (precise); its four-path amplitude; Pauli/exchange theorem (unrestricted band sum);
  absence of zero-phonon interference at second order; adiabatic (Herzberg–Teller) form of the amplitude.
- Key results: four-path amplitude and the exchange theorem [derived in D.35; REF]; no interference with the zero-phonon line at O(g²)
  [derived; REF]; the adiabatic form and its error ω_Qν/δ_off [derived; REF]; new as a dark-matter channel: derived here [NOVELTY N5
  search].
- Computational connection: the adiabatic form is what a frozen-phonon or DFPT calculation evaluates. The density response that DFPT is
  built for needs only the change of the occupied manifold; the transition amplitude also needs the occupied-band part of ∂ψ_v and the
  change ∂ψ_c of the empty states, which a standard DFPT run does not produce, so these are computed separately (App. E.4). The route
  (bands and vertices on coarse grids, Wannier interpolation of g, the window, the f-sum check), its controls and the cost of a silicon
  run are in App. E.5; the full first-principles calculation is specified in App. E.14.
- Sources: Public: Giustino 1603.06965; Noffsinger et al. 1202.3454. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M1–M2, M6, M9, M10; returns/R1/D1.md,
  D2.md; CONTROLS.md C1–C8; returns/R15/COMP-PAER.md §7; state/NOVELTY.md N5.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 13.2 the four paths as diagrams. Worked example 13.2: a two-band, one-phonon model where the
  exchange partners are shown term by term. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T5.
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### 13.3 The phonon-assisted rate   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Assemble the rate and its dielectric form, its zero-phonon limit and its threshold behavior.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the rate with the spin factor and the Bose factors n + 1, n; (ii) write it as a
  phonon-assisted loss function L^PA(q, ω) and its relation to ε₂^PA; (iii) recover the crystal form-factor rate as the zero-phonon limit;
  (iv) derive the onset exponent of the rate near its threshold.
- Prerequisites: four-path amplitude → 13.2; crystal form factor → 10.1; loss-function rate → 10.2; ε₂^PA → 6.5; n_B → 2.5; master rate
  formula → 8.2; reference appendices → App. C.17.
- Introduces: phonon-assisted loss function L^PA(q, ω); onset exponent p_on; phonon-assisted rate dℛ/dω.
- Key results: the rate and its loss-function form [derived in D.35; REF]; zero-phonon limit = crystal form-factor rate [derived; REF];
  onset exponent p_on ≈ 3 in Si (4 at the LA kink) and Ge [derived; REF; new: derived here]. The rate itself is the book's derivation
  (Appendix F row L8, no limitbox): the text says "derived here".
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M3; state/NOVELTY.md N18.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 13.3 the phonon-assisted loss function of silicon near threshold (schematic from the derived
  structure). Worked example 13.3: the onset exponent from the joint density of states. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T5.

### 13.4 Screening of the phonon-assisted rate   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Screen the phonon-assisted amplitude correctly, including the electron–hole pair channel and external local fields.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) show that the vertex g inside is the screened density-functional vertex and that one external
  1/ε(q, ω) remains; (ii) derive the pair-channel screening W^scr(P, ω_f) of the amplitude; (iii) derive L^PA = ε₂^PA/(ε₁⁰)² + O(g⁴)
  where ε₂⁰ = 0; (iv) explain the extra source term of an adiabatic local-density kernel.
- Prerequisites: phonon-assisted loss function → 13.3; screening for every coupling, Coulomb-line classes → 8.4; W^scr → 4.8; screened
  vertex → 6.2; TDDFT → 4.6.
- Introduces: pair-channel screening of the phonon-assisted amplitude; external local fields in the window.
- Key results: screened rate [derived in D.36 at random-phase level; REF; new: derived here]; L^PA = ε₂^PA/(ε₁⁰)² [derived; REF].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M7; returns/R3/X2.md; returns/R8/WP0-v3.md (5.5)–(5.7′); returns/R16/CW1.md §18–§21; state/NOVELTY.md N5.

### 13.5 The optical limit   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Recover phonon-assisted optical absorption as the q → 0 limit, and use it to calibrate and test the theory against data.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) take q → 0 of the rate and recover ε₂^PA, whose Hall–Bardeen–Blatt edge was derived in 6.5 (D.18)
  and is cited, not re-derived; (ii) show that ε^L = ε^T in this limit; (iii) transfer measured absorption data from 77 or 300 K to
  millikelvin by the inverse branch transfer, explain why it is ill-posed without branch resolution, and size the error of using the 77 K
  data untransferred; (iv) state what the optical limit does not test (finite q, Umklapp).
- Prerequisites: phonon-assisted loss function → 13.3; Hall–Bardeen–Blatt, ε₂^PA → 6.5; ε^T → 4.4; n_B → 2.5; absorption on electrons
  → 10.7.
- Introduces: optical limit of the phonon-assisted rate; temperature transfer of absorption data.
- Key results: Limit 13.5a: phonon-assisted absorption as the q → 0 limit [derived; cited: Hall, Bardeen and Blatt (1954); Noffsinger et
  al. 1202.3454; REF]; inverse temperature transfer, exact for branch-resolved data [derived; VER]; the uncorrected 77 K data of
  1608.01994 overestimate the millikelvin dark-photon rate by more than 2 within ≈ 20 meV of the phonon-assisted edge (×2.6, 1.9, 1.5,
  1.3 at 15, 25, 50, 100 meV) [derived; VER; the home of this result, to which 10.7 points].
- Sources: Public: 1202.3454; Macfarlane et al. (1958). Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md §4 (L9); returns/R16/CW1.md §23.

### 13.6 The off-shell window   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the regions where the second-order expansion is singular: soft acoustic phonons, resonant intermediate states, and the
  boundary of the window in which the phonon-assisted rate is defined separately from the zero-phonon line.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the soft acoustic limit of the amplitude from the Ward identity of 8.3; (ii) show that the
  rate is separately computable only where every first-order transition at the same (q, ω) is off shell, and state the pointwise window
  ω ≤ E_min^ren(q, T) − δ_w; (iii) regularize resonant denominators with widths and the finite part; (iv) apply the Dyson convention
  and the Fano term of 8.6 outside the window, by reference, and size the Fano term near a resonance.
- Prerequisites: four-path amplitude → 13.2; translation Ward identities, soft acoustic limit → 8.3; Dyson convention → 8.6; finite part,
  widths → 2.4; ε^ren → 6.3; reference appendices → App. C.4.
- Introduces: off-shell window of the phonon-assisted rate; resonant region; pointwise window bound E_min^ren(q, T) − δ_w, with δ_w the
  off-shell margin of the window.
- Key results: soft acoustic limit [derived in D.37; REF]; window theorem [derived; REF]; resonance regularization [derived; REF].
- Computational connection: the width-to-detuning map of the Fano term in GaAs is specified in App. E.14.
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md M4, M8; returns/R3/X1.md; returns/R5/O4.md; returns/R16/CW3.md (Fano kernel).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 13.6 the off-shell window in the (q, ω) plane for silicon. Worked example 13.6: the soft-phonon
  amplitude to first order in Q. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T4.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 13.7 Temperature and zero-point renormalization   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the zero-phonon sector at finite temperature and zero point, and the special-displacement method as a deterministic
  quadrature of the thermal average.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the zero-phonon response with renormalized bands; (ii) show that the special-displacement
  method reproduces the thermal average through alternating-sign displacements (the method itself is in App. E.8); (iii) bound the
  non-adiabatic correction to the silicon gap renormalization (8 meV, Poncé et al. 1504.05992 Table VI) and its effect on the window (≤ 0.002 MeV); (iv) state the
  temperature dependence of the onset.
- Prerequisites: ZPR, AHC, special displacements → 6.3; phonon-assisted rate → 13.3; zero-phonon electronic sector → 8.6; n_B → 2.5.
- Introduces: renormalized zero-phonon response; special displacements as a quadrature; non-adiabatic ZPR correction.
- Key results: Limit 13.7a: zero-point renormalization and special displacements [derived; cited: Zacharias and Giustino 1604.02394;
  Allen and Cardona (1981); REF]; non-adiabatic ZPR bound for silicon [derived estimate; EST; Si ZPR from Poncé et al. 2011.12765].
- Computational connection: special displacements in practice, their supercells and convergence (App. E.8).
- Sources: Public: 1604.02394; 2011.12765. Writer only: returns/R8/WP3-v3.md §5; returns/R16/CW1.md §25.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 13.7 the silicon gap against T with the window edges. Worked example 13.7: the window shift between
  10 mK and 300 K. Limitbox plots: 13.7a (one panel).
- Threads: advances T7.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 13.9 The silicon window   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: State what is established about the silicon window, what would be seen at threshold, and what remains open for silicon,
  germanium and GaAs.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) give the established edges: the first zero-phonon edge 0.405–0.408 MeV (best 0.407 with
  spin–orbit; the 1s exciton line 0.402–0.405 MeV), off the valley axis in a {001} mirror plane, the second (mirror-even) heavy-hole edge
  from 0.415–0.420 MeV, and the opening of the allowed (2̄00)-class zero-phonon line at ≈ 0.50 MeV, with their ordering; (ii) explain the
  selection rule (glide parity) that makes the first zero-phonon line second class, using the diamond space group (point and space groups
  are recapped in App. C.17); (iii) state, conditionally on the open onset and on rates known only to a factor of ten (fifteen at 0.36 MeV, where the reach is the band 3e-30–3e-29 cm² between the 0.5 K fit and the 20 K data), the masses over
  which the phonon-assisted rate would be the only electron-recoil channel; (iv) list what is open: the onset, the lead of the
  phonon-assisted rate, the two-channel band, the zero-phonon lead, the rate and reach, germanium and the GaAs check.
- Prerequisites: window kinematics, m_min^ZP, m_min^PA → 13.1; phonon-assisted rate → 13.3; temperature and ZPR → 13.7; Umklapp, basis
  structure factor → 3.1; exciton–phonon replicas → 13.8; reference appendices → App. C.17.
- Introduces: glide-parity selection rule of the silicon zero-phonon vertex; allowed and second-class zero-phonon lines; two-channel band.
- Key results: the established rows of Table 13.1 [computed; VER: the zero-phonon edges, the selection rule (derived, checked by
  calculation) and the ordering]; spin–orbit lift of the on-axis vertex 3.6×10⁻⁶ (Γ₈ sum) [computed; VER]; the allowed Umklapp (2̄00) in

### Chapter 14. Magnetic excitations   [PLANNED; 6 sections, 27,000 words]
Opening: In a magnet the electron spins are ordered, and their collective excitations, magnons, have gaps of a meV or less. A dark
particle that couples to electron spin rather than charge sees almost nothing in silicon, but it can create magnons in an
antiferromagnet such as NiO, and a bosonic field with a spin coupling can be absorbed by the uniform precession of a ferromagnet. This
chapter asks which magnetic targets respond to which couplings, how far measured neutron data can stand in for computed responses, and
when an ultralight field must be treated as a coherent classical wave rather than as particles. It builds on the spin waves of 7.1;
superconductors, the other meV-gap targets, follow in Chap. 15. The chapter summary collects the magnet inputs and the size of the
1/|𝐒| corrections in one table.
Writer note: the summary carries Table 14.1 (ex-section 14.7, merged by review item S17). As written (Ch14 ruling R8), the Summary (\section*{Summary}, no numbered section), the nuisance table tab:14-nuisances and the inputs table tab:14-inputs are in tex/ch14/s14-summary.tex, which ch14.tex inputs last, after 14.6.

### 14.1 Magnon scattering   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the rate of single-magnon emission by a spin-coupled dark particle from the spin-density response at linear spin-wave
  order.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the one-magnon rate from the spin row of the master formula and the Holstein–Primakoff
  expansion; (ii) include the magnetic form factor and the Bogoliubov coefficients of an antiferromagnet; (iii) derive the ratio of
  longitudinal to transverse weights W_∥/W_⊥ = ΔS(1 + ΔS)/(|𝐒|(1 + 2ΔS)), with |𝐒| the spin length, and the zero-point spin reduction
  ΔS; (iv) estimate the NiO rate near its gap.
- Prerequisites: LSWT, magnon, Holstein–Primakoff, Bogoliubov transformation, F^mag, s_e(q) → 7.1; spin-dependent couplings, target
  operators per species → 8.1; master rate formula → 8.2; reference appendices → App. E.9, C.19.
- Introduces: one-magnon rate; longitudinal and transverse spin weights W_∥, W_⊥; zero-point spin reduction ΔS.
- Key results: Limit 14.1a: magnon rate at linear spin-wave order [derived in D.38, which starts from the two-sublattice result of D.19;
  cited: Trickle, Zhang and Zurek 1905.13744; 2009.13534; REF]; W_∥/W_⊥ at O(|𝐒|⁰) with the transverse sum-rule defect ΔS/|𝐒| as a lower
  estimate of the one-magnon defect [derived; VER].
- Computational connection: exchange constants and anisotropies from DFT + U (App. E.9); specified for the magnet survivors in App. E.20.
- Sources: Public: 1905.13744; 2009.13534; Hutchings and Samuelsen (1972). Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §1–§2.1; returns/R16/CW7.md §7
  (magnon row).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 14.1 the NiO one-magnon rate against m_χ. Worked example 14.1: ΔS and W_∥/W_⊥ for NiO.
  Limitbox plots: 14.1a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

  interactions add and why bound states appear in anisotropic magnets; (iii) derive the magnon–phonon hybridization gap and the mixed
  response; (iv) say when a hybrid mode dominates the absorption.

### 14.3 Absorption into magnons   [status: ACCEPTED]

### 14.4 Spin responses from neutron data   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Show what measured neutron scattering can and cannot supply for dark-matter spin responses.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) relate the neutron cross-section to the transverse projection of the spin response (the magnetic
  cross-section is recapped in App. C.19); (ii) show that no neutron route, polarized or not, gives q̂·S·q̂ for a general target, as a
  consequence of the standard perpendicular-projection rule; (iii) state the isotropy hypothesis under which a two-angle inversion
  recovers the longitudinal part; (iv) evaluate the quenched-ion ratio [λ_S/(g_ion/2)]² ≈ 0.83 for Ni²⁺, with the spectroscopic g_ion, that converts neutron data into the
  dark-matter spin response.
- Prerequisites: one-magnon rate → 14.1; spin-density, magnetic form factor → 7.1; positivity → 8.3; Landé factor g_J → 14.3;
  reference appendices → App. C.19.
- Introduces: data-driven spin response; transverse projector P_T in the neutron cross-section; isotropy hypothesis.

  absence of a neutron route to the longitudinal part, which follows from the textbook perpendicular projection (Lovesey) and is

### 14.5 Chiral and nonreciprocal targets   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat responses that are odd under parity or time reversal: optical activity, circular dichroism, chiral phonons, single-molecule
  magnets and multiferroics.

  response; (ii) show which point groups allow a q-odd part (point groups, characters and polar and axial irreps are recapped in
  App. C.17); (iii) write the one-phonon response of a chiral phonon with angular momentum and the metastable line of a single-molecule
  magnet; (iv) derive the cross response of a multiferroic below its ordering temperature T_CM and say why it is open near it.
- Prerequisites: odd-ℓ terms in inversion-broken targets → 11.7; minimal response table → 10.4; one-magnon rate → 14.1; single-phonon
  nuclear response → 11.1; Kramers–Kronig → 4.3; reference appendices → App. C.17.
- Introduces: chirality and circular dichroism (precise); finite-q optical activity; chiral phonons; single-molecule magnets;
  magnetoelectric (multiferroic) cross response; ordering temperature T_CM of a multiferroic.
- Key results: optical activity and circular dichroism at finite q [derived; REF]; Limit 14.5a: chiral phonons (panel 1) and
  single-molecule magnets (panel 2) [derived; cited: 2301.07617 Eqs. (6)–(7); 2511.20461 Eqs. (3)–(4), (11)–(12); 2601.01043 Eqs. (3),

  returns/R12/WP7-D-v2.md §1.9.

### 14.6 Coherent fields and precessing nuclear spins   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the regime in which ultralight dark matter acts as a classical field on a coherent target, and derive the nuclear-spin
  precession signal and its statistics.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) show when a Gaussian classical field replaces the golden rule and where the signal weight sits
  (k = −m_φv); (ii) derive the transient and steady-state transverse magnetization of a polarized nuclear-spin ensemble and its Rabi
  frequency, from the Bloch equations; (iii) derive the exponential-bin likelihood (likelihoods are recapped in App. C.14) and the
  scalings t^{−1/2} and (t t_coh)^{−1/4}; (iv) state the fixed-amplitude penalty and why it must not be applied twice.
- Prerequisites: master-formula conditions, Gaussian field average → 8.2; nuclear spin density → 8.1; absorption operators → 8.1;
  Lorentzian, widths → 2.4; Poisson → 1.5; nuisance parameter → 8.8; reference appendices → App. C.14.
- Introduces: Bloch equations and nuclear magnetic resonance (recap: precession, T₁ and T₂, polarization of a spin ensemble); coherent
  classical-field regime; coherence time t_coh; nuclear-spin precession signal; exponential-bin likelihood.
- Key results: Limit 14.6a: transverse nuclear magnetization [derived; cited: Budker et al. 1306.6089 Eq. (1); 2101.01241 Eq. (1); REF;
  1306.6089's verbal gloss misstates the shift by 2, amplitude correct, VER]; Limit 14.6b: Gaussian-field signal and its power spectrum
  [derived; cited: Foster, Rodd and Safdi 1711.10489 Eqs. (8), (10), (24); REF]; Limit 14.6c: statistics and scalings [derived; cited:
  1306.6089 Eqs. (8)–(9); 1711.10489 Eqs. (54), (63), (69); 1905.13650; REF].
- Computational connection: the coherent-regime material inputs (T₂, polarization, line widths of candidate targets) are specified in
  App. E.26.
- Sources: Public: 1306.6089; 2101.01241; 1711.10489; 1905.13650. Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-D-v2.md §1.1–§1.6; returns/R16/CW3.md §4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 14.6 the signal power spectrum against t_coh. Worked example 14.6: the scaling crossover for a
  1 neV axion-like field. Limitbox plots: 14.6a, 14.6b (one panel each), 14.6c (one panel: the sensitivity of the fixed-amplitude
  and Rayleigh-amplitude likelihoods against t/t_coh, with the two power laws and the crossover marked).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### Chapter 15. Superconductors   [PLANNED; 4 sections, 18,500 words]
Opening: A superconductor's gap is a thousand times smaller than silicon's. In aluminium the paired electrons are bound by about
0.18 meV each, so breaking a pair costs about 0.36 meV, and dark matter of keV mass can do it. The question of this chapter is whether
the rate of breaking pairs can be computed as reliably as the semiconductor rates of Chap. 10, given that the BCS response must conserve
charge, that the edge of the gap needs a resummation, and that real sensors are thin, disordered films. It applies the conserving
response of 7.2 through the master formula of Chap. 8; the quasiparticles it creates are counted in 20.4. The chapter summary collects
the superconducting inputs in one table.
Writer note: the summary carries Table 15.2 (ex-section 15.5, merged by review item S17).

### 15.1 Pair breaking by dark matter   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the scattering rate of a superconductor from the conserving BCS density response.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the rate from the master formula with the conserving response of 7.2; (ii) reduce it to the
  bare quasiparticle bubble with coherence factors, then to the Lindhard limit, and state where each truncation fails; (iii) show that the
  first BCS correction to the loss function at fixed q, −2(Δ_BCS/qv_F)𝔰_BCS 𝔤_I L^nor, with 𝔤_I the case-I coherence integral and L^nor
  the normal-state loss function, describes the bare bubble only (the pair-field ladder reverses the shift of Re ε near the edge), and
  reaches 17% at y = 5 on the kinematic edge; (iv) evaluate the aluminium rate near 2Δ_BCS with the bulk gap Δ_BCS = 0.18 meV.
- Prerequisites: BCS, Δ_BCS, coherence factors, conserving response, Nambu → 7.2; Lindhard function → 4.5; loss-function rate → 10.2;
  master rate formula → 8.2; pair-breaking threshold → 7.2.
- Introduces: superconductor rate (conserving form); generalized RPA (conserving) vertex; first-order BCS correction to Re ε; edge
  variable y = ω/Δ_BCS; kinematic edge q = ω/v_max (distinguished from the pair-breaking edge ω = 2Δ_BCS); vertex fraction 𝔳; Anderson–Bogoliubov energy Ω_AB at finite q.
- Key results: Limit 15.1a: superconductor rate, conserving response → bare bubble → Lindhard [derived; cited: Hochberg et al. 1512.04533;
  2101.08263; 2609.06423; REF; the case-I closed form as quoted by Hochberg, Lin and Zurek from Allen, Phys. Rev. B 3, 305 (1971); the
  phase term is credited to 2609.06423]; the conserving response in closed form through one pair function F_μ [derived; the algebra and
  the quadrature in D.39]; on the aluminium kinematic edge, within 10% of the conserving loss function: Lindhard for y ≥ 7.9, 𝔤_I L^nor for
  y ≥ 2.25, the bare bubble for y ≥ 9.1 [computed, clean, v_F = 6.75×10⁻³]; first-order correction of the bare bubble 17% at y = 5
  [derived; REF]; the bulk aluminium gap Δ_BCS = 0.18 meV, the BCS value 1.764 T_c that tunnelling measurements confirm to a few
  percent, is settled and stated as established [REF]; the 0.3 meV used by 2109.04473 is Δ in the film convention (half the film gap),
  labelled as such; the rate at 1 keV changes by ×1.23 between the two.
- Computational connection: the conserving BCS response of aluminium on a full q, ω grid is specified in App. E.20.
- Sources: Public: 1512.04533; 2101.08263; 2609.06423; Tinkham Ch. 3. Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §4.1–§4.4; returns/R16/CW4.md §11;
  CONVENTIONS.md §13 row 33; state/NOVELTY.md N10.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 15.1 the aluminium loss function near 2Δ_BCS. Worked example 15.1: the aluminium rate at
  m_χ = 1 keV, with the change from a thin-film gap to the bulk gap. Limitbox plots: 15.1a (one panel).
- Threads: advances T3.
- Budget (words): 5,500.

### 15.2 Screening at the kinematic edge   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Give the resummed loss function that replaces the first-order correction at the edge.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the resummed bare loss function L^bare = 𝔤_I L^nor(1 + 𝔯_ε²)/[(1 + δ_𝔰)² + 𝔤_I²𝔯_ε²] in
  clean quasiclassical BCS by Kramers–Kronig of δIm ε, and the same form for the conserving response; (ii) evaluate it for aluminium: the
  conserving (physical) edge values 0.87/1.00/1.07/1.07/1.02 × 𝔤_I L^nor at y = 2.2/2.5/3/5/10 (DC-41; clean values, which hold at y ≤ 2.5
  only for 𝔩_e ≳ 10 μm), against the resummed bare bubble, 0.33–0.40 at y = 2.2 to 0.94–0.96 at y = 10, which violates charge
  conservation; (iii) state the two-sided edge remainder, −2% to +9% about 𝔤_I L^nor for y ≥ 3, and why energies y < 3 are excluded (there
  the conserving edge value varies by more than 10% over 𝔩_e = 30 nm–100 μm); (iv) list its corrections O(Δ²/q²v_F²), all in the ladder.
- Prerequisites: superconductor rate, first-order BCS correction → 15.1; Kramers–Kronig → 4.3; nuisance types → 8.8.
- Introduces: resummed edge loss function (bare and conserving); Lindhard bracket 𝔣_L(𝔴); two-sided edge remainder; the nuisance set of
  the superconducting sector (gap, film relaxation rate, edge remainder), collected in Table 15.2 of the chapter summary.
- Key results: Limit 15.2a: the resummed loss function on the kinematic edge [derived in D.39; cited: 2609.06423 Eqs. (8)–(9), the bare
  BCS ε evaluated numerically, the closed form is the book's; VER]; the conserving edge values with their mean-free-path range [computed;
  DC-41]; the superconducting nuisances and their sizes [derived; REF].
- Sources: Public: 2609.06423. Writer only: returns/R16/CW4.md §8; returns/R16/CW7.md (one-sided rows); state/DEFERRED-COMPUTE.md (DC-41,
  DC-42, DC-50).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 15.1 edge loss functions of aluminium against y (bare and conserving, clean and with impurities).
  Worked example 15.2: the edge suppression at y = 3.
  Limitbox plots: 15.2a (one panel).
- Threads: advances T7.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 15.3 Films and disorder   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the film geometry and disorder of real superconducting sensors, with relaxation-time models of the response.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the film loss function with relaxation rate γ_rel (Drude and Mermin forms) and their
  conservation properties; (ii) explain the strong disorder of amorphous WSi, k_F𝔩_e of a few (1.8–5.5 from its measured transport), with
  𝔩_e the electron mean free path, and why mean-field BCS fails there; (iii) state the branch-cut and causality requirement of the superconducting ε; (iv) say which film
  properties a rate needs.
- Prerequisites: Mermin function, γ_rel → 4.5; superconductor rate → 15.1; resummed edge loss function → 15.2; analyticity → 4.1.
- Introduces: electron mean free path 𝔩_e, the dirty limit and Anderson's theorem (recap, one paragraph: nonmagnetic disorder leaves the
  BCS gap unchanged at weak disorder); film loss function; disordered superconductor; gap inhomogeneity.
- Key results: Drude and Mermin film forms [derived; cited: 2101.08263; REF]; branch cut and causality [derived; REF]; the conserving edge
  response with Born impurities, its single normalization and its local Mattis–Bardeen limit (the mean-free-path passage, one home) [computed;
  DC-41]; sheet screening of a film thinner than 1/q, the q_∥d ≪ 1 limit of Lasenby–Prabhu 2110.01587 Eq. (19) (540 at leading order, 588
  exact slab, for 50 nm Al) [derived; REF].
- Computational connection: the amorphous WSi response (molecular dynamics, Kubo–Greenwood and RPA, gap inhomogeneity) is specified in
  App. E.20.
- Sources: Public: 2101.08263; Mermin (1970). Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §4.4–§4.5; returns/R16/CW4.md §4–§5.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 15.2 film and bulk loss functions of aluminium. Worked example 15.3: the Mermin film function of a
  50 nm Al film. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the disorder-averaged response of amorphous WSi, which mean-field BCS cannot give (LE-98, remainder;
  App. E.20).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 15.4 Absorption in superconductors and metals   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive absorption of dark photons and axion-like particles by superconducting and normal metals, including the phonon-assisted
  (Holstein) part.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Mattis–Bardeen absorption from the conserving current response; (ii) derive the
  Holstein absorption at second order in Nambu space, with the amplitude −ê·(v_{k+Q} − v_k)g(u′v + v′u)/ω (case I); (iii) show that it
  recovers Allen's result; (iv) state the strong-coupling corrections and what remains open in them; (v) derive the direct
  pair breaking by the uniform spin current of a clean singlet superconductor and state the conditions on its use.
- Prerequisites: Mattis–Bardeen, conserving response, coherence factors → 7.2; α²F, λ_ep → 6.4; absorption on electrons → 10.7;
  superconductor rate → 15.1; electron–phonon vertex → 6.2.
- Introduces: Mattis–Bardeen absorption (precise); Holstein (phonon-assisted) absorption in superconductors; transport Eliashberg function
  α²F_tr; spin-current absorption (direct pair breaking by the spin current).
- Key results: Limit 15.4a: Holstein and Mattis–Bardeen absorption, the second-order Nambu derivation recovering Allen (1971) [derived in
  D.40; cited: 1604.06800 Eqs. (8), (13), (16), (17), (19)–(20); Allen (1971) as cited there, closed access; REF, the Nambu derivation VER];
  spin-current absorption: the uniform spin current of a singlet superconductor is η-type in Nambu space and breaks pairs at q = 0,
  σ₁^𝒥 = 2πω_p²Δ²/[ω²(ω² − 4Δ²)^{1/2}], with the integrated weight (π/2)ω_p² fixed by the exact spin f-sum (zero spin stiffness of the
  singlet) [derived in D.40; checked independently; VER with caveats]; published projections take the spin-current response equal to the
  current response and leave the channel out; for clean aluminium at 0.72 meV three events per kilogram-year need g_aee = 5.1×10⁻¹³
  against 5.6×10⁻⁹ with the phonon-assisted σ₁, with three conditions stated in the text: (i) clean only for 𝔩_e ≫ ξ_coh (1.6–2.4 μm, bulk
  high-purity crystals); (ii) the 10⁴ gain is over phonon-only clean-aluminium projections only (a 50 nm film gives 1.5×10⁻¹² against
  2.0×10⁻¹² from its own Mattis–Bardeen σ₁); (iii) the line shape depends on F₁ᵃ, a triplet p-wave interaction and strong coupling (×1.2 in
  g_aee), the integrated weight does not [computed; no novelty wording]; strong coupling: the normal-metal factor 1/(1 + λ_tr)² = 0.52 is
  known (Allen 2015 Eq. (3)), the superconducting case is open.
- Computational connection: Eliashberg spectral functions from first principles (App. E.9); specified in App. E.20.
- Sources: Public: 1604.06800; Tinkham Ch. 3. Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-B1-v2.md §9; returns/R16/CW4.md §12.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 15.3 Mattis–Bardeen and Holstein parts of the aluminium σ₁. Worked example 15.4: the Holstein
  onset in aluminium. Limitbox plots: 15.4a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): which renormalization factors survive in strong-coupling (Eliashberg) Holstein absorption (LE-108,
  remainder; App. E.20); the coherence factor of phonon-assisted spin-current absorption near the edge (XG-15); scalar absorption near
  the edge, which couples to the amplitude channel of the pair field (XG-16).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### Chapter 16. Novel targets   [PLANNED; 9 sections, 38,500 words]
Opening: Bulk semiconductors, magnets and superconductors do not exhaust the solids a detector can be built from. Narrow-gap Dirac
materials reach lighter masses than silicon; layered and low-dimensional systems change how charge is screened; molecular crystals carry a
strong direction dependence; and dopants, color centers and glasses offer final states that a sensor can read one at a time. This chapter
asks which of these targets add a channel the bulk crystals lack, and what limits the theory of each, which in most cases is the
material input rather than the framework. It applies Chaps. 10–15 to them; the tunnelling defects of glasses, treated at the end, also
shape the delayed signals of 19.6.

### 16.1 Dirac-material rates   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive scattering and absorption rates of Dirac materials with the background dielectric constant placed correctly.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) obtain the Dirac-material rate from the master formula with the RPA polarization of 7.3 and ε_bg
  in the total ε; (ii) show that leaving the background dielectric constant out of the total ε overstates scattering reaches by ε_bg² and
  absorption reaches by ε_bg, and re-plot a limit; (iii) state the validity (weak screening, α̃ small) and what replaces it at α̃ ≳ 1;
  (iv) evaluate the ZrTe₅ rate with Δ_D as the half-gap (central Δ_D = 11.8 meV with the whole measured-structure row of 1910.02091
  Table 1; the other computed rows give the range).
- Prerequisites: Dirac Hamiltonian, v_D, Δ_D, ε_bg, α̃ → 7.3; loss-function rate → 10.2; absorption on electrons → 10.7; screening for every
  coupling → 8.4.
- Introduces: Dirac-material rate with background screening; reach re-plot factors ε_bg², √ε_bg.
- Key results: Limit 16.1a: Dirac-material rate, ε_bg in the total ε; reaches of 1708.08929 and 1910.02091 too strong by ε_bg² (scattering)
  and ε_bg (absorption) [derived in D.41; cited: Hochberg et al. 1708.08929; Geilhufe, Kahlhoefer and Winkler 1910.02091; REF; no
  erratum or comment found]; 1910.02091 Eq. (3.14) typo [VER; one sentence, the detail in the Appendix F row].
- Computational connection: the anisotropic ZrTe₅ parameters and the Dirac re-plots are specified in App. E.20.
- Sources: Public: 1708.08929; 1910.02091; Armitage, Mele and Vishwanath (2018). Writer only: returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §5; returns/R16/CW4.md
  §6, §9; state/NOVELTY.md N14.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 16.1 ZrTe₅ reach as published and corrected. Worked example 16.1: the factor ε_bg² for ε_bg = 40.
  Limitbox plots: 16.1a (one panel).
- Threads: advances T2.
- Open problems (exact gaps): Dirac targets at α̃ ≳ 1, beyond one-loop RPA (LE-102).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 16.5 Dopants   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat dopants as targets of scattering and absorption and as final states of energy deposits.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) start from the effective-mass donor of 7.4 (D.23), with its valleys and central cell, and derive
  only its response to the dark-matter operators; (ii) derive dopant absorption in an effective-medium host, with screening counted once;
  (iii) state where the impurity band (𝓃_D/𝓃_c ≈ 0.3–0.6, with 𝓃_c the Mott critical density) invalidates the isolated-donor picture;
  (iv) say when a dopant is a useful final state.
- Prerequisites: donor, E_b^D, central-cell, 𝓃_D → 7.4; absorption on electrons → 10.7; screening → 4.4; master rate formula → 8.2.

  gives 1.47/0.91/0.59/0.56 of the correct scattering rate at 0.02/0.1/1/10 MeV (heavy mediator; absorption unaffected), computed, VER:
  one sentence, the detail in the Appendix F row; its local-field factor (E_eff/E₀)² = 4.84 gives the continuum alone an oscillator
  strength 4.85, above the 3.85 the f-sum allows the whole donor, VER]; dopants as final states
  [derived; REF].
- Computational connection: the disorder-averaged donor responses are specified in App. E.21.
- Sources: Public: 2212.04504; Kohn and Luttinger (1955). Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-B1-v2.md §10; returns/R8/WP4-v3.md §6; returns/R16/CW4.md
  §13.

### 16.6 Color centers and broken bonds   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the creation of color centers and the breaking of bonds by dark-matter recoils as detectable final states.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the color-center creation rate by seeding the defect yield from the nuclear response
  (seeding is made precise in 18.3, forward; the defect yields of a recoil are treated in 19.4); (ii) write bond breaking in molecular
  solids with the cage-escape probability; (iii) state which yields (smooth threshold, survival, charge state) are measured and which are
  not; (iv) say what a single-center readout at mK requires.

  final-state-resolved response → 8.7.
- Introduces: color-center creation rate; bond-breaking channel; cage escape.
- Key results: Limit 16.6a: color-center creation (panel 1) and bond breaking (panel 2) [derived; cited: 1705.03016 Eqs. (1)–(2),
  (5)–(7); 1608.02940 Eqs. (3.3), (3.8), (3.11), (3.12), (3.17), (4.3), (4.5) (arXiv numbering); REF].
- Computational connection: excited states of color centers in supercells (App. E.10); NV⁻ transition densities, ionic color centers
  and bond breaking are specified in App. E.21.

  nuclear recoil. Limitbox plots: 16.6a (two panels).

  escape and cross-section yields (LE-151).
