### 16.7 Glasses, random media and two-level systems   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat amorphous targets: tunnelling two-level systems as targets of scattering and absorption, dark-photon absorption in
  glasses, and scattering off a random medium.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the standard tunnelling model and the two-level response driven by strain and electric
  field; (ii) derive the dark-photon absorption rate ∝ ζ²tanδ₀tanh(m_φ/2T)/ε_r; (iii) derive dark-matter scattering on two-level systems
  in a-SiO₂ and its core term; (iv) derive glass absorption below the Ioffe–Regel frequency (where the phonon mean free path falls to one
  wavelength), C^IR = A^IR + B^IRω²; (v) state when the Born approximation fails for coherent scattering off a whole medium, with
  dark-matter–nucleus cross-section σ_χN ≳ m_χ⁴v⁴/(4π𝓃₀²).
- Prerequisites: absorption on electrons → 10.7; single-phonon absorption → 11.8; spin and nuclear couplings → 8.1; Born approximation →
  2.2; Lorentzian → 2.4; tanh from detailed balance → 2.5; reference appendices → App. C.20.
- Introduces: tunnelling two-level system (TLS) and the standard tunnelling model; loss tangent tanδ₀ and relative permittivity ε_r of
  the glass; Ioffe–Regel frequency; TLS scattering rate; glass infrared coefficient C^IR; Born validity for a random medium (σ_crit).
- Key results: Limit 16.7a: TLS response to classical strain and field [derived; cited: 1705.01108 Eq. (20); 2103.06977 Eqs. (4), (12),
  (13), (15); REF]; Limit 16.7b: dark-photon absorption on TLS [derived; cited: 1705.01108 Eq. (20); REF; new as a dark-photon channel:
  derived here]; TLS scattering of dark matter, a-SiO₂ ≈ 2.3×10⁻² per kg, day and meV at 1 MeV and σ̄_e = 10⁻³⁸ cm² with the longitudinal
  tunneling strength C_l = 1.9×10⁻⁴ (3.1×10⁻² with the polarization-averaged C = 2.6×10⁻⁴) [EST, ×3; new: derived here]; Limit 16.7c: random media and the Born limit [derived; cited: 2512.16451 Eqs. (2), (9)–(12), (14); REF; the published reach
  4×10⁻³⁸ cm² lies at V_opt/E = 1.86, outside Born validity, |V_opt|/E < 0.092]; Limit 16.7d: glass
  absorption [derived; cited: 2603.22390 Eqs. (2)–(3); prior art cond-mat/0604205 Eq. (1); REF].
- Computational connection: glass models and scattering off random media are specified in App. E.19; the TLS core vertices and
  ensemble sampling in App. E.22.
- Sources: Public: 1705.01108; 2103.06977; 2512.16451; 2603.22390; Phillips (1987). Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-T-v2.md §1–§2;
  returns/R12/WP7-B2-v2.md §2.3, §3; returns/R16/CW2.md §17, §22; returns/R16/CW5.md (tanδ₀ values); state/NOVELTY.md N9, N11.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 16.7 the TLS density of states and the absorption line. Worked example 16.7: the dark-photon rate
  on thermal SiO₂ with tanδ₀ = 3×10⁻⁴. Limitbox plots: 16.7a, 16.7b, 16.7c, 16.7d (one panel each).
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the TLS parameters P̄, the deformation potential and the dipole moment of specific films, where the
  universality of the standard tunnelling model is only assumed (LE-130).
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### 16.8 Two-level systems at millikelvin   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the quantum kinetics of tunnelling two-level systems at millikelvin, which set heat release, delayed signals and
  backgrounds in glasses and films.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive two-level-system relaxation by one phonon (Jäckle) and by conduction electrons (Black),
  with the superconducting factor 2/(1 + e^{Δ_BCS/T}) of Meißner et al. times sinh(u)K₁(u), u = Δ_tl/2T (D.42), and the ohmic
  coupling α_ohm = 4K_b; (ii) derive the ohmic scaling limit and its
  anti-Arrhenius law (the spin–boson model and its ohmic bath are recapped in App. C.20); (iii) derive thermally activated hopping and
  its crossover to tunnelling; (iv) derive the heat release of a glass cooled from T_hi to T_lo, (π²/24)P̄V(T_hi² − T_lo²)/t.
- Prerequisites: TLS, standard tunnelling model → 16.7; golden rule → 2.1; BCS, Δ_BCS → 7.2; phonons → 5.3; detailed balance → 2.5;
  reference appendices → App. C.20.
- Introduces: TLS relaxation rates; ohmic coupling α_ohm; heat release of a glass.
- Key results: Limit 16.8a: TLS relaxation by phonons and electrons (panel 1) and the ohmic coupling α_ohm = 4K_b in the Born limit
  (panel 2) [derived in D.42; cited: 2103.06977 Eqs. (7)–(9); 1610.03974 Eqs. (4)–(5); 2009.03804 Eq. (4); REF, the Born-limit coupling
  VER; the quasiparticle golden rule multiplies the published superconducting factor of 2103.06977 Eq. (9) by sinh(u)K₁(u), 0.86 at
  Δ_tl = T and 0.71 at 2T]; Limit 16.8b: ohmic scaling limit [derived; cited: cond-mat/9409105 Eqs. (8)–(10); REF; its small-coupling
  form differs from the scaling limit by Γ(1 + α_ohm)/Γ(1 − α_ohm), 0.71 at α_ohm = 0.28]; Limit 16.8c: hopping and tunnelling
  [derived; cited: 2409.02820 Eqs. (1)–(4); REF]; Limit 16.8d: heat release [derived; cited: 1610.03974 Eqs. (1), (6)–(8); REF].
- Computational connection: α_ohm from first principles (App. E.10, E.11); the ohmic couplings and the TLS ensemble sampling are
  specified in App. E.22.
- Sources: Public: 2103.06977; 1610.03974; 2009.03804; 2409.02820. Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-T-v2.md §4–§5; returns/R16/CW5.md §3.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 16.8 relaxation rates of a TLS in aluminium against T. Worked example 16.8: the heat release of
  1 g of fused silica after cooling to 10 mK. Limitbox plots: 16.8a (two panels), 16.8b, 16.8c, 16.8d (one panel each).

### Chapter 17. Comparison across channels   [PLANNED; 6 sections, 26,500 words]
  Framing (user, 2026-11-01): "I think this is more valuable because we want to help experimentalists identify where the next future experiment target might lie". The book's target-selection guide per channel (the leading materials with their uncertainty, what each would need demonstrated to become a next experiment's target, and the deciding measurements) needs the pair energies, readout and backgrounds of Part III, so it closes Part III (21.7); this chapter supplies its response-level half.
Opening: Part II has computed each channel in its own chapter; an experimentalist choosing a target needs them side by side. The question
here is, for one kind of coupling, which excitation of which material gives the largest rate and the lowest reachable mass, and why. The
chapter reads the atlas of Chap. 9 along the interaction axis, reduces each channel to the few material numbers that set its rate and
reach, and ranks materials by those numbers with their uncertainties. What a response-level ranking cannot decide, the energy per pair,
the readout and the backgrounds, is the subject of Part III, and the guide to next targets that combines both closes it in 21.7.

### 17.1 Scattering across channels   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Compare electronic, lattice, Migdal and phonon-assisted scattering in one target, and show which sector dominates in each
  range of mass and threshold.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) overlay the scattering rates of Chaps. 10–13 for silicon at fixed coupling and read which
  dominates; (ii) explain each crossover by kinematics and screening; (iii) explain why screening decides the comparison between
  electron-coupled and total-charge-coupled probes; (iv) repeat the comparison for germanium and GaAs.
- Prerequisites: kinematic map → 9.2; loss-function rate → 10.2; single-phonon nuclear response → 11.1; crystal Migdal rate → 12.3;
  phonon-assisted rate → 13.3; screening for every coupling → 8.4; silicon window → 13.9.
- Introduces: dominant-sector map (Fig. 17.2); kinematic ceilings of the sectors; multiphonon–Migdal crossover threshold ω_th^×.
- Key results: dominant-sector map for Si, Ge, GaAs [derived from the rates of Chaps. 10–13; REF; the window band is drawn with the
  status of 13.9: established edges solid, estimated leads hatched]; the multiphonon–Migdal crossover thresholds of silicon, 0.178 …
  5.33 eV at 1 … 100 MeV (Table 17.1) [computed]. Coherent-band qualifier (Ch12 erratum): up to 1 MeV the Migdal rate comes from
  0.75 keV ≲ q ≲ 2q_BZ, where the diagonal soft limit is not known to equal the coherent response; the Migdal band at 0.32–1 MeV and the
  1 MeV column of Table 17.1 carry that unsized factor (likely a suppression near threshold), a third of the rate at 2 MeV and ≤ 7 % from
  3 MeV, stated in the nuisance row "Coherent Migdal response at q < 2q_BZ" of Table 17.9 [unsized].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §6.2; returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §1; returns/R16/WP8-F-v3.md §2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 17.1 the chapter map; Fig. 17.2 the dominant-sector map in (m_χ, threshold) for silicon (two
  panels: nucleon and electron coupling). Table 17.1 the multiphonon–Migdal crossover; Table 17.2 the edges of the map in Si, Ge and GaAs.
  Worked example 17.1: the crossover between the multiphonon and Migdal rates in silicon at 10 MeV. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: closes T2.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the coherent Migdal response between 0.75 keV and 2q_BZ in silicon (sizes the Migdal band below about
  2 MeV); the germanium phonon density of states and zero-phonon edge (Ge crossover); the GaAs elastic tensor (acoustic edge).
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 17.2 Absorption across channels   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Compare absorption of bosonic dark matter on electrons, phonons, magnons, superconductors and special targets.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) overlay the absorption rates of Chaps. 10–16 against m_φ for each boson; (ii) explain the role of
  in-medium mixing in each comparison; (iii) say which target covers each decade of m_φ; (iv) state by how much a counter of quanta
  whose detection band is the line width beats a quantum-limited linear readout of the same mode, e^{m_φ/2T} in signal, and why the
  advantage falls below e^{1/2} for m_φ < T and tends to one (no coherent crossover).

  coherent classical-field regime → 14.6; dark-photon absorption on TLS → 16.7; reference appendices → App. C.14.
- Introduces: absorption coverage map (Fig. 17.3); screening ceiling of the loss function; counting against linear readout.
- Key results: absorption coverage by target and mass [derived; REF]; screening ceiling L ≤ 1/(2|ε₁|) [derived]; counting against linear
  readout N̄_sig^lin/N̄_sig^cnt = e^{m_φ/2T} for a counter whose band is the line width (a band B wider than the line multiplies the
  ratio by (B/Δω_eq)^{−1/2}; Lamoreaux et al. 1306.3591 carry (Q_c/2πQ_a)^{1/2}) [derived from the 14.6 variance; cited: 1306.3591
  Eqs. (9), (12)–(14), (18)–(20); no novelty wording].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-D-v2.md §1; returns/R8/WP3-v3.md §4; returns/R16/WP8-F-v3.md (scalar row).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 17.3 absorption coverage from µeV to keV (three panels: dark photon, axion-like particle, scalar);
  Table 17.3 absorbing targets by decade of mass. Worked example 17.2: the mass at which phonon absorption in GaAs and electronic
  absorption in Si cross. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

- Purpose: Compare channels for dark matter with a mass splitting, including down-scattering into collective modes.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the exothermic rate into a collective mode (phonon, magnon, plasmon) from the master formula

### 17.4 Interference between channels   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the interference between the Migdal and phonon-assisted amplitudes and the other cross-sector terms, and show when
  they matter.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Migdal–phonon-assisted interference term in the phonon-dressed electronic sector for
  any coupling with both electron and nucleus charges; (ii) show it vanishes for pure electron or pure nucleon couplings; (iii) bound it by
  the positivity of the response matrix; (iv) list the other cross terms of the sector table (Born-charge interference, ionic correction
  of the zero-phonon line).
- Prerequisites: decomposition theorem, sector table → 8.6; positivity, interference bound → 8.3; crystal Migdal rate → 12.3; small-q
  Migdal rate → 12.4; phonon-assisted rate → 13.3; overlap regions → 9.3.
- Introduces: cross-sector interference (precise); Migdal–phonon-assisted interference term.
- Key results: interference term and its vanishing conditions [derived; REF]; bound by positivity [derived; REF]; at small q the bound is
  saturated and the phonon-dressed rate carries |Ṽ_e − (m_e/M_κ)Ṽ_nuc|² (eq:8.3-boostward on the one-phonon sector, consistent with the
  Ch12 erratum; remainder O(q/q_BZ), order of magnitude only in the windows) [derived].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP0-v3.md §6.2 (sector table, cross terms); returns/R8/WP3-v3.md Part II §1.5.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 17.5 the interference region in (q, ω). Worked example 17.4: the interference for a dark photon
  with a heavy mediator in GaAs. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: closes T4.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

- Purpose: Reduce each channel to the response-level material numbers that control its rate and reach, with their validity windows.

- Purpose: Rank materials per channel, at the response level, with probabilities and tiers that respect the uncertainty of every input.

  small-gap crystals.

---
## Part III — Measurement

Part III is planned in full (SPINE-II; revised in SPINE-II-REV). Its heading is declared as \part{Measurement} before \input{ch18/ch18} in
main.tex and gate.tex (ledger merge of Chapter 18). It follows the energy from the final states of Part II to the quanta a
sensor counts (Chaps. 18–20), then to a limit and to the guide to next targets (Chap. 21), and ends with an index of what remains open
(Chap. 22). The status tags of the head of Chap. 8 apply.

### Chapter 18. Ionization yield   [PLANNED; 5 sections, 23,500 words]
Opening: A dark-matter event in a semiconductor deposits energy, but the detector counts electron–hole pairs, and between the two lies
a cascade of hot carriers that ionize, emit phonons and slow down. The mean energy spent per pair and its fluctuation set the threshold
of every electron-recoil search, and for a long time both were fitted rather than computed. This chapter asks how far they can be
derived from the same rates as the rest of the book, with the carrier itself as the projectile of the master formula, and what the
cascade inherits from the final state that Part II hands over. It opens Part III, which follows the deposit to a measured signal.

### 18.1 The carrier cascade   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Show that every kernel of the hot-carrier cascade is a master-formula rate with a target-internal projectile, and write the
  cascade as a branching process.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the impact-ionization and phonon-emission rates of a hot carrier from the master formula with
  the carrier as projectile; (ii) define the branching generating functional and derive its recursion; (iii) state the controlled
  reductions (energy-only, random-k) and their hypotheses; (iv) explain why the cascade must be seeded with the operator structure of the
  final state.
- Prerequisites: master rate formula → 8.2; loss-function rate → 10.2; electron–phonon vertex → 6.2; final-state-resolved response,
  seeding → 8.7; W^scr, GW → 4.8; reference appendices → App. C.11.
- Introduces: carrier cascade; impact ionization (precise); impact-ionization threshold ratio θ_ii; branching generating functional;
  energy-only and random-k reductions.
- Key results: every cascade kernel is a master-formula rate [derived; REF]; generating functional and its recursion [derived in D.43;
  REF]; reductions and their hypotheses [derived; REF]. All Chapter 18 model numbers use one continuum-calibrated silicon set, A_AB =
  5.703 eV³ at E_g = 1.160 eV (fixed once in 18.4; 18.1 uses it with a forward reference): 50 eV gives 12.99 pairs, p_ii = ½ at 5.9 eV,
  10 eV seed means 2.985/2.691/3.003. The full-band cascade of the R19 FBC run is quoted as a reference for both reductions (exchange
  factor 0.66–0.84 for electrons, 0.51–0.68 for holes above 3 eV; ε decided at 2.5–4.5 eV; fewer than 1 % of 4 eV events with two pairs;
  momentum memory negligible within the deformation-potential model).
- Computational connection: impact-ionization and phonon rates from GW and DFPT (App. E.13); specified in App. E.24.
- Sources: Public: Ramanathan and Kurinsky 2004.10709; Kane, Phys. Rev. 159, 624 (1967), as quoted by Kotani and van Schilfgaarde 0812.2923. Writer only: returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §1.1–§1.4, §2;
  returns/R16/GAP-EF-v2.md §1.

### 18.2 Mean energy per pair and the Fano factor   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive ε_eh and the Fano factor F_eh as moments of the cascade, and recover the classic limits.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive ε_eh and F_eh from the first and second moments of the generating functional within its
  hypotheses; (ii) decompose F_eh = 𝒱_< + 𝒱_> + 2𝒱_<> and compute each term for silicon (0.092 + 0.290 − 0.268 = 0.114, on the calibrated set of 18.4); (iii) recover the
  random-parking (Rényi) limit F = 0.0510 and the renewal value; (iv) show that the renewal (final-state) estimate is a lower bound in every
  computed case; (v) derive the linear mean yield n̄(ω) = ω/ε_eh + o(ω) from renewal theory.
- Prerequisites: generating functional, θ_ii → 18.1; ε_eh, N_eh → 1.5; reference appendices → App. C.11.
- Introduces: Fano factor F_eh (precise); variance decomposition 𝒱_<, 𝒱_>, 𝒱_<>; renewal (final-state) Fano estimate; random-parking limit.
- Key results: Limit 18.2a: ε_eh and F_eh as cascade moments, plotted against ω/E_g (the approach of the finite-deposit definitions; the
  phonon-loss dependence is Fig. 18.2) [derived in D.43; REF]; Limit 18.2b: the random-parking limit, ρ∞ = 0.74759792,
  F = 0.0510387 [derived; cited: 0911.1532 Eqs. (2), (23); REF; the source's two printed digits differ (one sentence; the detail in the
  Appendix F row)]; Limit 18.2c: renewal estimate as lower bound in every computed case [derived; cited: 0911.1532 Eq. (7); REF; the
  general bound is open, below]; linear mean yield [derived in D.44; VER].
- Sources: Public: 0911.1532; 2004.10709. Writer only: returns/R16/GAP-EF-v2.md §1 with returns/R17/GAP-EF-v3-errata.md; returns/R16/CW5.md
  §2.7; returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §6.4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 18.2 F_eh and its three terms against the phonon loss parameter. Worked example 18.2: the
  random-parking Fano factor written out. Limitbox plots: 18.2a, 18.2b, 18.2c (one panel each).
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the constant term and the variance of the yield at second order in renewal theory (XG-6); the general

### 18.4 Calibrated pair energies   [status: ACCEPTED]

  linear law ε_eh ≈ 1.61E_g + k₂, with k₂ its fitted constant, inside its validated range; (iv) state why predictions for halides,

### Chapter 19. Low-energy nuclear recoils   [PLANNED; 6 sections, 29,000 words]
Opening: A nucleus recoiling at a few hundred eV moves through the lattice for less than a picosecond, and in that time it decides how
much of its energy becomes heat, how much is stored in defects and how much becomes charge. The yields that standard searches take from
Lindhard's model were derived for faster ions and are least reliable exactly where light dark matter deposits its energy. This chapter
asks what happens to the recoil energy once the nuclear response of Chap. 11 hands the nucleus over to dynamics on the
Born–Oppenheimer surface, and it writes one balance law that books every share once. It also connects the energy stored in defects to
the delayed events that limit low-threshold detectors, and so to the readout of Chap. 20.

### 19.1 Nuclear recoil on the Born–Oppenheimer surface   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Hand the nuclear recoil from the response of Chap. 11 to classical and quantum dynamics on the Born–Oppenheimer surface.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) take the hand-over from the electron-following nuclear response at the recoil energy; (ii) state
  the regime boundaries (harmonic, anharmonic, cascade) of the recoil dynamics; (iii) say what molecular dynamics with machine-learned
  potentials must reproduce; (iv) define the partition of the recoil energy into heat, defects and charge.
- Prerequisites: multiphonon response, impulse approximation → 11.3, 11.4; energy surface → 5.1; anharmonic → 5.7; final-state-resolved
  response → 8.7; displacement threshold → 7.4.
- Introduces: recoil hand-over; recoil-dynamics regimes; partition of the recoil energy (heat, stored energy E_st, charge).
- Key results: the hand-over [derived; REF]; regime boundaries [derived for the boundaries, estimates for the numbers; REF].
- Computational connection: molecular dynamics with machine-learned potentials (App. E.11); specified in App. E.23.
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §3.1–§3.2, §3.4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 19.1 a 200 eV silicon recoil cascade (schematic). Worked example 19.1: the harmonic-to-cascade
  boundary for silicon. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T6.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 19.2 Electronic excitation by the recoil   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the charge channel of a slow recoil: electronic excitation in close collisions and the threshold below which it closes.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the binary electron–hole threshold E_R,th ≈ 352 eV (Si), 399 eV (Ge) and say why it is soft;
  (ii) write the close-collision excitation kernel and its exchange–correlation nuisance; (iii) state what the measured silicon yield at
  0.1–3.9 keV tests and why the chain has not yet been computed against it; (iv) relate the Migdal effect to this channel at low E_R.
- Prerequisites: recoil-dynamics regimes → 19.1; crystal Migdal rate → 12.3; E_g → 3.3.
- Introduces: binary electron–hole threshold E_R,th; close-collision excitation kernel; ionization yield of a nuclear recoil Y_q(E_R);
  Lindhard-type yield models (named; forward, made precise in 19.3).
- Key results: binary threshold [derived; REF]; the test against the silicon yield is specified, not computed (Appendix F row L43, GAP;
  stated only in the open-problems passage).
- Computational connection: the close-collision excitation kernels and the charge yield below the binary threshold are specified in
  App. E.23.
- Sources: Public: 2303.02196 Table II. Writer only: returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §3.3; returns/R14/WP5-AUD.md §4.

  from the gap and the mass ratio. Limitbox plots: none.

  (XG-8; App. E.23).

### 19.3 Stopping of slow ions   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Recover Lindhard quenching and the Bethe formula as limits, and state their implicit assumptions.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Lindhard integral equation as the binary-collision, continuum limit of the partition;
  (ii) state the implicit assumptions (no stored energy, full collection, no trapping) and where they fail below a keV; (iii) derive the
  Bethe stopping formula from the loss function with ln Ī = (2/πω_p²)∫ωL(0, ω)ln ω dω; (iv) explain why track physics of fast ions lies
  outside the book's scope.
- Prerequisites: partition of the recoil energy → 19.1; stopping, loss function → 4.4; f-sum → 4.3; ionization yield of a nuclear recoil →
  19.2.
- Introduces: Lindhard quenching (precise; one line in the text separates it from the Lindhard function of 4.5, a different object
  named after the same author); modified Lindhard model; Bethe stopping, starting from the dielectric stopping of 4.4 (cited, not
  re-derived); mean excitation energy Ī.
- Key results: Limit 19.3a: Lindhard quenching with its implicit assumptions [derived; cited: Lindhard et al. (1963), to confirm at
  briefing; 2209.04503; 2001.06503 App. B; REF]; Limit 19.3b: Bethe stopping from the loss function [derived; cited: PDG RPP 2024 Eq. (34.5);
  REF, the Bethe logarithm from the loss function VER]. The modeling of the softest collisions of the slowest secondaries is a
  sensitivity, not an uncertainty: the local-slope continuation raises the yield by 13 % at 1 keV against Lindhard's x^{1/3} law
  (−11/+13/+40 % for exponents 0.30/0.363/0.40), and the ε_L^{1/6} law follows from that continuation [computed, lind19_3.log]; the
  silicon Ī from the measured loss function is 161 eV against the adopted 173.0 eV.
- Sources: Public: 2209.04503; 2001.06503; PDG RPP 2024 ch. 34. Writer only: returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §6.1; returns/R12/WP7-D-v2.md §4.1;
  returns/R16/CW3.md (L-D11′).

### 19.5 The energy balance law   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Write one time-resolved balance law per event that books every share of the deposit once, and derive heat quenching and its
  calibration identity from it.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write E_dep = E_pairs + E_prompt + E_st(t) + E_esc + E_sens with stored energies
  configurational; (ii) derive the unit heat-quenching truncation and its condition (all pairs recombined or collected by the read window);
  (iii) derive the phonon-yield ratio of nuclear to electron recoils for a general collection ratio 𝔯_coll, and show its degeneracy
  between stored energy and collection (illustrated by the CDMS II silicon datum 0.952 through the two-ε calibration chain: the
  reconstruction adopts ε_cal = 3.82 eV against ε_eh = 3.75 eV, an offset of −0.0064 at 4 V; the datum is reproduced by a stored
  fraction of 0.042 or, with no stored energy, by 𝔯_coll = 1.128 [1.099, 1.152], which the mean-field transport estimate of 20.5 does
  not reach (0.81–1.02 with energy conserved, 0.82–1.01 at the mean Luke energy of Si electrons at 1 V/cm; up to 1.5 only for a selective
  sink not identified in a detector), so collection alone is not reached by transport but not excluded until computed kernels test real
  detectors, and the two-bias measurement decides); (iv) break the degeneracy with two biases; (v) derive the gap-energy release law
  (the dense-track time of eq:19.5-bimol is t_rel; t₀ is the pair-breaking time of 20.3–20.4).
- Prerequisites: stored energy, defect fraction → 19.4; partition of the recoil energy → 19.1; ε_eh → 1.5; seed distribution → 18.3.
- Introduces: energy balance law; heat quenching; phonon-yield ratio Y_ph; collection ratio 𝔯_coll and collection offset δ_coll;
  calibration coefficient C_cal; the Luke effect (recap, one paragraph: drifting carriers give their bias work to phonons; op.; the
  emission itself is precise in 20.1) and the Luke gain g_Luke = eV_bias/ε_eh, with the gain g_cal = eV_bias/ε_cal of the pair energy a
  reconstruction adopts; ionized fraction f_ion; gap-energy release law; two-bias
  inversion.
- Key results: the balance law [derived in D.45; REF]; Limit 19.5a: unit heat quenching [derived; cited: 2007.14289; REF]; Limit 19.5b (merged
  with the former 19.4 box): stored energy from the phonon-yield ratio, the calibration identity for general 𝔯_coll with the two gains, and

  0.81–1.02, VER]; gap-release
  law and two-bias inversion (40 V readings 0.919 and 0.947, difference 0.028) [derived; VER].
- Sources: Public: 1803.02903; 2007.14289. Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-E-v3.md §1, §3; returns/R14/WP5-AUD.md §2; returns/R16/CW5.md

### Chapter 20. Charge, phonon and quasiparticle readout   [PLANNED; 5 sections, 23,000 words]
Opening: The shares of the balance law are not yet a signal. Charge must drift to an electrode, phonons must reach a sensor film and
break pairs there, and each step loses some memory of the event. This chapter asks what a charge or phonon readout actually measures,
given transport in the crystal and across interfaces, and whether that measurement can still tell a nuclear recoil from an electron
recoil. The validation of transport codes against these results is not yet refereed and appears only as open material; what is
measured feeds the limits of Chap. 21 and the readout column of the guide to next targets (21.7).

### 20.1 Charge transport and the measured charge   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the measured charge from drift, trapping and induced signals, and the Fano factor of the measured charge.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the Hecht relation from exponential trapping in a uniform field; (ii) derive the induced
  charge and bias work with Ramo's theorem; (iii) derive the measured-charge Fano factor F_meas = f_det F_eh + 1 − f_det with detected fraction f_det; (iv) write the
  space-charge field of trapped charge and its sheet bound (the face value 6.3×10⁴ cm⁻² applies only to a vanishing slab); (v) derive
  the golden-rule Luke-phonon emission rate of drifting carriers, Γ_L = (c_LA/3l₀)(k/k_s)²(1 − k_s/k)³, growing as k², and say why it is
  still open (LA only, isotropic band; the published total rates, 1109.1193 Eq. (21) and 2302.05998 Eq. (8), are low by k_s/k while their
  angular laws integrate to the golden-rule rate; E.12 eq:E.12-luke is fixed to it). The measured-charge convention: F_eh = 0.114 is the
  cascade value, 0.119 the measured value used for electron-recoil signals.
- Prerequisites: energy balance law, g_Luke → 19.5; Fano factor → 18.2; electron–hole pair → 1.5; classical electromagnetism → READER.md
  "assumed"; reference appendices → App. C.11.
- Introduces: Hecht relation; Ramo induced charge and bias work; measured-charge Fano factor; space-charge field; Luke-phonon emission.
- Key results: Limit 20.1a: Hecht collection [derived; cited: Hecht, Z. Phys. 77, 235 (1932); 1701.03459 Eqs. (1)–(4); REF]; Limit 20.1b:
  induced charge and bias work [derived; cited: 1803.02903 Eq. (1); REF]; Limit 20.1c: measured-charge Fano factor [derived; cited:
  2004.10709 Eqs. (A6)–(A7); REF]; space-charge field [derived; VER]; Luke-phonon emission rate [derived (golden rule); the printed
  normalizations low by k_s/k; VER].
- Computational connection: carrier drift with Luke emission and trapping by transport Monte Carlo (App. E.12); athermal emission and
  space charge are specified in App. E.25.
- Sources: Public: 1701.03459; 1803.02903; 2004.10709; 1109.1193. Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-E-v3.md §2; returns/R16/CW6.md §6;
  returns/R15/CMPMC-AUD.md §1.2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 20.1 Hecht curves. Worked example 20.1: the measured Fano factor for 95% collection in silicon.
  Limitbox plots: 20.1a, 20.1b, 20.1c (one panel each).
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): athermal emission from traps and space-charge nonlinearity over an exposure history (LE-122); the Luke
  emission rate of drifting carriers beyond the LA-only golden rule (transverse coupling unsized), and its code validation from
  1109.1193 Eq. (22), unrefereed [CL] (XG-9); validation of the
  transport engine against these results, unrefereed [CL] (XG-10; App. E.25).
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 20.2 Athermal phonons in the substrate   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Follow prompt phonons from the event through anharmonic decay and isotope scattering, and show what memory of the event survives.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the anharmonic decay rate ∝ E_ph⁵ and the isotope rate ∝ E_ph⁴ (phonon energy E_ph)
  with the Tamura constant; (ii) derive the self-similar scaling spectrum in E_ph/E_*(t); (iii) show that in bulk Si and Ge prompt populations lose frequency and
  polarization memory (estimates: 6.8 ns (Si) and 47 ns (Ge) for births near the band top, 0.15 µs and 1.0 µs for births over the upper
  factor two of the band, about 1 µs for the 22 meV TA phonons of optical decay; a birth is forgotten once E_* ≈ 0.52 of its energy, so
  the time scales as the birth energy to the −5), except near an instrumented face (13.5 % of a 4 mm crystal at 1 µs); (iv) state why this
  fails for sapphire.
- Prerequisites: three-phonon, linewidth → 5.7; partition of the recoil energy → 19.1; mass-defect local density of states → 11.6; energy
  balance law → 19.5.
- Introduces: athermal phonon; anharmonic down-conversion; isotope scattering rate (Tamura); scaling spectrum; memory loss of prompt
  phonons.
- Key results: Limit 20.2a: down-conversion and the scaling spectrum [derived for the scaling, estimate for the numbers; cited: 1109.1193
  Table I; 2302.05998; 2408.04732 Tables 3–4; REF; the memory-loss times are estimates, EST]; Limit 20.2b: Tamura isotope rate [derived;
  cited: 2408.04732 Eq. (4), Table 4; VER; Rayleigh law within 10 % in Si only below 5.6 meV]; transfer-kernel bound on observable
  differences [derived; VER; loose for the model spectra, 0.15 against an actual 0.012 at 1 µs]. The Ge anharmonic constant is quoted in
  two values attributed to Tamura (6.43×10⁻⁵⁵ and 1.65×10⁻⁵⁴ s⁴, a factor 2.6 apart; the slowest is used).
- Computational connection: phonon transport Monte Carlo (App. E.12); the created-phonon spectra at the sensors are specified in
  App. E.25.
- Sources: Public: 1109.1193; 2302.05998; 2408.04732. Writer only: returns/R16/WP8-R-v2.md §2; returns/R16/CW6.md §1–§4.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 20.2 the phonon spectrum of a silicon event at 1, 10 and 100 ns. Worked example 20.2: the
  isotope rate of natural silicon at 1 THz. Limitbox plots: 20.2a, 20.2b (one panel each).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 20.3 Phonons into films   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the transmission of phonons from the substrate into a sensor film and the film escape time.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive acoustic- and diffuse-mismatch transmission for an isotropic welded interface from the
  acoustic impedances (continuum elasticity is recapped in App. C.18), with reciprocity 𝔫₁t̄₁₂ = 𝔫₂t̄₂₁, where 𝔫_i is the
  interface-weighted phonon density of side i; (ii) show that the fitted Si→Al value 0.3–0.55 is an absorption probability t̄·P_abs that
  constrains the pair-breaking length over the thickness, not the escape probability; (iii) derive the film escape time
  t_l = 4d_f/(v_s t̄_esc) for film thickness d_f and mean phonon speed v_s in the film, and its hypotheses; (iv) state what anisotropy and
  interlayers change. Aluminum enters with the 0 K isotropic (Voigt) constants, c_LA = 6.63 and c_T = 3.30 km/s at 2.70 g/cm³ (Ch15's
  6.47/3.40 km/s are [100] speeds; the room-temperature constants are a sensitivity): Si→Al t̄ 0.938, t̄_esc 0.368 (DMM 0.282), critical
  angle 37.7°, 56 % of the film flux trapped, t_l 0.187–0.244 ns and t_l/t₀ 0.72–0.94 for 60 nm, the lumped film understating thin-film
  absorption by 2.72 (thin limit; 1.82 at 60 nm), and the fitted value constraining 𝔩_f/d_f ≈ 5–23.
- Prerequisites: athermal phonon → 20.2; sound speed → 5.2; reference appendices → App. C.18.
- Introduces: acoustic mismatch (AMM) and diffuse mismatch (DMM) transmission; reciprocity of transmission; film absorption probability
  P_abs; film escape time t_l.
- Key results: Limit 20.3a: AMM and DMM transmission and reciprocity, with the reading of the fitted value [derived; cited: 1805.02495;
  REF; the numbers are estimates, EST]; film escape [derived; VER].
- Computational connection: interface transmission in transport Monte Carlo (App. E.12); anisotropic interface transmission is
  specified in App. E.25.
- Sources: Public: 1805.02495; Swartz and Pohl, Rev. Mod. Phys. 61, 605 (1989). Writer only: returns/R16/WP8-R-v2.md §3; returns/R16/CW6.md
  §3–§5.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 20.3 AMM transmission of Si/Al against angle. Worked example 20.3: t̄_esc of a 60 nm Al film.
  Limitbox plots: 20.3a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 20.4 Quasiparticle yield in superconductors   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the fraction of phonon energy converted into quasiparticles in a superconducting film.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the pair-breaking efficiency 2Δ_BCS/E_ath below 4Δ_BCS and its fall in thin films above;
  (ii) write the Kaplan cascade of phonons and quasiparticles in a lumped film; (iii) state the dependence on t_l/t₀ (escape over
  pair-breaking time) against the Chang–Scalapino equations (the cascade lies within 0.011 of 1401.1937 Fig. 4 for t_l/t₀ ≤ 4); (iv)
  explain why recombination phonons from electrodes are soft (85–92 % at or below 2Δ_BCS of the electrode) and ballistic. The cascade
  gives the no-escape yield 0.590 (maximum 0.600 at 5.46Δ_BCS); the film loss is 41 % at 10Δ_BCS and 47–58 % over the arrival band
  13.6–43.8Δ_BCS at t_l/t₀ = 0.5 (35–50 % for 60 nm Al); the pair-breaking rate is Kaplan et al.'s.
- Prerequisites: Bogoliubov quasiparticles, Δ_BCS, pair-breaking → 7.2; α²F → 6.4; film escape time → 20.3.
- Introduces: quasiparticle (pair-breaking) efficiency Y_qp(E_ath); Kaplan cascade; recombination phonons.
- Key results: Limit 20.4a: pair-breaking efficiency, derived below 4Δ_BCS and estimated above [derived; cited: 1401.1937
  pp. 2, 5 and Fig. 4; REF; above 4Δ_BCS an estimate, EST].
- Computational connection: film cascades in transport Monte Carlo (App. E.12); the collection efficiencies are specified in App. E.25.
- Open problems (exact gaps): a transport-code reproduction of 1401.1937 Fig. 4 to ≤ 0.011 exists but is unrefereed [CL] (XG-11).
- Sources: Public: 1401.1937; Kaplan et al., Phys. Rev. B 14, 4854 (1976). Writer only: returns/R13/WP5-v4.md §5; returns/R16/WP8-R-v2.md;
  returns/R16/CMPMC-DEV.md.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 20.4 Y_qp against E_ath/Δ_BCS. Worked example 20.4: Y_qp for 3Δ_BCS phonons in aluminum (2/3).
  Limitbox plots: 20.4a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 20.5 Collection kernels and recoil discrimination   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Combine transport into collection kernels and decide whether phonon or charge readout can separate nuclear from electron
  recoils.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) define collection kernels and derive the contrasts ϖ_O, ϖ_gap, the position spread and 𝔯_coll from
  them; (ii) show that the sign of 𝔯_coll − 1 is set by the Luke energy within a few gaps of the film thresholds and by what the detector
  does with the phonons a film emits (𝔯_coll − 1 = s_ng − (1 − κ_sub)s_sub for one film type): with energy conserved the mean-field estimate
  is 0.81–1.02 over ⟨E_L⟩ = 0.75–4.7 meV and 0.82–1.01 at the operating point 0.84 meV (Si electrons, 1 V/cm), the selective-sink values
  1.05–1.53 an upper envelope, so 1.128 is not reached by the transport estimate, is not excluded until computed kernels test real
  detectors, and the two-bias measurement of 19.5 decides; (iii) derive the high-voltage readout ceiling (1.22 at 220 eV at central
  inputs; over 1σ, 1.36 in the binomial limit at f_det = 0.873, 1.52 at full collection, 1.72 in the mean-charge limit; low-bias ceiling
  10.8 at 100 eV) and why its no-go at E_R ≤ 300 eV is conditional on (Y_q, F_NR, collection, confidence level); (iv) state the
  discrimination facts as conditional on the estimated memory-loss times of 20.2 (6.8/47 ns near the band top, 0.15/1.0 µs over the
  upper factor two, about 1 µs for 22 meV TA phonons; the primary-phonon limit an estimate, not a bound) and on the unrefereed transport
  validation: no mode, frequency or angle selection of prompt phonons in bulk Si and Ge, and the zero-field gap-energy channel
  unresolved. The verdicts subsection is cut; the facts are stated in the Summary, an explicit input to the readout column of 21.7.
- Prerequisites: athermal phonon, memory loss → 20.2; AMM, film escape → 20.3; Y_qp → 20.4; Luke-phonon emission, Ramo → 20.1; collection
  ratio, Luke gain → 19.5; ionization yield of a nuclear recoil → 19.2.
- Introduces: collection kernel; collection contrasts ϖ_O, ϖ_gap; position spread; high-voltage readout ceiling; recoil-discrimination
  verdicts.
- Key results: collection kernels [derived for the functionals, VER; mean-field estimates, EST]; HV ceiling and the conditional no-go
  [derived for the algebra, REF; estimates for the numbers, EST]; discrimination verdicts [EST: phrased conditionally on the estimated
  memory-loss numbers of 20.2 and on the transport validation, which is open (20.1)].
- Computational connection: collection kernels from transport Monte Carlo (App. E.12); the kernels and the gap-energy release are
  specified in App. E.25.
- Sources: Public: 2303.02196; 2012.12430; 1109.4343. Writer only: returns/R16/WP8-R-v2.md §4–§7; returns/R16/CW6.md; returns/R16/CW5.md
  (OC-7); state/SOTA.md R14–R16 block (IF-R6 statement).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 20.5 the HV ceiling against E_R with 1σ bands; Fig. 20.6 the mean-field collection ratio
  (fig:20-rcoll); Table 20.1 the nuisance table of the Summary (tex/ch20/s20-summary.tex, input last by ch20.tex). Worked example 20.5:
  the ceiling at E_R = 220 eV. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: advances T6.
- Open problems (exact gaps): the release-time law and site of the gap energy at zero field (LE-119; App. E.25).
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### Chapter 21. Exclusion limits and next targets   [PLANNED; 7 sections, 31,500 words]
Opening: Everything so far produces a rate; an experiment produces a count, often zero. This chapter asks how a rate becomes an exclusion
limit that others can trust and reuse: how the halo enters, what can be said without assuming the standard halo, how a limit behaves at
its low-mass edge, how the theory uncertainties of Part II become a band on it, and what a published response library must contain. It
then answers the question that motivated Chap. 17, where the next experiment's target might lie, by combining the response-level rankings
of 17.6 with the pair energies, readouts and backgrounds of Part III. Chap. 22 indexes what stays open.

### 21.1 Rate assembly   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Assemble the rate of any channel from the master formula and the halo, and recover the standard-halo-model rate.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the rate as a linear functional of the velocity distribution with a kernel per channel;
  (ii) recover η(v_min) and the standard rate of 1509.01598 in the standard halo model; (iii) state why m_min values computed with the
  reference speed are floors, and that a floor belongs to its halo and Earth speed: the exposure-averaged rate (June included, floor 3.9 %
  lower for silicon) is the basis of a limit, June's Earth speed is kinematics, and the reference floor is the convention for comparing targets; (iv) evaluate the same rate for a halo that is not the standard one, from the kernel alone.
- Prerequisites: master rate formula → 8.2; f_χ, SHM, v_E(t) → 1.2; η(v_min), m_min → 1.3; exposure, exclusion → 1.5; crystal form factor →
  10.1.
- Introduces: halo functional of a channel; rate kernel.
- Key results: Limit 21.1a: η(v_min) of the standard halo model in closed form, with escape truncation and v_E(t) [derived; cited:
  1509.01598 Eq. (17), eq. to fix: if this is the equation boxed in 10.1a, the crystal rate is cited from 10.1a and only η(v_min) is
  boxed here; Baxter et al. 2105.00599; REF]; m_min with the reference speed is a floor of the channel's edge for that halo and Earth
  speed, not of the exposure-averaged rate [derived; REF].
- Sources: Public: 1509.01598; 2105.00599; Lewin and Smith (1996). Writer only: returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §1–§2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 21.1 the rate kernel of silicon at three masses. Worked example 21.1: the silicon rate at
  m_χ = 10 MeV for a halo with an added debris-flow component, from the kernel (the standard-halo rate is worked example 10.1).
  Limitbox plots: 21.1a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 21.2 Halo-independent bounds   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive limits that hold for every velocity distribution consistent with stated constraints, including direction-dependent
  responses.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) write the rate as linear in a non-negative distribution and the bound as a semi-infinite linear
  program; (ii) derive the stream (extreme-point) solution and its dual certificate; (iii) extend it to direction-dependent crystal
  responses in the laboratory frame; (iv) bound the leakage of a discretized program.
- Prerequisites: halo functional, rate kernel → 21.1; anisotropic response → 11.7; reference appendices → App. C.12.
- Introduces: halo-independent bound; stream method; dual certificate; directional extension in the laboratory frame.
- Key results: Limit 21.2a: the stream method extended to electron and directional responses, with its dual certificate and leakage bound
  [derived in D.46; cited: 1403.4606, 1703.09168 (abstract-level structure); 2105.08101; VER].
- Computational connection: the linear program on a sphere grid is specified in App. E.27.
- Sources: Public: 1403.4606; 1703.09168; 2105.08101. Writer only: returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §4; returns/R16/CW7.md §3.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 21.2 a halo-independent bound against the SHM limit for silicon, with the pair bins of the Ch18
  calibrated cascade (one-pair bin binding at 10 MeV, σ̄_lim 3.72e-42 cm²; cost ×2.12 at 10 MeV, ×3.70 at 2 MeV). Worked example 21.2: one
  stream saturating a bound. Limitbox plots: 21.2a (one panel).
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 21.3 The kinematic edge   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Derive the shape of a limit near its low-mass edge and the degeneracies it hides.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) derive the power law of the rate near m_min from the onset of the response and the escape tail of
  the halo; (ii) show how the onset exponent of a channel and the halo truncation combine; (iii) state the degeneracy between threshold,
  gap and halo parameters at the edge; (iv) apply it to the silicon phonon-assisted and zero-phonon edges.
- Prerequisites: kinematic edge, m_min → 1.3; onset exponent → 13.3; halo functional → 21.1; v_esc → 1.2; silicon window → 13.9.
- Introduces: edge power law; edge degeneracy.
- Key results: edge power laws and degeneracies [derived; REF].
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §3.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 21.3 the low-mass edge of a silicon limit with its power law. Worked example 21.3: how a 10 meV
  gap shift moves the edge. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: closes T1.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 21.4 Theory nuisances in a limit   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Turn counts into a limit, starting from the Poisson limit with known background, and propagate the truncation nuisances of 8.8
  into it as a band, channel by channel.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) set a Poisson upper limit with a known background, the starting point of every limit in the
  book (the statistics are recapped in App. C.14); (ii) attach to each channel its nuisance set from Chaps. 10–16 with priors, profile
  them in a likelihood and report the limit band; (iii) state which nuisance dominates each channel's band; (iv) explain one-sided
  nuisances and masked cells.
- Prerequisites: nuisance parameter, nuisance types → 8.8; electronic-sector nuisance set → 10.10; lattice-sector nuisance set → 11.9;
  superconducting edge remainder → 15.2; halo functional → 21.1; Poisson, exclusion → 1.5; reference appendices → App. D.47, C.14.
- Introduces: theory band of a limit; profiled nuisances; masked cells.
- Key results: profile-likelihood band, with one statistical construction chapter-wide (Neyman, nuisances at their conditional best fit;
  ln ratio σ_β²𝗍′/2, exact for one bin; factor 1.427 for silicon at 3 MeV) [derived in D.47; cited: Cowan et al. 1007.1727; REF]; dominant nuisance per channel [derived
  estimates; REF].
- Sources: Public: 1007.1727. Writer only: returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §5–§6; returns/R16/CW7.md §1–§2.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 21.4 a silicon limit with its theory band. Worked example 21.4: the band from the random-phase level.
  Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: closes T7.
- Budget (words): 4,500.

### 21.5 Statistics, combination and recasting   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Treat the statistical procedures that turn counts into limits, combine experiments and recast published limits under new
  physics.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) set limits with an unknown background (optimum interval, stated), building on the Poisson limit
  of 21.4; (ii) combine experiments with shared nuisances; (iii) recast a limit for a corrected response (Dirac ε_bg² re-plots,
  boosted-flux corrections); (iv) state when a recast is exact: when the published limit is the minimum over bins, and not otherwise.
- Prerequisites: theory band, profiled nuisances → 21.4; halo functional → 21.1; Dirac-material rate, reach re-plot factors → 16.1;
  boosted dark-matter flux → 10.9; Poisson → 1.5; reference appendices → App. C.14.
- Introduces: combination with shared nuisances; recasting condition; corrected-response recast.
- Key results: combination and recast rules, with the recast exactness condition [derived; REF]; corrected Dirac recast [derived; REF];
  the boosted-flux recast is open and stated in 10.9.
- Sources: Public: Yellin, Phys. Rev. D 66, 032005 (2002); 1007.1727. Writer only: returns/R8/WP6-v3.md §2, §6; returns/R16/CW4.md §9, §18.
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Fig. 21.5 a corrected Dirac re-plot. Worked example 21.5: combining two silicon exposures (cascade one-pair bin). Limitbox
  plots: none.
- Threads: none.

### 21.7 Next targets   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Give experimentalists a guide per channel: the leading materials with their uncertainty, what each still needs demonstrated
  to become a next experiment's target, and the measurements or computations that would change the ranking. It combines the
  response-level rankings of 17.6 with the pair energies of 18.4, the readout verdicts of 20.5 and the internal backgrounds; current
  capability is a column of the guide, never a filter.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) read Table 21.2, whose columns are: channel; leader of the full pool and leader of the survivor
  pool, side by side, each with P(first) and its 68% rank interval; the figure of merit and the provenance of its inputs (measured,
  computed, estimated); capability (crystal mass or array of crystals, sensor readout at millikelvin, radiopurity, synthesis at gram
  scale), stated as a column; the missing demonstration; the deciding measurement or computation; and what the reading is conditional
  on; (ii) state the leader of each channel (Ge for the electron-recoil floor and for quanta; TeO₂ for the heavy-mediator nuclear channel;
  diamond for the nuclear edge under an array rule; LiF for the polar rate; GaAs for the polar reach), say in one sentence why silicon
  leads none, and say where no reading survives (Migdal, light mediator); (iii) estimate the sub-keV internal background of a crystal
  from β emitters and ²¹⁰Pb, a recap of the physics that the guide's radiopurity column needs; (iv) read the rows for absorption, magnons,
  superconductors and the special targets of Chap. 16, which carry a leader where the coverage map of 17.2 has one; (v) explain why every
  probability in the table is conditional on the width conventions of 17.6.
- Prerequisites: ranking procedure, tiers, P(first) → 17.6; figures of merit → 17.5; absorption coverage map → 17.2; silicon window → 13.9;
  observed linear law of ε_eh, validated range → 18.4; recoil-discrimination verdicts → 20.5; Y_qp → 20.4; theory band → 21.4; ε_eh → 1.5.
- Introduces: next-target guide (Table 21.2) and its columns; survivor pool (eligible by data quality only) and conditional survivor;
  capability column; internal radioactive backgrounds (recap: β self-deposit, ²¹⁰Pb–²¹⁰Bi, Compton deposits, cosmogenic activation);
  deciding measurement.
- Key results: the guide [VER; every leader conditional on the 17.6 widths and, for quanta, on the 50 mK fit]; no Migdal reading at
  independent method errors (largest P(first) 0.32) and no light-mediator ranking (×1.08 spread) [VER]; the full-pool leaders (Pb salts,
  CsI, h-BN, NaCl) are conditional on estimated inputs [VER]; the β self-deposit and Compton sub-keV rate [EST]; the ²¹⁰Pb–²¹⁰Bi sub-keV
  rate in a lead compound, 724 per (kg d keV) per Bq/kg with the relativistic Fermi function, so 1.38 mBq/kg for a budget of one per
  (kg d keV) [EST; CL].
- Computational connection: L(k, ω) of every survivor (App. E.16); elastic constants of TeO₂ and of the crystals with computed tensors,
  and the cold density of CsI (App. E.17; diamond and CsI tensors measured); cosmogenic production and the remaining figure-of-merit inputs (App. E.27).
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R16/WP8-S-v2.md §0, §2–§5; returns/R17/WP8-errata.md §13, §16; state/SOTA.md "R17 final state" (guide
  headline, read as the authority for every leader and tag); returns/R16/WP8-U-v3.md §4; CONVENTIONS.md §13 row 38 (array rule).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 21.2 the next-target guide, with a caption stating that the probabilities depend on the 17.6
  width conventions; Fig. 21.6 leaders per channel with P(first), full pool and survivors side by side. Worked example 21.7: what would
  move CsI into the survivor list. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Open problems (exact gaps): L(k, ω) for every survivor, without which the Migdal channel has no ranking (XG-12; App. E.16); elastic
  constants of TeO₂ and of the crystals with computed tensors, and the cold density of CsI (diamond and CsI tensors measured) (XG-13;
  App. E.17); the internal backgrounds from β emitters and ²¹⁰Pb, estimated
  only (XG-14).
- Budget (words): 6,000.

### Chapter 22. Open problems   [PLANNED; 3 sections, 13,000 words]
Opening: The book has stated as open everything it could not establish, each item once, in the section where it arises. A reader who
wants to know what a calculation or a measurement would change needs those statements side by side. This chapter gives them as indexed
tables that point to the home of each item, with the result it limits and the check that a resolution must pass; it restates nothing.
It also names what lies outside the framework altogether.

### 22.1 Open calculations   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Index the computations the framework specifies and nobody has done, ordered by the results they would change, with the
  sum-rule checks each must pass, and say which channels are limited by theory and which by data.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) name the computations that would change a leading result (the silicon rate and reach, the
  germanium window, the Migdal ranking, the nuclear-recoil charge yield) and find the section that states each; (ii) state for each the
  check it must pass (f-sum, partial sum rules, controls); (iii) find its specification in App. E; (iv) say which channels are
  theory-limited and which data-limited, and which deciding measurements of 21.7 and computations here would most change the field.
- Prerequisites: exact relations → 8.3; silicon window → 13.9; next-target guide, deciding measurement → 21.7; ionization yield of a
  nuclear recoil → 19.2; response library → 21.6.
- Introduces: priority list of computations.
- Key results: the priority list and the checks [REF for the checks]; the classification of channels by limiting factor [from the
  homes indexed; REF] (22 channels: 12 limited first by calculation, 9 by data, 2 by theory).
- Sources: Writer only: state/DEFERRED-COMPUTE.md (summary table); state/SOTA.md "R17 final state".
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 22.1 the open calculations that would change a printed result, limit or ranking, each once,
  with its home section, the result it changes and the check it passes;
  Fig. 22.1 channels by limiting factor. Worked example 22.1: the f-sum check a phonon-assisted calculation of silicon must pass.
  Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: closes T3.
- Budget (words): 5,000.

### 22.2 Exact remaining gaps   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Index every exact remaining gap of the book to its home, each with its strongest derived statement and its single missing
  step, without restating it.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) state the book's definition of an exact remaining gap; (ii) find any gap in Table 22.2 by
  chapter and follow its pointer to the passage that states it; (iii) say for each gap whether it limits a number or only a proof.
- Prerequisites: decomposition theorem → 8.6; electronic-sector nuisance set → 10.10; cascade distribution → 18.3; priority list of
  computations → 22.1.
- Introduces: exact remaining gap (the book's definition: strongest statement plus missing step).
- Key results: Table 22.2, an index of every open-problems label to its one home, with a one-line strongest statement and missing step
  and a pointer [definition; the statements are in the homes] (74 rows: proof 3, bounded 10, estimate 17, unsized 39, test 5; 71 involve
  a number; missing step a calculation in 56, a measurement in 11, a proof or method in 7; the Chap. 9 group holds one grouped row, the
  eight ○ cells of tab:9-atlas, unsized, a calculation; added at the Chapter 9 merge, 73 rows before).
- Sources: Writer only: state/LOOSE-ENDS.md (all CLOSED-AS-EXACT-GAP rows); LIMITS.md "H6 exact-gap register"; this file's
  open-problems fields (LE-n and XG-n labels).
- Figures/tables; worked examples: Table 22.2 exact gaps by chapter, indexed to their homes. Worked example 22.2: the strongest statement
  and missing step of one gap, read from its home. Limitbox plots: none.
- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

### 22.3 Channels outside the framework   [status: ACCEPTED]
- Purpose: Name the processes the framework does not cover and say why.
- Learning outcomes: the reader can (i) list the channels outside the operator basis (charged-current absorption with a nuclear
  transition and a Coulomb-distorted electron) and outside the response picture (radiative decays of dark states, fast-ion tracks);
  (ii) state the collective sectors not yet treated (charge-density-wave phasons and amplitudons); (iii) say what each would need.
- Prerequisites: operator basis → 8.1; master-formula conditions → 8.2; in-medium decay interface → 10.9; Bethe stopping → 19.3.
- Introduces: scope boundary of the framework.

  state, 13 to 14 MeV up [derived; computed].

  modes (LE-81).
- Sources: Writer only: returns/R12/WP7-D-v2.md §4; returns/R16/CW3.md §1.4, §4.1.

- Threads: none.
- Budget (words): 4,000.

## Appendices
Appendix sections are reference units, shorter than chapter sections; one writer assignment covers a group of them (A in one assignment;
B is generated from book/NOTATION.md; C in two; D in batches of four to five, each batch after the sections it serves are drafted; E in
batches of two to three). Each row gives content, the sections that point into it, and a budget. SPINE-II added the Parts II–III rows:
C.10–C.13, D.24–D.47, E.13, the Parts II–III users of existing rows ("Parts II–III:" in A–C rows, extended "(→ …)" lists in D–E rows),
and Appendices E and F.

### Appendix A. Conventions and units   [PLANNED; 8,000 words; one assignment]
- A.1 Natural units (ħ = c = k_B = 1) and conversion factors (ħc, eV ↔ K, km/s ↔ c, cm⁻³ ↔ eV³, barn ↔ eV⁻²). Used by: all; first 1.1. 800. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.2 Charge conventions: Heaviside–Lorentz, e² = 4πα, e > 0, electron charge −e, Coulomb kernel v_C(k) = e²/k²; converting formulas in both
  directions between Heaviside–Lorentz and Gaussian (e_G² = α) or SI (e²/ε₀^SI = 4πα) units. Used by: 2.3, 4.4, 5.5, 6.2; Parts II–III: 8.4. 900. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.3 Fourier and normalization conventions: O(q) = ∫d³r e^{+iq·r}O(r), f(ω) = ∫dt e^{+iωt}f(t); box normalization Σ_q ↔ V∫d³q/(2π)³,
  δ_{qq′} ↔ (2π)³δ³(q − q′)/V; Brillouin-zone averages; matrices indexed by the unreduced q. Used by: 2.1, 2.2, 2.3, 3.1, 4.1, 4.4. 1,100. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.4 Kinematic signs: q from dark matter to target, never folded; ω given to the target; Δ_χ; ω < 0 at T > 0. Used by: 1.3, 2.2, 4.2; Parts II–III: 8.2. 600. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.5 Bloch and Wannier conventions: ψ_nk over V, u_nk over Ω, periodic gauge, ρ_mn(k, q) with ρ_nn(k, 0) = 1, the phase of v_mn and of the
  optical limit of ρ_mn, one gauge for all interpolated quantities; band subscripts v, c. Used by: 3.2, 3.6, 6.2. 900. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.6 Phonon conventions: displacement expansion, Σ_κ|e_κν|² = 1, e_κν(−Q) = e_κν(Q)*, ℓ_Qν with N outside, g normalization and hermiticity,
  emission/absorption weights n + ½ ± ½. Used by: 5.3, 6.2, 6.5. 1,000. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.7 Response conventions: R (not χ), S_ab, FDT, detailed balance, ε, ε^{-1}, L, σ_cond, the S–L relation, the spin factor 2 for spin-summed
  independent-particle forms, moment normalization (π over the real line) and its conversion to the per-particle ∫₀^∞ form. Used by: 4.1,
  4.2, 4.3, 4.4, 4.5, 4.6, 4.9; Parts II–III: 8.3, 8.4. 1,200. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.8 Halo conventions and rate normalization: SHM parameter set, v_E(t) in integrals, v_E^ref and v_max for reference scalars, rates per
  unit target mass. Used by: 1.2, 1.3, 1.5; Parts II–III: 8.2. 800. [status: ACCEPTED]
- A.9 Cross-section conventions: σ̄_e (vacuum, q₀ = αm_e), F_DM, σ_p, σ_N, dσ/dq normalization; how to convert other papers' conventions.
  Used by: 1.4, 2.2; Parts II–III: 8.1, 10.7. 700. [status: ACCEPTED]

### Appendix E. Ab initio methods   [PLANNED; 85,000 words]
User directive (2026-11-01) as in the earlier outline: how each material quantity in the book is computed, built up from scratch. Each
section: what is computed; which approximation it embodies (its place on the truncation label 𝔗 of 8.5); convergence parameters; typical
cost; pitfalls. Chapters point here wherever a quantity "is computed". Sections E.1–E.13 teach methods; E.14–E.27 specify, per quantity, the
calculations the book explains but does not report.
**Part I needs, in order of first pointer:** E.7 (2.2), E.1 and E.3 (3.3), E.2 (3.5), E.5 (3.6), E.6 (4.4), E.4 (5.4), E.8 (5.7),
E.9 (6.4), E.10 (7.4). Second and later pointers are listed in each row. E.11–E.13 serve Parts II–III only (first pointers 19.6, 20.1,
18.1).
- E.1 Density-functional theory in practice: functionals, plane-wave cutoff, k-point sampling and smearing, self-consistency, convergence
  and cost; computed bands and effective masses (→ 3.3, 3.4). 4,500. [status: ACCEPTED]
- E.2 Pseudopotentials, PAW datasets and all-electron reconstruction: construction, testing, the large-q requirement (→ 3.5, 10.1, 10.6). 4,000. [status: ACCEPTED]
- E.3 Quasiparticle corrections: scissor shifts, G₀W₀ energies and lifetimes, the effect on matrix elements, convergence (→ 3.3, 3.4, 4.8).
  3,500. [status: ACCEPTED]
- E.4 Density-functional perturbation theory: phonons, Born charges and the Berry-phase polarization, dielectric tensors, Debye–Waller
  exponents and displacement correlations from DFPT phonons, electron–phonon potentials, the occupied-band part of ∂ψ_v and the change of
  the empty states ∂ψ_c that a transition amplitude needs beyond standard DFPT, elastic constants; grids and convergence (→ 5.4, 5.5, 5.6,
  6.2, 11.1, 11.2, 11.3, 11.8, 12.5, 13.2, 17.5). 5,000. [status: ACCEPTED]
- E.5 Wannier functions in practice: projections, disentanglement, interpolation of bands and of electron–phonon vertices, polar
  long-range terms, the grid sizes and the matrix-element and memory counts of a converged rate (silicon on a 40³ fine grid, formerly
  worked example 6.3), phonon linewidths and α²F on fine grids, and the convergence of phonon-assisted ε₂; the route of a phonon-assisted

### Appendix F. Literature formulas as limits   [PLANNED; 12,000 words]
The book's complete table of the widely used formulas recovered as limits: for each, the formula in the book's notation with its source
and equation, its point on the truncation label 𝔗, the approximation chain from the master formula, the controlling parameter, the fuller
result it is compared with, the validity region, the leading correction, the limitbox and section where it is derived, and its status.
The limitbox in the chapter shows the formula, a one-line citation, the comparison plot and one sentence; this table is the formal
record. The rows below are the appendix sections; the map that follows is the writer-side source of the table (one row per research row;
the "Row" column is writer-only and never printed).

  comparison plot are built), and the column key (→ 9.4). 500. [status: ACCEPTED]

- F.4 Magnetic, superconducting and special targets: Chaps. 14–16 (→ none; as F.2). 2,500. [status: ACCEPTED]

**F map** (every LIMITS.md row outside the superseded hypothesis table and the gap register; "—" in Box = no limitbox, because the row is
not a literature formula, or is a list, a convention or a no-go that has no curve to compare: a result of the book's own derivation, a
computed value, a model statement or a code validation; such rows are key results or open-problems material in the section named). A
box may hold a row and its amendment row (primed or lettered labels), which share one panel; two distinct formulas in one box get two
panels, marked "panel 1", "panel 2". **Columns (SPINE-II-REV, editor decision 1):** Formula = the literature formula in the book's
notation (∣·∣ is written with the divides sign so that the table parses), with its public source; 𝔗 = the point on the truncation label,

"detector" for rows of Part III that are not response truncations; x = the controlling parameter on the plot's x-axis; Compared with = the
book's fuller result (equation or section); Validity = the shaded region, where formula and fuller result agree within 10% unless stated.
"Fallback" marks STYLE's fallback plot (the formula's parameter dependence with the validity boundary marked) where no fuller result is
computed cheaply. Status tags as at the head of Chap. 8.

### 2026-11-02 (fourth entry): revision of Chap. 8, Parts II–III and the appendices after both reviews (SPINE-II-REV)
Inputs: ledgers/REVIEWS/spine/structural-II.md (S1–S32 and its §1–§11) and ledgers/REVIEWS/spine/reader-II.md (persona (a) rows, the
duplication and length lists, the open-problems and guide checks, personas (b) and (c)), with the editor's decisions 1–11 of
briefs/spine/SPINE-II-REV.md; state/SOTA.md "R17 final state" and CONVENTIONS §12–§13 for every status. Scope: this file (front matter,
design principles, Budget, Chap. 8, Parts II–III, Appendices C–F, this log), NOTATION.md, ledgers/CONCEPTS.md, THREADS.md, CLAIMS.md and
check_concepts.py. Chap. 8 is Part I by number, but S3, S11, S12, S14, S23 and the reader's 8.x rows edit it, so it is in scope. Part I
entries 1.1–7.4 are unchanged. STYLE.md is outside the scope (S29 is recorded in the design principles instead).

**Section map (old → new).** 10.7 (anisotropy) merged into 11.7 "Anisotropy and daily modulation" (S10; the reader's merge option);
10.8 split into 10.7 "Absorption on electrons" and 10.8 "Two-insertion processes" (S18); 13.7 removed (S5, decision 4), 13.8 → 13.7,
13.9 → 13.8, 13.10 → 13.9; 14.7 and 15.5 merged into their chapter summaries (S17); 17.7 → 21.7 "Next targets" (S6, decision 2); 22.4
merged into 22.1 (S25). Retitled (S20): 9.4 "Limitboxes", 12.4 "Phonon final states at small momentum", 13.1 "The indirect-gap window",
Chap. 16 "Special targets", 16.1 "Dirac-material rates", 16.8 "Two-level systems at millikelvin" (its defectons and crowdions moved to
19.6, S18), 18.4 "Pair energies in Si, Ge and GaAs", 19.1 "The recoil hand-over", Chap. 21 "Exclusion limits and next targets". Limitbox
names follow: old 10.8a–d → 10.7a, 10.7b, 10.8a, 10.8b; old 10.7a → 11.7a; old 13.8a → 13.7a; old 16.8e–f → 19.6a–b; 8.1a, 8.2a and
11.1d are no longer boxes. Figures and worked examples renumber with their sections. Appendix D: D.38–D.42 renumbered into section
order (14.1, 15.2, 15.4, 16.1, 16.8; S27). Sections: Part II 67 → 63, Part III 26 → 26 (Chap. 21 gains one, Chap. 22 loses one).

### 2026-11-01 (third entry): detailed spine of Chap. 8, Parts II–III and Appendices E′, F (SPINE-II)
Inputs: the final research state (state/SOTA.md "R14–R16 consolidated state" and "R17 final state"; LIMITS.md; FRAMEWORK.md;
state/LOOSE-ENDS.md; state/NOVELTY.md; state/DEFERRED-COMPUTE.md) and the returns named in each entry. Scope: Chap. 8, Parts II–III, the
Parts II–III rows of Appendices C–E, Appendices E′ and F, the totals and this log; NOTATION.md; ledgers/CONCEPTS.md, CLAIMS.md, THREADS.md
and check_concepts.py. Part I entries are unchanged except the Chap. 1 header total (19,500, after the user-directed cut of 1.1, which the
header had not followed) and the "Used by" / "(→ …)" lists of the A–E rows that Parts II–III now also point to. Closes LE-171 (placement and
gates) and LE-172 (Parts II–III ledger rows).

**Status rule applied.** Only REFEREED or VERIFIED (claimed and verified once) content is a key result; CLAIMED content and exact gaps are
named as open in each section and collected in the uncertainty sections and Chap. 22. Concretely: the silicon zero-phonon edges, the
glide-parity rule and the window ordering are established (verified); the phonon-assisted onset 0.333/0.337 MeV, the plane-wave f-sum
check, the transport-engine validation rows, the ²¹⁰Pb background estimate and the nuclear-recoil charge yield (not computed) are open.
Every chapter is PLANNED; none is BLOCKED, because every chapter's core is refereed or verified and its open items are stated as open.
Priority claims carry the NOVELTY verdict: "to our knowledge" for NONE FOUND (N1–N9, N11, N14, N16–N18), the prior source cited for PRIOR
ART (N12, N13; Allen 1971; Davydov; Pines 1956; Braun–Kivshar; cond-mat/0604205; 1109.1193).

**Structural changes (old outline → new), with reasons.**
1. Chap. 8: the outline's eight items became eight sections (38,000); content is the refereed framework. Section 8.6 carries the rigour gaps.
2. Chap. 9 (4 sections, was 3 items; review item S30): the atlas table (one home per cell, checked), the kinematic map, a new 9.3 placing
   every excitation on the (q, ω) plane with its sector (the overlap regions that 17.4 needs), and 9.4 on limitboxes. 9.3 is distinct
   content, not padding: it is the only place the sectors of 8.6 are drawn for a real material before Part II computes them.
3. Chap. 10: plasmons moved before the other couplings (10.3, beside the electron-gas models they share); boosted, fermionic and
   in-medium-decay channels joined inelastic dark matter (10.9; WP7-B1 I3, I5 and WP7-D A10 electronic part); two-insertion processes
   (pair absorption, dark Compton) joined absorption (10.8); 10.10 retitled "Uncertainties and open problems" (S26).
4. Chap. 11: polar phonons split from acoustic (11.1, 11.2), because the WP8-F figures of merit and the polar band integral each need a
   section; the isotope B − L channel and the Lifshitz defect join nuclear-spin couplings (11.6); exothermic dark matter into phonons moved
   to 17.3 (it is one formula for all collective modes, WP4 §7); bulk acoustic resonators and resonant-mass drives (WP7-D) join absorption
   (11.8). 11.6 has its own sources (WP7-B2 §2, CW2 §18–§19).
5. Chap. 12: unchanged in order; 12.6 adds Migdal-type absorption (WP7-B2 X10) to bremsstrahlung.
6. Chap. 13 (10 sections, was 9): screening got its own section (13.4; X2 and the pair channel), and the closing section is now "The
   silicon window" (13.10), which presents the established window table and closes T5.
7. Chap. 14 (7, was 5): neutron-data responses (14.4; WP7-B2 S6) and coherent fields with precessing nuclear spins (14.6; WP7-D S4, S9,
   A9) are new; chiral targets absorb chiral phonons, single-molecule magnets and multiferroics (14.5).
8. Chap. 15: the closed forms at the edge are the resummed L^BCS (CW4); 15.4 adds Holstein absorption (WP7-B1 A2).
9. Chap. 16 (9, was 5; retitled "Dirac, low-dimensional, molecular and defect targets"): the WP7-B1 low-dimensional, nanotube, dot and
   molecular channels (16.2–16.4), dopants (16.5), color centers and bond breaking (16.6, WP7-B2 D2, D5), glasses and two-level systems
   (16.7) and millikelvin defect kinetics (16.8; WP7-T). The outline's "scope set by later research" is now set.
10. Chap. 17 (7, was 5): figures of merit (17.5, WP8-F/U), ranking under uncertainty (17.6, WP8-S) and the next-target guide (17.7) carry
   the user's framing; 17.1 closes T2 and 17.4 closes T4, as the THREADS note proposed.
11. Part III retitled "Measurement" (S22, deferred in the second entry). Chap. 18 gains 18.5 (end of the cascade: capture and excitons;
   GAP-EF §4). Chap. 19 gains the energy balance law (19.5, WP7-E) and delayed release (19.6); the Lindhard section (19.3) also carries
   Bethe stopping (WP7-D). Chap. 20 (5, was 2 items; S30) is retitled "Charge, phonon and quasiparticle readout" and takes WP7-E charge,
   WP8-R phonon collection and the transport results. Chap. 21 splits halo-independent bounds (21.2) from rate assembly; closures move to
   21.3 (T1), 21.4 (T7), 21.6 (T6). Chap. 22 (4, was 3; S30) separates exact theory gaps (22.2) from computations (22.1) and scope (22.3).
12. Appendices: C.10–C.13 (moment problems, branching and renewal, linear programming, interval orders); D.24–D.47 (24 derivations,

### 2026-11-01 (second entry): revision after the spine reviews (SPINE-I-REV)
Inputs: ledgers/REVIEWS/spine/structural.md (30 items, S1–S30 below, plus its §1 soft edges and §7 notes) and
ledgers/REVIEWS/spine/reader.md (rows R-… below), with the editor's ten decisions (E1–E10). Scope: Part I, the appendices, the front
matter and the budget of this file, NOTATION.md and ledgers/CONCEPTS.md, THREADS.md, CLAIMS.md; the Part II–III entries are unchanged
except for spelling (S29), so the review items that concern them are recorded as deferred.

**Section map (old → new).** Chap. 4: 4.1–4.4 unchanged; old 4.7 (electron gas) → 4.5; old 4.5 split into 4.6 (RPA, local fields and
TDDFT kernels) and 4.9 (excitons and the BSE); old 4.6 split into 4.7 (Green's functions) and 4.8 (Feynman diagrams). Chap. 6: 6.1, 6.2
unchanged; old 6.3 (computing the vertex) removed, its physics folded into 6.2 and its method moved to E.4/E.5; old 6.4 → 6.3; new 6.4
(phonon self-energy and the Eliashberg function); 6.5 unchanged. Part I: 39 → 41 sections. Figures and worked examples are renumbered
within Chaps. 1, 4, 6 and 7.
**Order of Chap. 4 (E2).** The reader proposed 4.4 → electron gas → RPA/BSE → diagrams (old numbers 4.4 → 4.7 → 4.5 → 4.6). That order
is valid for the RPA but would still set up the BSE before the ladder. The order adopted is 4.4 → 4.5 electron gas → 4.6 crystal RPA,
local fields and kernels → 4.7 Green's functions → 4.8 diagrams → 4.9 BSE. Justification: (a) the electron gas needs only the Lehmann
representation (4.1), the independent-particle S (4.2), the f-sum (4.3), ε and k_TF (4.4) and k_F (now 2.5); its old "diagrams → 4.6"
prerequisite served only the static local-field factor, which moves to 4.6 (as G_LF ↔ f_xc); (b) R⁰ and the RPA are first met in closed
form, and 4.6 carries them to crystals (Adler–Wiser) without any diagram; (c) 4.7–4.8 need only 2.3–2.5 and 4.1, and 4.8 identifies its
bubble with the R⁰ of 4.5–4.6; (d) the BSE needs the ladder and W^scr (4.8) and the exchange (local-field) kernel (4.6), so it comes last;
(e) the later users are satisfied: 5.4 needs the crystal R⁰ (4.6), 7.3 the RPA (4.5), 6.3–6.4 and 7.2 the diagrams (4.8).
**Appendix D map (old → new).** D.1–D.7 unchanged; D.9 → D.8; D.10 → D.9; new D.10 (imaginary-time formalism); D.8 → D.11 (extended to
T = 0, imaginary time and the Feynman rules); new D.12 (BSE from the ladder); D.11 → D.13; D.12 → D.14; D.13 → D.15; D.14 → D.16; new D.17
(phonon self-energy, α²F, λ_ep); D.15 → D.18; D.16 → D.19; D.17 → D.20; new D.21 (conserving BCS response); D.18 → D.22; D.19 → D.23.
D.24 onward are reserved for Parts II–III (were D.20 onward).

D.17; CONCEPTS rows; NOTATION Π_ν, γ^ep_Qν, α²F, λ_ep; CLAIMS I-104–I-106. 7.2 now points to 6.4 for λ_ep and to E.9 for Eliashberg gaps. |
